PREFACE 

This manual has been constructed to accompany Brinkley's Principles of General Chemistry; consequently the material has been so arranged as to obtain, for the most part, close correlation between the two. At the same time there should be little difficulty in adapting this manual to other texts which assume a secondary school preparation in the subject. As the present trend is decidedly toward the inclusion of more analytical work in the elementary courses, a section on Elementary Qualitative Analysis has been added to the manual.

Since the manual is designed for students with some chemical background, the usual experiments dealing with hydrogen and oxygen have been omitted, and in their place are offered two inorganic preparations, each involving two or more consecutive operations and of sufficient length to occupy a class for several laboratory periods. These preparations have been selected with the objects of presenting a brief review of some of the general reactions of elementary chemistry and of affording an opportunity for the student to gain some proficiency in mechanical operations carried out on more than a test tube scale. 

Corresponding to the text sections on States of Matter and Reactions of Gases, two new exercises have been constructed. 

These deal, respectively, with the determination of transition points and the effect of conditions on the rate of chemical reactions. Such exercises afford the student more contact with the physical side of chemistry than is obtained in the usual elementary course.

The authors are of the opinion that work of a qualitative nature is done better after the student has seen the necessity for reasonably careful manipulation. Hence Section I (Inorganic Chemistry) includes, near the outset, several quantitative experiments, while the last part has a definitely qualitative outlook. The qualitative experiments in this section are not, however, designed to replace a course in Qualitative Analysis. Rather, they are intended to show to the student some of the applications of general principles and of specific reactions of substances. For the course which in-

vi 

PREFACE 

cludes Qualitative Analysis, the authors recommend the omission of the latter half of Section I and the use of Section II in its entirety. 

Throughout the first section, the effort has been made to include more experiments than can be performed intelligently in the time usually available for laboratory work. This will permit the instructor to make a selection of the experiments best suited to his particular course and to the needs of his students. Independent thought by the student is encouraged through the inclusion of certain experiments in which he must formulate his own directions on the basis of previous experience. 

Section II, Elementary Qualitative Analysis, designed pri marily for the elementary student, is essentially the same as that appearing in the authors' manual accompanying Brinkley's Introductory General Chemistry. No attempt is made to include directions for the separation and identification of all of the ions, but only for those likely to be encountered in elementary work. In this section the emphasis is upon the general principles employed in the qualitative separations, and not upon the refinements of analytical procedure. Since the student is expected to use his text as a reference, the discussion of principles and reactions is limited to those phases which he is not likely to find in his text.

GENERAL DIRECTIONS 

1. Taking of notes. Notes on an experiment must be recorded as you perform that experiment. Use either the blank pages of this manual or a bound notebook of appropriate size. Notations on loose pieces of paper will not be permitted. The instructor in charge of the course will issue specific directions as to what he requires in laboratory notes and reports. In general, your record should include: (a) The date. (b) The number and title of the exercise. (c) The number and subtitle of the specific experiment. (d) A brief, concise account of the observations made and conclusions drawn from each experiment. (e) Answers to all questions in the body of the exercise. (f) Equations for all reactions which occur. 

Answers to the review questions at the end of exercises need not be written in the notebook. These questions should be studied, however, in preparation for the classroom. 

2. Laboratory regulations. Read the complete set of directions for a given experiment before starting to work on it. Always know what you are doing, and THINK as you perform the experiment.

Work independently except in those cases where permission is granted for coöperation. Bring your textbook to the laboratory and, when in difficulty, consult this, not your neighbor.

When using materials, always replace the stopper and return the bottle to its proper place on the shelf.

When quantities are not specified, use small amounts. If, unfortunately, an excess has been taken, throw this into the waste jar. Never return any material to the bottle.

Unless otherwise specified, use dilute solutions of acids and bases.

All discarded solids and rubbish such as match sticks, broken apparatus, and used filter papers, must be placed in the waste jars provided for this purpose. Liquids may be poured into the sink and washed down the drain pipe with running water. ix

GENERAL DIRECTIONS 

Keep your working space clean and neat. 

Clean up spilled materials promptly, whether at your desk or elsewhere. 

Work carefully, and endeavor to avoid accidents and injury to yourself and others. In case of personal injury, even if apparently slight, report at once to the instructor. 

For further information on laboratory regulations and customs, read the posted statement on what is expected of you in your particular laboratory.

EXERCISE I PRELIMINARY OPERATIONS 

The purpose of this exercise is to acquaint you with the apparatus in your desk and with the proper technique in performing many of the operations required in the pursuit of a laboratory course. 

1. Inspection of equipment. Remove all the apparatus from the drawer; then learn the name of each piece by reference to 

Fig. 1, and by checking against the list. Make note of any article which is missing, or not in good condition. Complete the inspection in accord with the directions on the back of the apparatus card. 

2. The Bunsen burner. Unscrew the barrel tube of the Bunsen burner and observe the gas and air intakes. Then reassemble the burner and light the gas. Adjust the gas supply so as to have a flame not more than 10 cm. high. Close the air intake, and observe the appearance of the flame. Hold a porcelain dish in this flame for a moment. What do you observe? Obviously such a flame is undesirable and, further, is not very hot. Now open the air intake gradually, noting the changes in the appearance of the flame, until the burner begins to roar. This indicates that too much air is being admitted. Hence adjust the air intake until the roaring just stops. This flame is the most efficient for general use. With too much air the flame may strike back, burning at the base of the burner inside the barrel. When this happens, shut off the gas for a moment, then begin again. 

Hold an iron wire in different parts of the flame. From your observations, which part of the flame is the hottest? Insert a match quickly into the flame just above the barrel. Account for the delay in the ignition of the match. Make a diagram of the flame, indicating the hottest part. 

3. Construction of stirring rods. Glass rods will be found very useful for stirring liquids and suspensions of solids in liquids. By following the directions given below, construct four stirring rods similar to that illustrated in Fig. 2, but varying in length from 

10 cm. to 20 cm. 3

PRELIMINARY OPERATIONS 

(a) Cutting glass rods. Place the glass rod flat on the table and at the desired point make a single deep scratch with the triangular file. Place the thumbs directly below this mark and break the rod by applying a gentle pressure with the fingers on each side of the scratch. If the rod does not yield to gentle pressure, make a deeper cut. (b) Fire-polishing. Since the edges of the cut rod will be sharp, they must be rounded before use. To do this, hold the rod at an angle of 45° with one end just above the top of the inner cone of the Bunsen burner, and rotate the rod until the end begins to glow. 

FIG. 2.-Wash Bottle and 

Stirring Rods.

The handle of the stirring rod may be made by heating a point on the glass rod about 3 cm. from one end until the glass softens. 

Then withdraw the rod from the flame and bend the arm to the desired position. 

4. Construction of a wash bottle. 

Construct a wash bottle like that in Fig. 2, using your 250 cc. flask and fitting this with a two-hole stopper. Cut the glass tubing and fire-polish the ends just as directed for glass rods. For further manipulation proceed according to the following directions. (a) Bending glass tubing. To bend glass tubing properly, the tube must be heated uniformly over a distance of 5 to 8 cm. For this purpose attach the wing top to the Bunsen burner. Then hold the tube lengthwise in the flame, slowly rotating it so that all sides may be equally heated. When the tube begins to sag, remove it from the flame and immediately make the desired bend. 

Do not, however, try to bend the tube after the glass has begun to harden. (b) Drawing out glass tubing. Hold the tubing in both hands and, while rotating it, heat a small section in the hottest part of the flame until the glass is thoroughly softened. Remove from the flame and, by immediately pulling at each end, draw out the tubing. A nozzle for the wash bottle may be made in this manner and the tubing cut in the proper place, then fire-polished.

PRELIMINARY OPERATIONS 

(c) Inserting glass tubing in stoppers. After the fire-polished edges have cooled, wet both the tube and the stopper; then insert the tube by applying gentle pres- 

FIG. 3.-Removal of Solid from 

Bottle. sure only. 

5. Blowing a bulb on a glass tube. Draw out a piece of glass tubing as directed in exp. 4(b), and seal off the tapered end by inserting it in the flame for a moment. Now heat the closed end of the tube, rotating it to insure even heating, until the glass is quite soft. Remove from the flame and blow into the open end until a small bulb forms. To enlarge this, again heat until the glass softens, then blow as before. This alternate heating and blowing is continued until a bulb of the desired diameter is obtained. The beginner encounters difficulty with "blow-outs" which result from uneven heating. 

Practice until you can obtain a bulb about 1 cm. in diameter. 

6. Removing chemicals from bottles. (a) Solids. As indicated in Fig. 3, the required amount of a solid may be removed from the bottle with the aid of the hollow glass stopper. This may be emptied on a piece of paper and the solid taken thus to your desk. (a) (b) (b) Liquids. Remove the stopper from the reagent bottle by taking it between the fingers, as shown in Fig. 4, with the palm of the hand up. This FIG. 4.-Removing Liquid from Bottle. leaves your hand free to grasp the bottle as indicated. Contamination of the liquid, such as must occur if the stopper is laid on the desk top, is also avoided. It is, however, permissible to rest a flat-topped stopper on the desk, provided that it rests on its flat top. 

When pouring out a liquid from a bottle, keep the lip of the bottle in touch with the edge of the receiving vessel. This

PRELIMINARY OPERATIONS 

will prevent the liquid from running down the side of the bottle. 

7. Heating liquids. (a) Partly fill a test tube with water and attach the test tube holder. Bring the water to a boil by holding the tube at an angle of 45° and passing it back and forth, with frequent shaking, over the tip of a flame so that this strikes the tube somewhat below the level of the liquid. This is important, for, if the flame plays upon the glass above the liquid, the glass may become so hot that it cracks when the liquid does touch it; on the other hand, if heat is applied only to the lowest part of the test tube, the contents of the tube may be suddenly thrown out by the formation of steam bubbles in the bottom of the tube. (b) Quarter-fill a small beaker with water. Boil this by setting the beaker on a wire gauze, which in turn rests on a ring of the ring stand so adjusted in height that, when the burner is placed directly under the middle of the gauze, the tip of the blue cone of the flame just touches the wire. It is well to stir with a glass rod until boiling begins. (c) Place a little water in an evaporating dish and boil this by heating the dish directly with the flame, without the use of the gauze. The water may be boiled away completely without breaking the dish. Why should a bottle or other thick-walled glass vessel never be used for heating a liquid? 

8. Heating solids. (a) A closed tube, called an ignition tube, is frequently used for the purpose of heating solid substances. To make such a tube, select a piece of glass tubing about 18 cm. long, and draw this out as directed in exp. 4(b). Break the resulting capillary tube, and seal the holes at the tapering ends of the two pieces by holding these in the flame for a few moments. (b) When quantities of solids larger than can be conveniently held in an ignition tube are to be heated, either the pyrex test tube or the crucible may be used—the test tube if the temperature required is below that at which the glass will begin to melt; the crucible to utilize higher temperatures. 

9. Working volume of apparatus. So that you may have a fairly accurate idea of the volume of a liquid with which you are working, make the following measurements. Fill a test tube with water and measure the volume of this water by means of the graduate. Now estimate to what point the test tube must be filled in order to have 10 cc. of liquid, add water to this point,

PRELIMINARY OPERATIONS 

and determine the accuracy of your guess by measuring this water in the graduate. Repeat the experiment, using what you think is 5 cc. of water. Practice until you can estimate closely 

5 and 10 cc. of water. Practice similarly with one of the small beakers until you can make a fairly accurate estimate of volumes of 50 and 100 cc. respectively. 

10. Mixing reagents. Intimate contact between two substances, when placed in a test tube, may be secured by shaking the tube or by pouring the contents from one tube to another. When a solid is to be added to a liquid, place the liquid in the test tube first, then add the solid. This will cause the solid to be thoroughly 

FIG. 5.-Filtration. 

wetted by the liquid. 

11. Decantation. Half fill a test tube with water, add some fine sand, and shake the tube. Allow the sand to settle, then carefully pour off the water.

This operation is known as decantation. When only can this method of separating a liquid from a solid be used? 

12. Filtration. Generally a solid will not settle readily. In such cases filtration is employed. To a test tube half-filled with water add a small amount of manganese dioxide and potassium chlorate. Shake the tube thoroughly and then heat the contents until boiling begins; the water will dissolve the potassium chlorate, but the manganese dioxide, being insoluble, remains as a solid, partly in suspension and partly settled to the bottom of the tube. 

To separate the solution of potassium chlorate from the insoluble material, the mixture is filtered; that is, poured on to a filter paper which retains the solid but allows the solution to run through. A filter is prepared, as illustrated in Fig. 5, by folding a filter paper along a diameter and again at right angles. Then open this so as to form a cone one side of which contains three thicknesses of paper and the other side one thickness. Fit this into a funnel,

PRELIMINARY OPERATIONS 

wet with water, and press the paper down firmly to make it adhere to the glass. Support the funnel on the filter stand. When adding the mixture to the filter, pour the liquid slowly along a stirring rod, and keep the level of the liquid in the filter below the upper edge of the paper. Spattering of the filtrate is prevented by keeping the stem of the funnel against the wall of the beaker. 

Now put a few drops of the filtrate on a watch glass, and evaporate this by placing the watch glass over a beaker of boiling water. What substance crystallizes out? What has remained on the filter paper? Would decantation have been a satisfactory method of separating the solution from the insoluble material in this case? Why? What is meant by the term, filtration? filtrate?

How would you proceed to obtain, separately, sand and sugar from a mixture of these two substances? 

13. Weighing. (a) The operation of weighing as required for the usual laboratory experiment is performed on the platform balance. Place a piece of glazed paper on each pan of the balance. 

These protect the pans and serve as counterpoises. The substance to be weighed is then placed upon the paper on the left-hand pan. 

For units smaller than 10 g. the rider on the graduated beam may be employed. A balance is obtained when the swing of the pointer in both directions is equal. Determine the weight of your largest beaker in this manner. (b) For the more accurate weighing demanded in experiments of a quantitative nature, employ the horn pan balance and set of weights located in one of the lockers at your working place. 

Hot objects should never be placed on the pans of this balance. 

You are responsible for the loss of any of the weights of the set. 

Assure yourself that all the weights are present before using the set. Report any missing ones to the assistant. Each set, before being returned to the locker at the end of an exercise, must be checked by the assistant. 

Time may be saved in the use of the horn pan balance by adopting the following suggestions. Whenever the weight desired is the difference of two weighing operations, it is not necessary that the two pans of the balance be counterpoised at the beginning. The weighing operation may be considered complete when the pointer of the balance swings through an equal arc on both sides of the midpoint. Do not attempt to stop the swing.

The weight of an object should be expressed as a single num-

PRELIMINARY OPERATIONS 

ber. If the individual weights used in a weighing are: 2 grams, 100 milligrams, and 50 milligrams, the weight of the object is indicated as 2.15 g. Directions for the proper treatment of balances of a greater degree of precision than that of the horn pan balance will be given by the instructor, when such balances are employed.

EXERCISE II CHANGES IN MATTER. WEIGHT RELATIONS DURING CHEMICAL CHANGE 

In this exercise some characteristics of physical and of chemical changes are noted, then applied in distinguishing between the existence of a mixture and the formation of a compound. Particular emphasis is given to the effects characteristic of a chemical change, and for this purpose certain quantitative experiments are included. 

A. Changes in Matter 

1. Physical change. (a) To a small amount of sulfur in a test tube add 5 cc. of carbon disulfide and shake the tube thoroughly. (Caution: Carbon disulfide is inflammable. Do not bring it near a flame. Any discarded carbon disulfide should be washed down the drain pipe of the sink with running water.) Filter some of the liquid through a dry filter paper and catch the filtrate on a watch glass. Allow this to evaporate in the hood. Describe the residue, and compare it with the original sulfur. Have the properties of the sulfur been altered? What type of change is involved in the solution of a substance and its recovery from solution by evaporation? (b) Heat an iron wire in the hottest part of the flame of the Bunsen burner for a few seconds. What changes in the appearance of the iron occur while it is being heated? Allow the wire to cool and again examine it. Do you observe any permanent change in properties? What type of change takes place when iron is heated to the temperature of the Bunsen burner? What are the characteristics of a physical change? 

2. Chemical change. (a) Note the properties of a small strip of magnesium ribbon. With the aid of the tongs hold the strip in the flame for a moment. Describe the result. Examine the product and compare its properties with those of the original substance. 

Is the change in properties a permanent one? Does there appear to be a change in composition? What type of change does magnesium undergo when heated in air? What two effects indicated by your observations accompany this type of change? 10

WEIGHT RELATIONS 

(b) Place a small amount of sugar in an ignition tube 15-18 cm. long, and heat this strongly until no further change is noted. 

Record your observations. During this heating what forms in the colder part of the tube? What is its source? Break the tube by plunging it, while still hot, into water contained in a porcelain dish. How does the residue compare with sugar in color, taste, and solubility? When sugar is heated, does a physical or chemical change occur? Give reasons for your answer. (c) Fill the bottom half inch of an ignition tube with mercuric oxide. Heat it patiently and continuously in the hottest part of the flame. From time to time insert a glowing splint in the open end of the tube. What is the result? Oxygen in the air supports combustion. What does your splint test prove? What is the mirror which forms in the cool part of the tube? Break the tube by dropping it while still hot into a porcelain dish containing water. 

Into what substance does mercuric oxide decompose when heated? 

What type of chemical change or reaction does this illustrate? 

What type of chemical reaction is demonstrated in (b)? in (a)? 

3. Mixtures and compounds. (a) To a small quantity of iron filings in a test tube add 10 cc. of dilute hydrochloric acid. Describe the result. Smell the evolved gas, and try the effect of bringing a lighted match to the open end of the test tube. This experiment illustrates a chemical property of iron. (b) By means of the horn pan balance weigh out accurately 4 g. of sulfur and 7 g. of iron filings and make an intimate mixture of these two substances by grinding them together in the mortar. 

Place a small amount of this mixture in each of two test tubes. 

Save the remainder for (c). To the first test tube add 10 cc. of dilute hydrochloric acid. Is the result the same as in (a)? Does the gas evolved have the same properties as that in (a)? Have the properties of the iron been altered? To the second test tube add 5 cc. of carbon disulfide, filter, and treat the filtrate as directed in exp. 1(a). Has the sulfur undergone any change in properties? (c) Heat the remainder of the mixture in a test tube until it begins to glow. Remove the tube from the flame. What do you observe? What type of change is taking place? Now heat the tube more strongly, then break it by plunging the hot tube into water. Pulverize the solid residue, divide it into two portions and repeat the tests made in (b). Record your observations and account for the differences in the results. What is the meaning of

WEIGHT RELATIONS 

the term, compound? What is the significance of the weights of sulfur and iron taken in (b)? What would have been the outcome if an excess of iron filings had been present when the mixture was heated? 

State two respects in which a mixture differs from a compound. 

B. Weight Relations during Chemical Change 

4. Law of definite proportions. In this experiment an accurately weighed quantity of copper is heated with an excess of sulfur, and a sulfide of copper thus formed. The excess of sulfur is volatilized and driven off, thus leaving the sulfide of copper in the pure state. 

Make a loose coil of fine copper wire weighing about 2 g. and weigh this accurately on the horn pan balance. In like manner determine the weight of a clean dry porcelain crucible. Place the coil in the crucible, add enough powdered sulfur to cover the copper wire, put on the porcelain crucible lid, and heat gently in the hood until the sulfur ceases to burn at the edges of the crucible cover. (Do not remove the cover.) Now heat more strongly for 

3-4 minutes, let cool to room temperature, remove the cover, and weigh the crucible and contents accurately. Add a little more sulfur (not more than a gram) and repeat the above operations. 

If the weight obtained agrees within 0.05 g. with the weight obtained previously, it may be assumed that all of the copper has been converted to the sulfide. The process should be continued until two consecutive weighings so agree. Take the mean of these weighings for your calculations.

Clean out the crucible and repeat the experiment with a coil of copper wire weighing about 3 g. Be sure to weigh accurately both the copper wire and the dry crucible as before. 

In quantitative work, all data should be presented in orderly manner. For this experiment the accompanying outline is suggested, and may be used as a basis for a formal report on the work to the instructor.

The data in each case give you the weight of sulfur combined with the weight of copper initially taken. To compare the two cases it is necessary to calculate the weight of sulfur combining with a fixed weight of copper, such as one gram. Do this for each case and compare the results. Should they agree? What particular characteristic of a chemical change have you demonstrated? Name and state the law for which this experiment, if properly executed, is evidence.

WEIGHT RELATIONS 

You have now obtained data for the weight ratio in which copper and sulfur combine. From these data derive the formula of the sulfide of copper obtained in this experiment. 

5. Equivalent weight of zinc. The gram-equivalent weight of an element may be defined as that weight of the element which will combine with or displace 8 g. of oxygen or its equivalent. Hence it is not necessary to cause an element, whose equivalent weight is desired, to combine directly with oxygen. It may be allowed to react with or displace another element whose equivalent weight is known.

In this experiment, an accurately weighed quantity of zinc displaces an equivalent quantity of copper from a solution of a copper salt. From the data so obtained and the equivalent weight of copper (31.8), the value for the equivalent weight of zinc may be calculated.

Weigh roughly (on the platform balance) not more than 8 g. of copper sulfate, pulverize this in a mortar, and then dissolve the salt in about 100 cc. of water. Solution may be hastened by warming the water. Into this hot solution drop an accurately weighed (use horn pan balance) piece of bright sheet zinc (not exceeding one gram). Let it stand with occasional stirring, shaking, and application of heat, until the zinc has dissolved completely. Filter the solution and, by means of the wash bottle, transfer all of the displaced copper to the filter paper. This is done by directing the jet of water from the wash bottle upon the precipitated copper, while the beaker is held at such an angle that the water runs on to the filter. Then wash the copper powder free of copper sulfate solution. In washing a precipitate, direct the jet of water around the upper edge of the filter paper. Use only a little water at a time, and let it all run through the filter before adding more. 

Finally, pour 10 cc. of alcohol over the filter paper. Remove the filter paper carefully from the funnel, unfold it, and spread it on a watch glass. Place the watch glass over an evaporating dish which contains a small quantity of water. Heat the dish with a very low flame until the precipitate and paper are quite dry. Weigh the precipitated copper on the filter paper, using another piece of filter paper of the same size as a counterpoise on the other pan of the balance. Now calculate the equivalent weight of zinc (the weight of zinc which ought, according to your data, to displace 

31.8 g. of copper).

EXERCISE III PREPARATION OF STRONTIUM CHLORIDE AND OF STRONTIUM NITRATE. RECOVERY OF A BY- PRODUCT, SODIUM SULFATE 

The major steps in this exercise are: (1) conversion of strontium sulfate to strontium carbonate by boiling with a rather strong solution of sodium carbonate; (2) dissolving the strontium carbonate in hydrochloric acid; then (3) crystallization of strontium chloride from this solution. 

The first step is possible because of the difference between the solubilities of the two salts: strontium sulfate, 0.0006 moles per liter; strontium carbonate, 0.00007 moles per liter. Hence, although but very little strontium sulfate can dissolve at any one time, as fast as it does go into solution the strontium ion is precipitated as the decidedly less soluble carbonate; and no equilibrium can result until practically all of the sulfate has been changed to carbonate. This reaction, naturally, does not proceed nearly so rapidly as it would if all the strontium sulfate were in solution at the same time. In order to increase the velocity of the reaction, the solution is heated and an excess of sodium carbonate is used. Write an equation for the reaction. 

The objects of this, and of the next exercise, are two-fold: first, to provide a brief review of some of the general reactions of elementary chemistry; and second, to afford opportunity for you to learn something of correct manipulation by carrying out a series of reactions on more than a test tube scale. In order to prevent mistakes, you must read the directions for an entire section and assure yourself of the sequence of operations before you commence work. Procedure 

1. Crystallized strontium chloride, SrC12.6H₂O. Place 350 cc. of water in a pyrex beaker of about 800 cc. capacity, and add 

61 g. of very finely powdered celestite (natural strontium sulfate, with some impurities) and 190 g. of hydrated sodium carbonate, Na2CO3-10H2O. Heat the mixture to boiling, and keep it boiling gently for 30 minutes. The strontium sulfate is very heavy and settles to the bottom producing a tendency for the solution 14

PREPARATION OF STRONTIUM CHLORIDE 

to "bump." To avoid this, and to insure complete reaction, stir the mixture constantly until the sodium carbonate is dissolved and frequently during the rest of the time. After 30 minutes of gentle boiling, add 100 cc. of water to replace that lost by evaporation, let the solid material settle, and carefully decant (p. 7) as much as possible of the fairly clear liquid into a clean beaker. What does this solution contain? Save it for use later. Wash the residue three times by decantation, each time using about 400 cc. of water and discarding the washings. Some small amount of finely divided residue will be carried away during the washing but, with reasonable care, this loss will not be large. After completing these washings, set aside an estimated tenth of the residue of strontium carbonate in a small beaker. Measure out 115 cc. of dilute (6 N) hydrochloric acid. To the remaining nine-tenths of the strontium carbonate add 50 cc. of water, heat to boiling, and while keeping the suspension gently boiling add the dilute hydrochloric acid, first a few cubic centimeters at a time, and later with great care, until effervescence ceases. Avoid much excess acid. Especially during the addition of the last third of the acid there is a tendency for the solution to froth. This is reduced if the solution is stirred constantly and kept hot. 

Add 10 cc. of chlorine water to this slightly acid solution, decant a few drops, and make a test for ferric ion,¹ traces of which are usually present as impurity in the original mineral. Now add, gradually, the remainder of the strontium carbonate which was previously set aside, and boil for several minutes. If the addition of the hydrochloric acid was done carefully, the slight excess of the acid will be neutralized by the strontium carbonate and all of the iron impurity precipitated as ferric hydroxide. Filter (p. 7) the now neutral solution and boil down the clear filtrate until a faint scum of crystals will form on removing the flame and blowing across the surface of the liquid. Cool the solution, stirring meanwhile to prevent caking of the crystals, and filter by suction. (Instructions from assistant.) Wash the adhering crystals from the beaker with some of the filtrate. Evaporate the filtrate exactly as before for a second crop of crystals; if these are colorless, add them to the first crop. Wrap the entire yield of the salt in 

1 Test for ferric ion by acidifying the sample with dilute hydrochloric acid and adding a few drops of potassium ferrocyanide solution. A blue color is indication of ferric ion.

PREPARATION OF STRONTIUM CHLORIDE 

several thicknesses of filter paper and dry for a short time. The crystals contain six molecules of water of hydration and are quite efflorescent. Hence weigh them as soon as dry and record the yield. Retain 5 g. of the salt (preferably from the second crop) and turn in the remainder in a labelled bottle. 

2. Recovery of sodium sulfate. The liquid which was first decanted from the strontium carbonate contains most of the sodium sulfate formed during this stage of the preparation, along with any excess sodium carbonate. Place in this solution a strip of litmus paper and slowly add dilute sulfuric acid, stirring all the time, until the solution is slightly acid to litmus. After filtering until a perfectly clear filtrate is obtained, boil this down to about one-half the original volume and set it aside in your desk, uncovered, until the next period. Drain on a suction filter any crystals which separate, and dry them for a short time by spreading them on a sheet of coarse filter paper. Weigh the crystals as soon as dry and turn them in in a tightly stoppered bottle, as this salt also contains water of hydration and is extremely efflorescent. 

3. Conversion of strontium chloride to strontium nitrate. Place about 5 g. of strontium chloride in a casserole, cover with 15 cc. of concentrated nitric acid, and evaporate the mixture slowly (hood) until a pasty mass remains. Add 5 cc. more of the nitric acid, and evaporate again until the residue appears perfectly dry and no odor of nitric acid can be noticed. Use a low flame during the removal of the last traces of acid, as the nitrate can be decomposed rather easily. When quite dry, dissolve a very small portion of the residue in 5 cc. of water, add a drop of nitric acid, and test for the presence of chloride by the addition of a little silver nitrate solution. If more than a faint turbidity results, repeat the evaporation with nitric acid until a test shows that the chloride has been entirely converted to the nitrate. 

Scrape the dry strontium nitrate from the casserole and mix it thoroughly on a sheet of paper (do not grind) with 0.8 g. of powdered sulfur and 0.1 g. of finely powdered charcoal. Take a portion of this mixture to the hood and ignite it.

EXERCISE IV PREPARATION OF FERRIC AMMONIUM ALUM FROM IRON 

The steps involved in this preparation are: the dissolving of iron in dilute sulfuric acid; oxidation of the resulting ferrous sulfate by nitric acid; and, after adding the calculated amount of ammonium sulfate, the crystallization of the alum, Fe2(SO4)3·(NH4)2SO4·24H 20. 

Since most of the available forms of iron dissolve quite slowly in sulfuric acid, the first part of this preparation should be started while working on the preceding exercise. 

1. Ferrous sulfate. Obtain 11 g. (approximately one-fifth mole) of iron wire. Place this wire in a 250 cc. flask, cover it with 75 cc. of water and slowly add 25 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid, rotating the contents of the flask to insure thorough mixing. Partly close the flask by means of a cork stopper in the side of which several grooves have been cut to allow for the escape of hydrogen. 

Set the flask in a hot water bath until all the iron has dissolved. 

This will require from one to two hours, depending on the kind of wire available, but no attention is necessary during this period. 

If the flask has to be set aside from one period to the next, some ferrous sulfate may crystallize out, but this is easily dissolved when the solution is again heated. 

2. Oxidation of ferrous sulfate. When all the iron has dissolved, filter the hot solution in order to remove impurities, such as carbon, which were in the iron. Place the clear filtrate in a large casserole or 400 cc. pyrex beaker, and heat it nearly to boiling. 

With constant stirring, slowly add to this hot solution 10 cc. of dilute (6 N) nitric acid, and then add more nitric acid drop by drop until the brisk evolution of brown fumes ceases. Avoid excess nitric acid. Some care must be used during the addition of the last of the nitric acid, or the evolution of gas may become so rapid that the solution boils over. When oxidation seems complete, add 14 g. of ammonium sulfate. Heat the solution to boiling for a moment in order to expel the last of the oxides of nitrogen, and 18

FERRIC AMMONIUM ALUM 

filter into a beaker or porcelain evaporating dish. Let this solution stand uncovered for a day or two. As the water slowly evaporates, large crystals of ferric alum should form on the bottom. 

Pick these out, rinse by dipping them in a beaker of water, and dry them on coarse filter paper. A second crop of crystals may usually be obtained by evaporating the solution still further. 

1. Why is ferrous sulfate, not ferric, formed when iron dissolves in dilute 

H2SO4? Would it make any difference if more H₂SO, were used? 

2. Write an equation for the oxidation of FeSO4 by HNO3 under the conditions of this experiment. Why did the solution of FeSO, become nearly black when the first nitric acid was added? 

3. Show by equations how to obtain ferrous ammonium sulfate from ferric ammonium alum. 

4. Would an alum be classed as a double salt or as a complex salt? Give reason for your answer. 

5. Using one-fifth mole of iron, as in this exercise, calculate: (a) Volume (at standard conditions) of hydrogen evolved when the iron dissolves in H2SO4; (b) Weight of anhydrous FeSO, formed in solution; (c) Weight of HNO, required to oxidize the FeSO4; (d) Volume of 6 N HNO3 required to furnish this weight of HNO3; (e) Volume of NO (at standard conditions) evolved during the oxidation; (f) Weight of alum formed from one-fifth mole of iron.

EXERCISE V DETERMINATION OF TRANSITION TEMPERATURES 

Every pure substance, if stable, is characterized by a definite melting point and boiling point. Melting point is the temperature at which the solid and liquid phases are at equilibrium; and at the boiling point the liquid and vapor phases are in equilibrium under a pressure of 760 mm. Quite frequently it happens that there is more than one solid modification of a given substance. Such solid modifications may be different crystal forms of the solid, or the solid with varying amounts of water of hydration. In any such case, however, there is a perfectly definite temperature-entirely analogous to melting point-at which one solid form is transformed into the other. In this exercise two such transitions are studied; the first in a qualitative manner; the second with somewhat more care. 

1. Determination of the transition temperature of cupro mercuric iodide. This solid exists in two modifications which have distinctly different colors. Hence the transition point may be determined approximately by noting the temperature at which the color change takes place.

Blow a small bulb (p. 5) on one end of a thin-walled glass tube about 12 cm. in length and introduce into the bulb a very little dry cupro-mercuric iodide.¹ Shake this until a thin layer of the solid adheres uniformly to the walls of the bulb. By means of a clamp and cork support this tube in such a way that the bulb is about in the middle of a 250 cc. beaker of water. Hang a thermometer in this water bath so that the bulb of the thermometer is close by the bulb containing the cupro-mercuric iodide. Heat the water, keeping it well stirred, and note the approximate temperature at which the salt begins to change color. Now cool 

Cupro-mercuric iodide is readily prepared in the following manner: To a solution of 5 g. of mercuric chloride in 50 cc. of water add 12 g. of potassium iodide in 

25 cc. of water. Dissolve 10 g. of sodium sulfite in 35 cc. of water, acidify with 

5 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid and pour this at once into the clear solution of potassium mercuric iodide. Immediately add 8 g. of copper sulfate dissolved in 

50 cc. of water. Stir for a few minutes and filter the precipitated salt. A very little of this substance suffices, and the same material may be used indefinitely. 20

TRANSITION TEMPERATURES 

the bath to at least 5° below this temperature and reheat very slowly (about 1° per minute), this time noting as closely as possible the temperature at which the color change begins. Then allow the bath to cool and read the temperature at which the reverse color change occurs. Average these two temperatures, which may be several degrees apart, and record the average as the transition temperature of this substance. 

The values obtained by heating at this rate will not be accurate, as sufficient time is not allowed for equilibrium to become established, but by approaching the point from both directions a sufficiently close value may be obtained. 

2. Transition temperature of Na2SO4 10H2O Na2SO4. Con struction of a cooling curve. Obtain a cork stopper which will fit your large pyrex test tube. Bore a hole in this stopper into which your thermometer will fit snugly, and cut a deep groove in the side of the cork through which a slender copper wire can pass. 

One end of this wire is bent into a loop a little smaller in diameter than the test tube, so that the wire can be used as a stirrer. Insert the thermometer through the cork so that, when the cork is inserted in the test tube, the bulb of the thermometer is about 1 cm. from the bottom of the tube. 

Place in the test tube 10-15 g. of freshly crystallized sodium sulfate, Na2SO4·10H2O. Melt most of this hydrated salt by dipping the test tube containing it into a beaker of hot water. 

There will always be some anhydrous sodium sulfate suspended in the melt. Insert the cork, together with the thermometer and stirrer, and cool the tube rather slowly till the thermometer registers 40°. It may be desirable to dip the tube briefly into cool water to avoid too long a wait. When 40° is reached, note the time, and now let the tube cool by itself, while you stir the contents frequently. During this cooling period read the temperature every three minutes and plot the change of temperature against time on the chart in your manual. (If by any chance the temperature falls to 30°, drop into the test tube a few crystals of hydrated sodium sulfate, and continue to stir and record the temperature as before.)

So long as the melt undergoes no change of state, a continuous cooling curve will be obtained; but if a new phase appears (in this case the solid, Na2SO4-10H2O), there will be a sharp break in the curve, and during the formation of this phase no further fall in

TRANSITION TEMPERATURES 

the temperature of the melt will occur. Why? Follow the cooling curve of your melt until such a break does occur, and then record five more temperature readings at three-minute intervals. Can you observe any change in the melt during this interval? What did you find to be the transition temperature of Na2SO4 Na2SO4 10H₂O? 

1. Name three elements which are known in more than one solid form. How might you determine the transition temperature in one of these instances? 

2. On the basis of the Kinetic-molecular Theory, suggest a reasonable explanation for the existence of a given substance in more than one solid form. 

3. Suggest a possible use for a substance such as Cu₂HgI, where the change in form is accompanied by a color change. 

4. Construct a diagram showing the type of curve which would be obtained if a sample of pure water were cooled at a uniform rate from +10° to -10°. 

Interpret the meaning of each part of the curve.

EXERCISE VI FACTORS AFFECTING THE RATE OF CHEMICAL REACTION 

Molecules, like people, are affected by a change of conditions, though not necessarily in the same way. Crowding more molecules into a given space naturally affords more opportunities of contact; heat speeds up molecular motion, again leading to increased chance of contact. Consequently a rise in concentration or in temperature should increase reaction velocity. The same effect may at times be secured by the use of a catalyst. This exercise provides a demonstration of the effect of these factors on different reactions. 

1. Effect of surface. Place not more than 0.5 g. of potassium permanganate crystals on a watch glass. Grind a similar amount of the salt to a very fine powder and spread the powder on a second watch glass. Take both of these to the hood and add to each not more than three drops of glycerine. Record, and account for, the difference observed. No equation is required for the reaction. 

2. Effect of catalyst. In a pyrex test tube place about 2 g. of potassium chlorate. Carefully melt this salt by holding the tube some distance above a Bunsen flame. When the salt is just melted, determine whether or not oxygen is being evolved by noting whether there is a visible evolution of gas from the liquid salt, and also by inserting a glowing splint into the upper part of the tube. Then remove the test tube from the flame, and at once add a pinch of ferric oxide. Test for oxygen as before, and record your observations. Define the term "catalyst." (Students should work in pairs while carrying out the next two experiments.) 

3. Effect of temperature. In each of three clean, dry test tubes place 10 cc. of the dilute, acid solution of potassium permanganate especially prepared for this experiment.¹ Make your measure- 

11.0 g. of potassium permanganate is dissolved in 800 cc. of water. Add 200 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid slowly, and cool. This solution should be freshly prepared. 23

Record the time, and drop into the test tube a bright iron nail about an inch long. Shake the tube gently during the ensuing reaction, but do not remove it from the hot water. Note, and record, the time in seconds which was required for the color of the potassium permanganate to disappear. Now heat the water bath to 75° and in exactly the same way find the time required to destroy the color in a second 10 cc. portion of the potassium permanganate solution. Finally, use the third 10 cc. portion at a temperature of 90°, and again record the time in seconds for the color to disappear. Record all the data in the following table.

EXERCISE VII SOLUBILITY 

Whether it is a matter of sweetening one's coffee, of removing a grease spot, or of aiding a chemical reaction, some phase of solubility is of interest to every one. In this exercise certain of the factors affecting solubility are noted, and some applications of differences in solubility pointed out. 

1. Factors affecting solubility. (a) Nature of the solvent. Put about a gram of sodium chloride in each of two test tubes. To one, add 5 cc. of water, and to the second add 5 cc. of alcohol. 

Shake the tubes gently for a few moments and compare the solubility of sodium chloride in the two liquids. In the same way compare the solubility of a very small fragment of rosin in 5 cc. portions of water and of alcohol. 

Place a very small crystal of iodine in each of three test tubes and add, respectively, 5 cc. of water, alcohol, and carbon disulfide. 

Is iodine soluble in each of these solvents? Can you observe any difference in the degree of solubility of iodine in the three solvents? 

Mix about 0.2 g. each of sodium chloride and lithium chloride; place the mixture in a dry test tube, add 5 cc. of alcohol, and warm the tube by dipping it into warm water, meanwhile shaking it gently. After a few minutes decant most of the alcohol into a clean porcelain dish. What is the insoluble residue? (Refer to your notes on the first paragraph of this section.) Dip a clean platinum wire into the liquid just decanted and hold the wire in a Bunsen flame. The red color which results is due to the lithium compound. 

This particular separation is often applied in Qualitative Analysis (p. 29 of Section II). Note: If no red flame coloration is obtained or if it is indistinct, burn off much of the alcohol and repeat the test. Ordinary alcohol contains at least 5% of water. Hence enough sodium chloride always dissolves to impart some yellow color to the flame.

In all the preceding illustrations it should be noted that solubility is dependent not only on the nature of the solvent, but also 26

SOLUBILITY 

27 on the nature of the solute itself. There is no satisfactory theory which will account for all such differences in solubility. (b) Temperature. Insert a thermometer into a test tube onethird full of water, wait a moment until the mercury has attained the temperature of the water, then note the thermometer reading. 

Add about 2 g. of potassium nitrate and stir until the salt dissolves. 

Again read the temperature. On the basis of this heat change, what prediction can you make about the expected change in the solubility of a solid with a rise in temperature? 

To a few drops of lead acetate solution in a test tube add about an equal amount of potassium iodide solution. Nearly fill the test tube with water, heat to boiling and, if all of the precipitate does not dissolve, let the excess settle and decant the clear solution into another test tube. Cool this hot solution by running tap-water over the tube. 

Result? Does this agree with the predictions you just made concerning the effect of a rise in temperature on the solubility of solids? 

Clamp an inverted test tube full of water in a beaker of water, and heat the upper part of the tube. If steam forms, it will condense to water as the temperature falls. What, then, are the gas bubbles which remain at the top of the tube after cooling? How does a rise in temperature affect the solubility of a gas? Account for this effect. Mention a second condition which affects the solubility of gases, and state the nature of this effect. Why does a bottle of ginger ale effervesce when opened? 

2. Supersaturation. Roll a paper so that the hollow cylinder formed fits into, and extends to the bottom of, a clean dry test tube. Through this paper roll introduce 5 g. of crystallized sodium acetate, then remove the paper. In this way no crystals are left on the walls of the tube. Add 2 cc. of water and heat the tube by dipping it into a beaker of boiling water, until all of the salt has dissolved. If, after continued heating, some of the salt still remains undissolved, add more water a drop or two at a time until a clear solution is obtained. Cover the test tube with a filter paper and set it aside where it will remain undisturbed until cold. Then drop a small crystal of sodium acetate into the solution. Account for the result. Feel of the tube after crystallization occurs to note the attendant heat effect. What is a supersaturated solution? 

Why is it necessary to dissolve all of the salt before setting the solution aside to cool? Can a supersaturated solution be obtained directly by shaking a solute with the solvent?

SOLUBILITY 

3. Extraction, and its application. In exp. 1 of this exercise you compared the solubility of iodine in water and in carbon disulfide, and may have found that the solubility of iodine in water was so slight that it escaped notice. Make a further comparison in the following manner. 

Shake a tiny fragment of iodine with 10 cc. of water. If not all of the iodine will dissolve after repeated shaking, carefully decant the solution into a clean tube. Now add 2 cc. of carbon disulfide to the water solution of iodine, close the tube with your thumb and mix thoroughly by shaking for a moment. Let the carbon disulfide settle, and account for the color which it now exhibits when viewed by transmitted light. Does any iodine remain dissolved in the water after this single treatment? Prove your answer. Such a process as you have just carried out is known as extraction, and is of considerable importance in chemical work. 

To 50 cc. of water add one drop of potassium iodide solution.

Mix thoroughly, and to 5 cc. of this very dilute solution add a few drops of dilute sulfuric acid and about 1 cc. of potassium dichromate solution. This oxidizing agent will oxidize an iodide to free iodine. 

Can you see any evidence of the liberation of iodine? Demonstrate that free iodine is actually present in this solution. 

Dilute the rest of the potassium iodide solution with 50 cc. more of water, and repeat the oxidation and test for iodine on 5 cc. of this solution. Continue in this way until you fail to obtain a decisive test. Would this procedure constitute a delicate test for an iodide in analytical work? 

4. Construction of a solubility curve for potassium dichromate. 

Place about 150 cc. of water in a 500 cc. flask, and heat until the water begins to boil.¹ Then add powdered potassium dichromate, a small quantity at a time, shaking the flask after each addition and maintaining the temperature at 95°-100°, until some of the solid remains undissolved. Since the success of this experiment depends on the preparation of a saturated solution (definition?) at this temperature, be sure to have a definite excess of the solid present. Since the rate at which a substance dissolves decreases as the solution approaches saturation, allow sufficient time for the solution to become truly saturated. 

1 In case this experiment is performed by successive divisions, considerable economy of material and of time may be secured by having the second (and following) classes use the solution turned in by preceding divisions.

SOLUBILITY 

In the meantime weigh four clean dry porcelain dishes as accurately as possible on the platform balance. Do not confuse the weights of the dishes, for this would nullify the remainder of the work.

Allow the saturated dichromate solution to cool to 90°, read the temperature as accurately as possible with an ordinary thermometer, then immediately decant what you estimate to be 25 cc. of the clear solution directly into one of the weighed dishes. Make no attempt to filter this solution, nor to measure the volume accurately. To do this will introduce errors. Weigh the dish and solution at once on the balance as accurately as you can. 

When the remainder of the dichromate solution has cooled to 70°, again decant about 25 cc. into the second weighed dish. 

Record the exact temperature just previous to decantation. 

Weigh the dish and solution as before. Then permit the dichromate solution to cool to 50° and repeat the operation of decanting a portion, using the third weighed dish. Use the remaining dish for a fourth portion of the solution decanted when the temperature has fallen to 30°. Each dish with its solution of the salt must be weighed immediately after decanting the liquid into it. It is suggested that different pairs of students choose different sets of temperatures; for example, 80°, 60°, 40°, 20°. By holding the flask, containing the saturated solution, under running tap-water, temperatures below that of the room may be reached. 

Evaporate each solution to dryness. A steam bath or its equivalent is preferable for this purpose, but if this is not available, heat the dish directly with a very low flame kept in constant motion under the dish. If, as the residue approaches dryness, there is a tendency to spatter, cover the dish with a watch glass. 

Particles of the dichromate adhering to the glass may be scraped back into the dish before weighing. Drive off the last traces of moisture by heating the dish more strongly for a few minutes. 

Do not, however, melt the salt, as decomposition may occur. 

Cool, and weigh the dish and residue. Determine: (1) the weight of the residue; (2) the weight of the water in which this residue was dissolved; (3) the weight of the dichromate held in solution by 100 g. of water at each temperature. EXERCISE VIII PROPERTIES OF SOLUTIONS 

Dissolved materials exhibit properties which are often strikingly different from the same material in solid form. At the same time, certain properties of the solvent are altered by the presence of the dissolved particles. Among the effects on the properties of the solvent, the change in vapor pressure—and the attendant change in boiling point—is perhaps the most important. This exercise provides opportunity for a quantitative study of this effect with the purpose, (a) of bringing out any connection between the concentration of the solute and the change of boiling point; (b) of determining whether different non-electrolytes produce the same or different effects; and (c) of establishing whether electrolytes differ from non-electrolytes in their effect. 

1. Depression of the vapor pressure by non-electrolytes. A liquid boils at the temperature at which its vapor pressure equals the pressure of the atmosphere. Consequently, if the addition of any substance lowers the vapor pressure of the liquid, more heatand hence a higher temperature-is necessary before the liquid boils. In other words, a rise in boiling point is a measure of the lowering of the vapor pressure.

Support a 60 cc. pyrex test tube in an upright position in a clamp on the ring stand. (A small beaker supported by a wire gauze may be used in place of the test tube, but with less satisfactory results.) Add about 30 cc. of water to the tube and hang a thermometer in such a way that the bulb is in the middle of the water. Bend one end of a copper wire into a loop a little less in diameter than the test tube, and use this as a stirrer when heating the water and reading the temperature. By means of a small flame bring the water to a steady but not vigorous boiling. Read the temperature recorded by your thermometer as accurately as possible, estimating to a tenth of a degree. The boiling point of pure water is 100° C., but your thermometer will probably indicate a temperature other than this because the thermometer is 31

PROPERTIES OF SOLUTIONS 

not an accurate one. However, since we are measuring only the difference in boiling points, it will serve as well as an accurateand more expensive-instrument. 

Now empty the test tube, and place in it exactly 25 g. (25 cc.) of water and 17 g. of cane sugar (very nearly 20 mole). Stir this well, heating it slowly with a small flame, and observe as accurately as can be done the temperature at which this solution boils, estimating to a tenth of a degree. Do not boil the solution too vigorously, or long enough to drive off an appreciable amount of water. Now add just 17 g. more of cane sugar to the solution in the test tube and repeat the observation of the boiling temperature. 

What relation is there between the rise in boiling point of the two solutions? What relation is there between the concentrations of these two solutions? From your data, calculate the molecular elevation of the boiling point of water for each of these solutions and record the average of the two as an approximate value. (The formula of cane sugar is C12H22O11.) Compare your experimental result with the correct value for the molecular elevation of the boiling point of water.

Empty the test tube, wash it out with water, then place in the tube exactly 25 g. (25 cc.) of water and 9 g. of dextrose. Proceed as above to determine the boiling temperature of this solution. 

Then add 9 g. more of dextrose to the solution in the test tube and again observe the boiling temperature. Record these values in the accompanying table. 

Compare (a) the change in boiling point produced by each portion of dextrose with the change produced by each portion of sugar, and (b) the values for the molecular elevation of the boiling point of water as calculated from the data of the two experiments. 

State any general principles which may become obvious from the results of these experiments. 

2. Abnormal depression of the vapor pressure caused by an electrolyte. A substance is classified as an electrolyte if its water solution will conduct the electric current. Remember, however, that this is merely an observation of fact, and that the ability of a solution to carry the current does not, in itself, afford any explanation whatever of why it is a conductor. 

Wash out the test tube in which the sugar solutions have been used, and prepare a new solution of 3 g. of sodium chloride (essentially 20 mole) in 25 g. (25 cc.) of water. By exactly the same

3. Deliquescence. Some substances are sufficiently soluble so that the vapor pressure of water from their saturated solutions is less than the pressure of water vapor in the air. If this is the case, moisture will condense from the air into these solutions until each becomes sufficiently dilute for equilibrium to be established. Observe, however, that deliquescence is more a property of the solution of a substance than of the substance itself; and that the phenomenon is also dependent on the relative humidity of the surrounding air. Hence in an arid climate-such as a steamheated chemistry laboratory-deliquescence is far less noticeable than in a summer home by the sea. 

Observe samples of the following materials which have been exposed to the air for some time in the laboratory: CuSO4.5H2O; sugar; MgCl2-6H2O; Na2SO4-10H2O; CaCl2-6H2O; NaOH; ZnCl2. 

Determine whether or not any of these materials exhibit deliquescence under the existing conditions by comparing the exposed sample of each compound with a reference sample of the same substance contained in a sealed tube. If any case of deliquescence is observed, explain carefully why it took place, and explain also why the other substances did not deliquesce. 

In one test tube place about a gram of crystallized calcium chloride, put a thin covering of cotton over the solid, and drop on to the cotton a crystal of cupric sulfate. In a second tube put a crystal of cupric sulfate only. Cork both tubes tightly and set aside until the next period. Compare the appearance of the cupric sulfate in the two tubes. Account for any difference.

EXERCISE IX IONIZATION AND ITS APPLICATIONS 

In the preceding exercise evidence was obtained that certain classes of solutes—the electrolytes—exert an "abnormal" effect on the vapor pressure of the solvent. Evidence of this nature was to a great degree responsible for the development of the theory of ionization. In the present exercise we begin a study of ions, with particular attention to some of the factors influencing their formation, and to the practical applications of some of these factors. 

1. Electrical conductivity. For testing the conductivity of different liquids, employ the assembly illustrated in Fig. 6. A pyrex wwww test tube is preferable, and the tin electrodes should project into the test tube not more than 

5-6 mm. beyond the stopper.

This assembly has the advantage of maintaining a constant electrode surface in contact with the liquid; it also permits the use of very small quantities of liquid, for only a quantity need be used sufficient to cover the electrodes when the test tube is inverted. After the liquid has been placed in the test tube, the stopper carrying the electrodes is inserted tightly, the tube inverted, and the electrodes brought in contact with the terminals of a direct current supply so assembled that a suitable resistance and galvanometer are in series with the cell. A second resistance, if necessary, is connected with the galvanometer in order to keep the deflection of the needle within the limits of the scale. In a similar way test the conductivity of solutions of alcohol and of sugar in distilled water.

How can you account for the differences in the conductivity of the solutions just tested? What objection is there to saying that the conductivity of such solutions is in itself a proof of the existence of ions?

EXERCISE X ELECTROLYSIS 

1. Electrolysis. When solutions of acids, bases, and salts are decomposed by electricity, one of three possibilities exists, based on the nature of the products appearing at each electrode: (a) primary products may be formed at both electrodes; (b) a primary product at one electrode, and a secondary at the other; (c) secondary products at both electrodes. In each of the following decompositions your observations will assist in determining which type of electrolysis has occurred, and the nature of the product formed. (a) Half-fill a small U-tube with dilute hydriodic acid solution. 

Insert a tin electrode in each arm of the U-tube, connect the electrodes to the terminals of a direct current outlet, and electrolyze the solution for several minutes. Observe the evolution of a colorless gas (hydrogen) at one electrode, and the deposition of a brown substance on the other electrode. What is this deposit? 

What is the nature of the products of electrolysis of hydriodic acid? Wipe the brown deposit off the electrode with filter paper, and wash both the U-tube and electrodes with distilled water before proceeding with (b). (b) Fill the U-tube as above with a solution of potassium iodide, and electrolyze this solution for several minutes. Account for the brown deposit on one of the electrodes, and the color of the solution in the neighborhood of that electrode. What is the colorless gas appearing at the other electrode? Account for its formation. Test the solution in the neighborhood of the pole with red litmus paper. What is present in the solution? At which pole has the secondary product appeared?

Clean the electrodes and U-tube as before, then place in the tube copper sulfate solution and electrolyze this. What substance appears at the electrode at which hydrogen was evolved in the preceding experiment? Account for its formation. At the other electrode the white substance appearing in the vicinity of the pole is an oxide of tin (SnO2). What is the source of the oxygen? 40

ELECTROLYSIS 

State how it is formed. At which electrode has the secondary product appeared in this case? The copper deposit may be removed from the pole by holding the electrode for a moment in warm, dilute nitric acid, then washing off the acid. (c) Electrolyze a solution of sodium sulfate in the same manner as in the preceding cases. Record and explain what you observe taking place at each electrode. Drop pieces of red and blue litmus into the solution in the immediate neighborhood of each pole. 

What products are indicated, respectively? Account for their presence. What type of decomposition product is obtained at each electrode in the electrolysis of sodium sulfate solution? 

2. Faraday's law. In the preceding experiment observations were made of the chemical changes taking place at the anode and cathode when an electric current is passed through a solution of an electrolyte. Such observations, however, fail to give a quantitative measure of the chemical change at each electrode. Faraday's law is a statement of the fact that a given quantity of electrical energy will liberate equivalent amounts of different elements from solutions of their salts. For the deposition of the gram-equivalent weight of any element, 96,500 coulombs of electricity are required. 

While working on this demonstration of Faraday's law, students will work in pairs. Obtain two electrodes of sheet copper, and two of sheet tin. Polish the surface of each electrode with fine emery paper until clean and bright. Bend the upper part of each electrode. at right angles so that they may be suspended from the lip of a small beaker. Scratch a number on each electrode and weigh each to the nearest centigram.

Place the weighed copper electrodes in one small beaker and the weighed tin electrodes in a second beaker of the same size. 

Connect one copper electrode with one of the tin electrodes in the second beaker. This may be done conveniently by means of a copper wire, to the ends of which small clips are attached. The remaining electrodes are connected to a source of direct current, with a 60 watt lamp in series to provide the necessary resistance. 

Cover the copper electrodes with a 1 N solution of copper sulfate, and the tin electrodes with a 1 N solution of stannous chloride. 

Turn on the current and allow the electrolysis to continue for about one hour.

Copper and tin are dissolved from the respective anodes and deposited on the cathodes. With a stirring rod break off the crystals

ELECTROLYSIS 

of tin as they grow out from the cathode, so as to avoid a short circuit in the cell. After the current is turned off, remove the copper cathode and the tin anode and wash them with distilled water, then alcohol. Dry them by holding them several inches above a low flame. When thoroughly dry, weigh them accurately. 

Note the gain in weight of the copper electrode and the loss in weight of the tin electrode. (The tin cathode cannot readily be dried without loss of tin; hence the loss in weight of the anode is determined. This should, of course, be equal to the gain in weight of the cathode.)

From the weight of the copper deposited, calculate what weight of tin should have been deposited at the same time. Compare this value with your experimental result, and calculate the per cent error. Your formal report on the experiment should take the form of the accompanying table.

EXERCISE XI MOLECULAR AND ATOMIC WEIGHTS 

1. Gram-molecular volume. Measurements of a high degree of precision have indicated that the weight of a liter of oxygen at standard conditions of temperature and pressure is 1.429 g. The mole of oxygen is equal to 32 g. Why? Compute the volume occupied by a mole of oxygen under standard conditions. Why does it follow that a mole of any gas, under the same conditions, will occupy this same volume? To what, then, does the problem of finding the molecular weight of any gas (or easily volatilized liquid) reduce? 

2. Molecular weight of a gas (carbon dioxide). Obviously it is inconvenient to work with volumes as large as 22.4 liters, and 

FIG. 7.-Molecular Weight of Carbon Dioxide. 

at exactly 0° C. and 760 mm. However, it is possible to determine experimentally the weight of a certain convenient volume of the gas under the existing conditions of temperature and pressure and, by application of the proper gas laws, compute the weight of 22.4 liters at standard conditions.

Equip a liter flask as indicated in Fig. 7. The short glass tube must have its lower end flush with the bottom of the stopper. 43

MOLECULAR AND ATOMIC WEIGHTS 

Rubber connectors carrying pinch clamps are attached to the outer ends of both glass tubes. Be sure that the flask and connections are dry, then weigh the flask with its stopper, glass tubes, rubber connections, and clamps, by suspending it from one side of the horn pan balance by means of a copper wire wound round its neck. Leave the wire attached to the flask during the remainder of the experiment.

For generating and drying carbon dioxide, arrange the remainder of the apparatus shown in Fig. 7. Cover the bottom of the generator bottle, G, with marble to the depth of 3 cm. In the second bottle, W, place 50 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid (handle with care). Connect the two bottles as indicated, then attach the flask to the second bottle by means of the rubber connector on the glass bend with the long arm. Remove the two pinch clamps, add enough water through the thistle tube to just cover the marble and the lower end of the thistle tube. Then add a few drops of concentrated hydrochloric acid. Allow a slow stream of carbon dioxide to flow through the flask, the rate of flow being observed by noting the rapidity with which the gas bubbles through the sulfuric acid. More of the concentrated hydrochloric acid may be added to the generator from time to time to maintain the flow of gas. At the end of thirty minutes, disconnect the flask from the rest of the apparatus, replace the pinch clamps, and weigh on the accurate balance. Connect the flask to the apparatus again and allow the carbon dioxide to flow through the flask for fifteen minutes. Then remove the flask, immediately replace the pinch clamps, and weigh again. Repeat until successive weighings agree. 

Record also the temperature and barometric pressure. 

The weight of the carbon dioxide is the difference between the weight of the flask full of the gas and the vacuous flask; the volume of the carbon dioxide is the volume of the flask. Determine the capacity of the flask by filling it with water up to the pinch clamps, leaving the glass tubing in place, and weighing the flask and contents on the platform balance. The weight of the water, and hence its volume (why?) or the capacity of the flask, may be taken as the difference between the above weight and the weight of the flask in air. To find the weight of the vacuous flask, the weight of the air contained at the observed temperature and pressure must be known. For this, refer to a table of the densities of air, select the value for the observed conditions, multiply this by the

MOLECULAR AND ATOMIC WEIGHTS 

volume of the flask, and thus arrive at the weight of the air contained in the flask. What then is the weight of the vacuous flask? 

You are now in a position to find the weight of the carbon dioxide. 

Record your data in the accompanying table. These data furnish you with the weight of a certain volume of carbon dioxide at operating conditions of temperature and pressure. Compute the molecular weight of the carbon dioxide. State all the laws which you have applied in making your calculations.

Note: When you disconnect and put aside the apparatus, wash the unused marble remaining in the generator, and return this to the pan provided for it. Pour the sulfuric acid from the second bottle carefully and cautiously into the jar provided for this acid. 

Then wash the bottle thoroughly with water. 

3. Atomic weight of tin. When the molecular weights of a number of compounds of a given element have been determined, it is then possible to derive the atomic weight of that element. 

Experimental methods are available for the determination of molecular weights (consult text). If each substance, for which a molecular weight has been determined, is analyzed, the weight of the element present in a mole of each of these substances may then be calculated. This weight will be either (a) the atomic weight (if only one atom of the element is present), or (b) a multiple of the atomic weight (if two or more atoms of the element are present in a molecule of the compound). If a large number of compounds of the same element are examined in this way, it will be safe to assume that the smallest weight of the element found in one mole of its compounds is the relative weight of one atom of that element. 

When the steps outlined above were carried out with those compounds of tin the molecular weights of which are readily determined by experiment, the relative weight of the tin atom is found to be about 120. Such a value can be only approximate, however, for the experimental difficulties are such that molecular weights of the compounds cannot be determined with any high degree of accuracy. The exact atomic weight may be obtained, however, through the application of the concept that the atomic weight of an element is an integral multiple of its equivalent weight. 

The equivalent weight may be determined very accurately. The multiple necessary to compute the true atomic weight is indicated by the value for the approximate atomic weight, given above for tin.

MOLECULAR AND ATOMIC WEIGHTS 

Recall the definition of the equivalent or combining weight of an element. The reactions employed in the experimental procedure for determining the equivalent weight of tin result indirectly in the conversion of tin to tin dioxide, and may be represented by the following balanced equations.

Sn + 4HNO3 → H₂SnO3 + 4NO2 + H2O H2SnO3 (heated) → SnO2 + H₂O. 

Weigh a clean dry porcelain casserole (small size) on the accurate balance. Place about one gram of tin in the dish and reweigh accurately. How much tin have you taken? Take the dish to the hood, add 10 cc. of water, and then 10 cc. of concentrated nitric acid. Immediately cover the dish with a watch glass, and apply a gentle heat until the reaction begins. What evidence do you have that a reaction is taking place? Allow the reaction to continue, warming occasionally, and adding a little more nitric acid if it appears necessary, until all of the tin has disappeared and a white solid remains. Remove the watch glass, and wash back into the dish any adhering solid by means of a jet of water from the wash bottle. Support the dish on a beaker of boiling water (or steam bath if available) and allow the solution to evaporate until all visible liquid has disappeared. Then heat the dish directly over a low flame to remove the last traces of moisture. Allow the dish to cool, then weigh it with its contents on the accurate balance. 

Again heat the dish for a few minutes, and repeat the cooling and weighing. Repeat these operations until successive weighings are in accord. What weight of tin dioxide is formed? Arrange your data in a systematic manner and calculate the equivalent weight of tin.

How many times the equivalent weight of tin is the approximate atomic weight of tin? What integral multiple will you then employ to find the true atomic weight of tin? What does this number represent? What then, on the basis of your experimental results, do you find the true atomic weight of tin to be? Compare this with the value given in the table of atomic weights and compute your per cent error. What sources of error are there in the performance of the experiment?
