EXERCISE XII ACIDIMETRY. ALKALIMETRY 

In neutralization the essential reaction is the union of hydrogen ions with hydroxyl ions to form the slightly ionized substance, water. 

When the neutralization is carried out in such a manner that the volumes of both acid and base solutions are known accurately, the procedure is designated as titration. If the actual concentration of one solution is known, then the concentration of the other solution may be calculated. The term acidimetry refers to the determination of the concentration of an acid solution. Alkalimetry is the term used to designate the determination of the concentration of a base in solution. 

1. Titration. For this experiment prepare solutions as follows in separate beakers, and stir thoroughly to insure uniform composition throughout. (a) 50 cc. dilute sodium hydroxide and 250 cc. water (b) 20 cc. dilute hydrochloric acid and 100 cc. water (c) 20 cc. dilute sulfuric acid and 100 cc. water 

In titration a piece of apparatus known as a burette (Fig. 8) is used. Clean and rinse with water each of the burettes at your desk. Be careful not to drop the stopcock. Finally rinse out one burette with a small quantity of the prepared sodium hydroxide solution, and clamp this burette firmly in a vertical position on the left side of the burette stand. Now fill the burette with the solution of the base. Open the stopcock and allow the solution to flow out until the tip is filled with the solution. Now adjust the level of the solution in the burette so that the bottom of the meniscus (the curved surface of the liquid) coincides with the line representing the zero mark. In doing this and in all subsequent readings, be sure that your eye is on a level with the meniscus. 

Rinse the second burette with some of the prepared hydrochloric acid. Clamp this burette in position on the right side of the stand. Fill it with the acid, fill the tip as in the case of the first burette, and adjust the level of the liquid to the zero mark. 48

ACIDIMETRY. ALKALIMETRY 

Run out about 10 cc. of the acid solution into a clean beaker. 

Record the exact amount taken, in terms of hundredths of a cubic centimeter. Add two or three drops of phenolphthalein solution. 

Then add from the other burette sodium hydroxide solution, a few drops at a time, with stirring, until upon the addition of one drop of the base, the solution takes on a faint pink color which persists after the solution is stirred. If you accidentally add too much base, additional acid may be added to the beaker from the acid-containing burette (noting of course how much additional acid is taken) and the neutralization completed. Note exactly how much of the solution of base is required to neutralize the volume of acid taken. Repeat with a second and with a third sample of the acid. 

The strength of the acid relative to the sodium hydroxide solution may be found by dividing the number of cubic centimeters of the base required for neutralization by the number of cubic centimeters of the acid taken.

The three ratios so obtained should agree closely, or the titrations should be repeated until three concordant ratios are obtained. Average the results. What does this average ratio represent? Record all data in the table at the end of this exercise.

FIG. 8.-Burettes and Stand. 

Empty the acid burette, rinse with water, then with a little of the prepared sulfuric acid solution. Finally fill the burette with the sulfuric acid and proceed as directed above to titrate this acid against the sodium hydroxide solution. Titrate two more samples of the same acid. For each titration calculate the ratio of base to acid, and take the average of these ratios, recording all data in the table.

ACIDIMETRY. ALKALIMETRY 

2. Alkalimetry. Again empty the acid burette and rinse it out with water, then with a little of the standard hydrochloric acid obtained from the side table. Note and record the concentration of this standard acid as given on the label. Proceed as before to titrate the prepared sodium hydroxide solution against this standard acid until three concordant ratios of base to acid are obtained. 

Obtain the average ratio and bear in mind that it represents the strength of the acid relative to the base. Knowing the normality of the standard acid, calculate the normality of the solution of the base which you prepared and have been using in these titrations. Record these data. 

3. Acidimetry. Empty the acid burette just used, rinse out with water, then with some of a white vinegar solution. Finally fill the burette with white vinegar and proceed to titrate the vinegar against the sodium hydroxide solution. Perform three such titrations. From the quantities of base and acid used, calculate for each titration the strength of the acid relative to the base. Average these ratios, if concordant, and with this average ratio and the normality of the solution of sodium hydroxide, calculate the normality of the acid in vinegar.

EXERCISE XIII THE HYDRO-ACIDS 

Solutions of all acids exhibit a similar set of general reactions which of course are due to the hydrogen ion. These reactions are more or less pronounced depending on whether the acid is well ionized (strong) or poorly ionized (weak). But the really interesting reactions are those due mostly to the non-metal ion, and which are largely responsible for the "personality" of the acid. Among the common hydro-acids, four are derived from the closely related members of the halogen family, and a rather close study of the following experiments is required to observe much individuality among them. Further experiments will indicate, on the other hand, that the hydride, hydrogen sulfide, differs greatly from the halogen hydrides. 

1. Preparation and properties of hydrogen fluoride. (Caution: 

This compound is very corrosive. Do not inhale the gas or allow its solution to come in contact with the skin.) Coat one surface of a glass plate with paraffin by warming the plate over a small luminous flame until a few shavings of paraffin on the upper surface have just melted. Tilt the plate about so that the paraffin will form a layer of uniform thickness. When the plate becomes cold, cut a design through the paraffin with the point of a file or knife blade. The lines of the design must be deep enough to expose the glass. In a lead dish make a paste of a small amount of calcium fluoride with concentrated sulfuric acid, stirring the paste with a splint. Warm the dish very cautiously in the hood, until the evolution of gas is sufficient to cause the formation of white fumes when a glass rod previously dipped in ammonium hydroxide is held over the dish. Then place the plate, with coated side down, over the dish and allow this to stand in the hood for an hour or more. Finally scrape off the wax and note the effect on the glass where it was not protected. Explain. Has this reaction any commercial value? 

2. Hydrogen chloride. Hydrochloric acid. (a) Preparation.

Place 2-3 g. of sodium chloride in a large pyrex test tube, take this to the hood, and add 5 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid. Note 51

THE HYDRO-ACIDS 

the rapid evolution of gas, even without the application of heat. 

What is it? Hold wet blue litmus paper in this escaping gas; blow across the mouth of the test tube from which the gas is escaping, and account for the fuming thus produced. Bring a strip of filter paper moistened with ammonium hydroxide near the mouth of the test tube. What is the white smoke so produced? This reaction affords a convenient means of identifying any of the halogen hydrides if they are present in reasonable quantity.

Clean the test tube and again place in it 2-3 g. of salt, but this time dissolve the salt in 10 cc. of water. Then add, cautiously, 5 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid, and make the same tests as before. Explain carefully why hydrogen chloride is not evolved in appreciable amount under these conditions. 

Describe the compound, hydrogen chloride, including, at least, a statement concerning its state, color, odor, and solubility in water. 

Describe accurately the substance in the bottle marked "conc. HCI."

From a study of this section formulate the conditions necessary to prepare hydrogen chloride. Verify the correctness of your deductions by actually preparing, and demonstrating that you have prepared, the substance from a salt other than sodium chloride, and by the use of an acid other than sulfuric acid. Write equations to represent any reactions you employ. (b) Reactions. Write equations to represent the reactions which this acid should exhibit due to H+. Is this acid strong or weak? 

Stable or unstable? Volatile or non-volatile?

Place a pinch of manganese dioxide in a test tube and add 5 cc. of concentrated hydrochloric acid. Warm the mixture in the hood, and hold a strip of wet litmus paper in the escaping gas. The bleaching of the litmus demonstrates the presence of free chlorine in the vapors. Write an equation to account for the formation of this gas. In the same way warm together equal parts of concentrated hydrochloric acid and concentrated nitric acid, and again test for free chlorine in the evolved gases. State one good reason why hydrogen chloride cannot be prepared by the action of nitric acid on sodium chloride.

To 1 cc. of dilute hydrochloric acid add an equal volume of silver nitrate solution. Divide the resulting suspension into two parts, and test the solubility of the precipitate in nitric acid, and in ammonium hydroxide.

What is this gas?

Account for the difference in the action of a bromide with concentrated sulfuric acid and with phosphoric acid. (c) Phosphorus tribromide and water. EXERCISE XIV . HYPOCHLOROUS ACID A. Nitric Acid 

Nitric acid is usually rated second to sulfuric acid as an industrial chemical. On the basis of tonnage used there can be no question regarding its second place; but with regard to the value of its derivatives, this acid may well be considered the equal of sulfuric acid in importance. Foodstuffs, munitions of war, the color of one's apparel, may all have depended in some way on nitric acid for their preparation or production. In this exercise, however, we are concerned chiefly with a study of the oxidizing ability of this acid, since it is this property upon which most laboratory applications of nitric acid depend. 

1. Preparation of nitric acid. water and stand in it an empty 

Nearly fill a 500 cc. bottle with test tube; this provides a watercooled receiver for condensing the nitric acid. Roll a piece of paper into a cylinder, insert this into the neck of a retort, introduce 10 g. of sodium nitrate into the retort, and withdraw the paper. In this way no particles of sodium nitrate are left adhering to the neck of the retort. By means of a funnel, run in 20 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid, and insert the neck of the retort into the test tube receiver (Fig. 9). The whole apparatus will support itself. Heat the retort rather slowly and boil the mixture gently until about 5 cc. of the product have distilled into the receiver. The nitric acid prepared in this way is very concentrated; save it for use in exp. 3(c). Let the retort cool to room temperature before attempting to empty or clean it. 

FIG. 9.-Preparation of Nitric 

Acid. 56

2. Acid reactions of nitric acid. Represent by equations the reactions exhibited by nitric acid which are due to the H+. In what respect does nitric acid differ, in these reactions, from a typical acid such as hydrochloric acid? 

3. Oxidizing reactions of nitric acid. Pure nitric acid is a colorless liquid. Account for the color of the sample you have just prepared. Describe accurately the composition of the reagent on your desk marked "conc. HNO3." Is this reagent also colored? (a) Oxidation of metals. Pour a few cubic centimeters of dilute nitric acid upon several small pieces of copper and of zinc in separate test tubes. After reaction has begun, hold the test tubes before a white background and compare the color of the gas at the mouth of the tubes with that inside the tube. Account for your observations. What is the chief reduction product of dilute nitric acid? Represent these two reactions by equations balanced electronically. (b) Oxidation of a low-valent metal ion. In separate test tubes obtain a cubic centimeter of a solution of a mercurous and of a mercuric salt. To each add a few drops of dilute hydrochloric acid. 

Observe that the mercurous salt forms a white precipitate (HgCl), while no reaction is obtained with the mercuric salt. This will enable you to determine which valence of mercury results in the next experiment. 

Boil 2 cc. of mercurous nitrate solution with 5 cc. of concentrated nitric acid for several minutes, then add a drop of hydrochloric acid. Does any mercurous ion remain? Represent the reaction by an electronic equation. (c) Oxidation of a non-metal. To a small quantity of powdered sulfur in a large pyrex test tube, add cautiously 5 cc. of the concentrated nitric acid prepared in exp. 1, and warm the mixture in the hood for a few minutes. Note the color of the fumes in the test tube even after the air has been displaced. What appears to be the chief reduction product of concentrated nitric acid? Dilute the solution with 10 cc. of water and filter to remove any undissolved sulfur. To the clear filtrate, which of course is still acid, add a few drops of a solution of barium chloride. What happens?

For what ion is this a test? On the basis of the evidence obtained in this experiment, derive an equation for the action of nitric acid on sulfur.

(d) Oxidation of a non-metal ion. To 5 cc. of hydrogen sulfide water add an equal volume of concentrated nitric acid. Account for the precipitate which forms, and write an equation for its formation. 

4. Nitration. In a small flask mix 5 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid with 5 cc. of concentrated nitric acid. To this mixed acid add dropwise 3 cc. of benzene, CH, shaking the reaction flask after each addition. If the reaction tends to become violent, cool the flask under the tap. After all the benzene has been added, heat the flask in a water bath for 15-20 minutes, and then pour the mixture into 200 cc. of water. Note the characteristic odor of the oil which separates. This is mono-nitro-benzene. Occasionally a little di-nitro-benzene also forms and separates as a solid. What is the purpose of the concentrated sulfuric acid in this experiment? 

B. Nitrous Acid 

5. Nitrites and nitrous acid. (a) Instability of nitrous acid.

Place a small amount of solid sodium nitrite in a test tube and add 

5 cc. of dilute sulfuric acid. Result? Write equations for the reactions and state whether a nitrate, such as sodium nitrate, would have behaved similarly. How do nitrous and nitric acids differ with regard to (1) strength and (2) stability? (b) Oxidizing action. Coat a strip of filter paper with freshly precipitated lead sulfide by moistening the filter paper with a few drops of a solution of a lead salt and then exposing the moistened paper to the fumes from a little ammonium sulfide solution in a test tube, until the paper turns black. Dissolve half a gram of sodium nitrite in 10 cc. of water. Acidify this solution with 5 cc. of dilute sulfuric acid (what forms?), and at once dip the blackened filter paper into this solution. Write an equation to account for the change observed. 

To 5 cc. of water add one drop of potassium iodide solution and a few crystals of sodium nitrite. Acidify with 1 cc. of dilute sulfuric acid, and demonstrate that the iodide ion has been oxidized to free iodine by the nitrous acid (refer to Ex. VII, exp. 3). In the same way determine whether nitrous acid is a sufficiently powerful oxidizing agent to liberate bromine from a bromide. (c) Reducing action. Dissolve half a gram of sodium nitrite in 10 cc. of water, acidify with 5 cc. of dilute sulfuric acid, and

at once add 3-4 drops of potassium permanganate solution. The reaction in this case may be expressed as follows: 5HNO2 + 2KMnO4 + 3H2SO4 → 5HNO3 + K2SO4 + 2MnSO4 + 3H₂O. 

6. Tests for nitrate and nitrite. (a) Nitrate. The standard nitrate test depends not on a direct test but on the recognition of NO produced by the reduction of the nitrate. Dissolve a small crystal of a nitrate in 5 cc. of water, slowly add an equal volume of concentrated sulfuric acid, and cool the mixture well with running tap-water. Incline the test tube and carefully run in 5 cc. of ferrous sulfate solution from another test tube, so as to form a layer on top of the acid solution. A bring ring, appearing after a short time, indicates the presence of a nitrate. This ring is caused by the formation of a compound of indefinite composition, and may be represented as FeSO4xNO. Write equations to represent the formation of the nitric oxide in this experiment. Would a nitrite give the same test? Why? (b) Nitrite. A nitrite may be distinguished from a nitrate in the following manner. Acidify 5 cc. of ferrous sulfate solution with a few drops of dilute sulfuric acid, and add to this a solution of a nitrite. The black color which develops is due to the same compound that formed the ring in the nitrate test. Demonstrate that nitrates will not yield this color under the conditions of the nitrite test, and explain why they will not. 

C. Hypochlorous Acid

Hypochlorous acid is a weak acid and so unstable that even in dilute aqueous solution it cannot be kept very long. It is, therefore, usually prepared as wanted by acidifying a solution of sodium hypochlorite. (What other acid is obtained in a similar way?) In the laboratory the acid is of interest only because of its moderate oxidizing property. 

7. Hypochlorous acid as an oxidizing agent. Dissolve a tiny particle of iodine in carbon disulfide. Shake 5 cc. of bromine water with 2 cc. of carbon disulfide. Note the color of the carbon disulfide solutions of these two halogens, and save them for reference.

Prepare a mixture of three drops each of solutions of a bromide and an iodide. Acidify with 5 cc. of dilute sulfuric acid, and add 

3 cc. of carbon disulfide. Dilute some sodium hypochlorite solution with four or five volumes of water, and add this diluted solution drop by drop to the acidified halide solution. Shake the tube after each addition. Note the first color of the carbon disulfide, and the change which it undergoes. As the sodium hypochlorite is added to the acidified solution, hypochlorous acid is formed. Why? 

By the action of the hypochlorous acid, the following valence changes are accomplished successively: (1) I-I₂ I2 (2) Br→ Br₂ (3) I2→HIO3. 

If too much hypochlorite is added,

Br2 → HBrO3. 

Explain why the above valence changes occur in successive stages, and not simultaneously. Derive by the electronic method the equations for the successive reactions. In what capacity is the hypochlorous acid acting?

Secure a solution from the assistant, and determine by the above method whether or not the solution contains bromide with the iodide.

1. H2SO4 is a weaker acid than HNO3. Account for the fact that HNO3 may be displaced from its salts by H2SO4. What conditions would be required? 

2. HCl is as strong as HNO3. State two reasons-and illustrate one reason by equations why HCl cannot be used to prepare HNO, from a nitrate. 

3. In what way, other than the one employed in this exercise, may a solution of HOCI be prepared? 

4. Of the acids: HNO3, HNO2, and HOCl, which is the stronger oxidizing agent? Cite evidence to support your conclusion. 

5. Explain why HNO₂ may act both as oxidizer and as reducer. Mention several other acids which should possess the same ability.

B. Phosphoric Acid-Phosphorous Acid 

6. Preparation of phosphoric acid. (a) From a phosphate.

Weigh out 5 g. of crystallized disodium hydrogen phosphate and transfer it to a beaker of suitable size. Dissolve the salt in 100 cc. of hot water and add barium chloride solution until no further precipitation occurs. Filter and, with the aid of the wash bottle, wash the beaker and precipitate several times with water. Transfer the precipitate from the filter paper back to the beaker in which the precipitation was made. Dilute 2 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid with 25 cc. of water, and add this solution to the suspension of barium phosphate. Stir the suspension thoroughly and warm for several minutes. Filter, using a porcelain dish to receive the filtrate. Evaporate this filtrate to about one-fourth its volume and test the solution with litmus paper. Note the syrupy consistency of the liquid. Write equations for all reactions. In commercial practice what phosphate serves as a source of phosphoric acid? (b) From phosphorus. Devise a method by which phosphorus may be converted into phosphoric acid. Have this plan approved, and carry out the experiment, starting with 1 g. of phosphorus and obtaining the acid in water solution essentially free from other impurities. Represent the reactions by equations. 

7. Properties of phosphoric acid. In separate test tubes place half a gram of potassium iodide and potassium bromide. Add to each about 5 cc. of phosphoric acid and warm the tubes gently. Test the gases evolved with moist litmus paper. Account for the difference in the reaction of sulfuric acid and phosphoric acid with bromides and iodides. Now heat this residue strongly. The flashes of fire which occur are due to the combustion of phosphine, formed as a result of the self-oxidationreduction of the phosphorous acid according to the following reaction: 4H3PO→ PH3 + 3H3PO4. 

9. Test for a phosphate. Demonstrate that phosphoric acid is a product in the preceding experiment by dissolving the residue in 10 cc. of hot water, filtering if necessary, neutralizing with ammonium hydroxide, and adding a few drops of "magnesia mixture." (This reagent is an ammoniacal solution of a magnesium salt containing sufficient ammonium chloride to prevent the precipitation of magnesium hydroxide.) The white (sometimes slow-forming) precipitate of magnesium ammonium phosphate (MgNHẬPO₁) indicates the presence of phosphate. 

C. Boric Acid 

10. Preparation of boric acid. In a 250 cc. flask dissolve 38 g. of crystallized borax (formula?) in the minimum volume of boiling water. To the solution add a few drops of methyl orange. (This indicator is yellow-orange in neutral or alkaline solution; in acid solution, pink.) Then add concentrated hydrochloric acid, a small amount at a time, until the solution becomes and remains decidedly pink, even after shaking and allowing it to stand. Cool the flask with running tap-water; then filter out the crystals on a suction filter. Purify by dissolving these crystals in as little hot water as possible, cooling the solution, and filtering out the crystals as before. Let these dry in your desk. Finally weigh the dry product and compare the yield you obtain with the theoretical yield. Upon what properties of boric acid does the preparation, and the purification, depend? EXERCISE XVI COLLOIDS 

In performing this exercise the student should keep in mind that the peculiar properties exhibited by substances in the colloidal condition are to be attributed much more to the huge surface furnished by the tiny colloidal particles than to the chemical nature of such particles. Experiments are provided which will demonstrate some of these surface effects, as well as illustrate some of the simpler methods of preparing substances in the colloidal state. Although some of the properties of colloids have been of decided value in the industries, these same properties may be the cause of much trouble and profanity in the laboratory. Hence certain experiments are included dealing with the coagulation, or destruction, of colloids. 

Throughout this work, the "Tyndall effect" will be found helpful in recognizing the colloidal state. This effect is that phenomenon observed when a focussed beam of light is directed upon a colloidal solution. In a true colloid no individual particles can be seen, yet the path of the beam is visible. 

A. Preparation of Colloids 

1. Colloidal silver. Dilute 10 cc. of the ammonium hydroxide on the reagent shelf with five or more volumes of water. Add this very dilute ammonium hydroxide solution drop by drop to 5 cc. of silver nitrate solution until the precipitate first formed just disappears. Dilute this ammoniacal solution to 100 cc. with distilled water. To one 10 cc. portion of this diluted solution add a few drops of tannin solution (a mild reducing agent). To a second 10 cc. portion add a few drops of hydrochinon solution. Why is no visible precipitate obtained as the silver salt is reduced by these chemicals to metallic silver? Examine each of these colloids by transmitted and by reflected light. 

2. Colloidal sulfur. Bubble washed hydrogen sulfide into a few cubic centimeters of sulfurous acid until all of the odor of sulfur dioxide is gone. Reaction? Attempt to filter out the sulfur and account for the difficulty. Save for a later experiment. 66

COLLOIDS 

3. Colloidal sulfides. Boil about 0.5 g. of arsenic trioxide with 

100 cc. of water and filter 25 cc. of the solution. Using a clean delivery tube, pass washed hydrogen sulfide into this solution. 

Try to filter a few cubic centimeters. Examine the solution for the Tyndall effect. Compare this product with that which you obtain when hydrogen sulfide is passed into 10 cc. of arsenic trichloride solution. Represent each of the reactions by an equation and explain why a colloid was not obtained in the second instance. 

Use a small portion of the colloid solution to verify your explanation. Save the remainder. 

4. Colloidal ferric hydroxide. In a very carefully cleaned beaker, bring 300 cc. of distilled water to a boil and add, drop by drop, a 30% solution of ferric chloride until a red colloid results.

Equation? Verify the colloidal nature of the product and save for further use. This colloid is remarkably stable. 

5. Protective colloids. Fill a test tube two-thirds full of water and add four drops of lead acetate solution. Prepare a dilute solution of potassium chromate in the same manner. Place half of the lead acetate solution in a beaker and add half the chromate solution. Result? To the remaining dilute solutions add 1 cc. of gelatin solution, then mix in a second beaker. Lead chromate is formed in each case. Allow the solutions to stand for a few minutes, and then filter them on separate filters. What difference is noted? How is this explained? 

6. Emulsions. Mix 5 cc. of cottonseed oil with 100 cc. of water by shaking them vigorously in a bottle. Let this stand for a few minutes. Is the emulsion temporary or permanent? Now add 5 cc. of soap solution and again shake the bottle vigorously. Why does the emulsion now obtained differ from the one without soap? 

Why is soap a cleansing agent for grease and oil? Note the formation of a "cream" on standing-a layer richer in oil than the remaining liquid. Save this emulsion for a later experiment.

EXERCISE XVII THE HALOGENS 

The elements fluorine, chlorine, bromine, and iodine constitute the family known as the halogens. This family is prominent in chemical circles because of the great activity of its members, but remains unpopular because of several unpleasant characteristics. Yet each member has distinctive properties which differentiate it from the other members of the group, and in this exercise we study some of these differences as well as methods of obtaining the free halogens. Fluorine will be omitted because of the great difficulty in preparing it. 

1. Preparation of chlorine. (a) From hydrochloric acid. Place about a gram of manganese dioxide in a test tube, add 2-3 cc. of concentrated hydrochloric acid, and heat the tube gently. Note the color and (very cautiously) the odor of the evolved gas. What is it? How do you explain its liberation from the hydrochloric acid? Supplement your answer with a balanced equation. Can the manganese dioxide be considered a catalyst in this case? Can you expect all oxygen-containing compounds to liberate chlorine from hydrochloric acid? Verify your answer by noting the effect of adding concentrated hydrochloric acid to each of the following in separate test tubes, using only small quantities of the reagents: lead dioxide, cupric oxide, red lead oxide, nitric acid, sodium sulfate. If chlorine is evolved, a moistened piece of litmus paper, when held in the escaping gas, will be bleached. (b) From metal chlorides. To small quantities of sodium chloride, barium chloride, manganous chloride, and calcium chloride in separate test tubes, add 2-3 cc. of dilute sulfuric acid and a little manganese dioxide. Warm each tube gently and determine, either from the odor of the evolved gas or by its effect on wet litmus paper, whether or not chlorine is obtained in each case. Represent by equations all reactions which occur. Why is sodium chloride usually employed as the source of chlorine?

EXERCISE XVIII OXY-HALOGEN COMPOUNDS 

The element chlorine forms an interesting series of compounds in which it exhibits positive valences. The use of such compounds as the hypochlorites is sufficiently wide-spread so that the name, at least, is familiar. The chlorates and perchlorates, however, are less well known, although you have probably used flashlight powder or fireworks containing a chlorate. Since the only approach to these compounds is through the use of free chlorine, a brief study of their preparation is made at this time. 

1. Chlorine and a cold solution of a base. Recall from the preceding exercise the reaction of chlorine with water, and write equations to show the effect of adding sodium hydroxide to chlorine water. 

Prepare a solution of potassium hydroxide by dissolving 5 g. of the solid in 15 cc. of cold water and cooling the resulting solution.

This solution must cover the lower end of the thistle tube. Maintain a steady flow of chlorine into the potassium hydroxide for 10-15 seconds, then remove this test tube, and replace it with the second solution of potassium hydroxide in accordance with the directions of exp. 2. 

Empty the contents of the first test tube into an evaporating dish.

3. Conversion of a chlorate to a perchlorate. Carefully clean a perfect porcelain crucible of at least 100 cc. capacity, and weigh into it 61 g. of potassium chlorate, making certain that the salt is free from any particles of dirt. Two reactions occur simultaneously when potassium chlorate is heated: (a) 2KCIO3 → 2KCl + 302 (b) 4KClO3 → KCl+3KCIO4. 

Of these two reactions, the one expressed by equation (a) is unduly favored by the presence of dirt or by cracks in the glaze of the crucible. Heat the crucible gently until the salt melts, and then continue to heat sufficiently to keep up a brisk evolution of oxygen; but do not increase the heat when the melted salt shows any tendency to solidify. After 30-40 minutes, depending on the rate of heating, the contents of the crucible will begin to become pasty and a good deal of solid will separate, especially around the edges. 

At this stage cool the crucible to room temperature, place it in a beaker with 50 cc. of cold water, and allow it to stand until the fusion mass is disintegrated. (This will require some time, and the soaking process may well be allowed to continue until the next period.) Filter the residue, which is largely potassium perchlorate, and wash with cold water. Purify by crystallization from a limited amount of hot water, until the purified salt fails to show the presence of chloride when a little of it is dissolved in water and treated

EXERCISE XIX NITROGEN AND AMMONIA. THE PHOSPHORUS FAMILY 

Reference to the Periodic Table will remind you that nitrogen falls in the same group with the elements phosphorus, arsenic, antimony, and bismuth. The properties of nitrogen, however, are in rather sharp contrast to those of the members of the phosphorus family, in that it is inert and, if forced into combination, exhibits a variety of valences greater than any of the other members of the group. Since the more important compounds in which nitrogen exhibits positive valences have been studied earlier (Ex. XIV), the experiments of this exercise will be limited to the consideration of the element and its hydride, ammonia (NH3), and to a study of the change in the properties of the remaining elements of the group with increasing atomic number. 

A. Nitrogen. Ammonia 

1. Preparation of nitrogen. (a) From ammonium nitrite. Provide a small flask with a one-hole rubber stopper carrying a bent de- Am AW 

FIG. 11.-Preparation of Nitrogen. 

livery tube. Clamp the flask in place on a wire gauze on the ring stand, and arrange to collect a gas over water. Place in the flask 78

NITROGEN AND THE PHOSPHORUS FAMILY 

8 g. of ammonium chloride, 10 g. of sodium nitrite, and 50 cc. of water. Warm the solution gently and, after allowing sufficient time for the air in the flask to be displaced, collect two or three bottles of the gas. Explain why nitrogen was freely evolved when the solution of ammonium chloride and sodium nitrite was warmed, although there was no noticeable reaction when cold. (b) From air and ammonia. Two students may work together if this method is followed. Set up the apparatus shown in Fig. 11. 

By adding water very slowly through the thistle tube of the large bottle A, air is forced at a slow rate into bottle B, where it passes through a concentrated solution of ammonium hydroxide and becomes saturated with ammonia vapor. The air and ammonia are then forced into the pyrex tube C, over heated copper turnings. The copper removes the oxygen from the air, and the oxide so formed is reduced again to copper by the action of ammonia. 

Hence the operation is continuous. Its success depends upon having the copper turnings spread out thinly and upon the very slow passage of the mixed gases. Represent both reactions by equations. The gases leaving the pyrex tube then bubble through dilute sulfuric acid to remove excess ammonia vapor, and the nitrogen is collected by displacement of water. Discard the first bottle so collected, as this will consist largely of air in the chain of apparatus. A number of bottles of nitrogen may then be collected, for when bottle A becomes full of water, it may be disconnected, emptied, and the whole operation repeated until the ammonium hydroxide in B is exhausted. 

2. Properties of nitrogen. Note the physical properties of this element. From the method employed for its collection, what is obvious about its solubility in water? Determine whether the gas burns, or is able to support combustion. Ignite a strip of magnesium ribbon and quickly insert the burning metal into a bottle of nitrogen. Does the result seem to indicate that magnesium can unite with nitrogen? 

3. Approximate per cent of nitrogen in the atmosphere. Provide a bottle or Erlenmeyer flask of 250-300 cc. capacity with a tightly fitting one-hole rubber stopper. Insert through the stopper a short glass tube so that one end is flush with the bottom of the stopper. To the other end of the tube attach a short piece of rubber tubing, provided with a screw pinch cock. If the rubber tube does not fit the glass tube tightly, it must be wired in place. Fill

NITROGEN AND THE PHOSPHORUS FAMILY 

the bottle completely with water, loosen the pinch cock, and push the stopper into the neck of the bottle as far as it will readily go; close the pinch cock, remove the stopper, and measure the volume of water in the bottle by pouring it into a glass graduate. 

In this way you determine the volume of the container. 

Fill a pneumatic trough, using enough hot water to bring the temperature of the water to that of the room. Measure carefully 

15 cc. of pyrogallic acid solution and 30 cc. of a 50% solution of sodium hydroxide. Pour these solutions into the bottle and immediately stopper the bottle and close the pinch cock. Put the neck of the bottle under water in the water pan and keep it under water until the experiment is finished. Why? Shake the bottle at frequent intervals for 15-20 minutes. Alkaline pyrogallic acid greedily absorbs oxygen, leaving the remaining components of the air unchanged. After the reaction is over, turn the bottle on its side, keeping the rubber tube constantly under water, loosen the pinch cock, and let water flow into the bottle to replace oxygen absorbed. When no more water enters, remove the bottle and measure carefully the volume of liquid which it now contains. 

The volume of air taken equals the original volume of the bottle minus 45 cc. (the volume of reagents used). The volume of oxygen absorbed equals the final volume of the liquid minus 45 cc. Calculate the per cent of nitrogen in air. 

Observe that this exercise illustrates an additional method of preparing nitrogen. What impurities would be contained in a sample of nitrogen obtained in this way? 

4. Preparation and collection of ammonia from an ammonium salt. The method of preparation and collection of ammonia is, as has been the case for the gases previously studied, definitely connected with the properties of ammonia. Some of these must be known before the proper method can be determined. 

Smell cautiously of a bottle of ammonium hydroxide. The odor is that of ammonia. Note the effect of this gas upon: (1) a piece of moistened red litmus, and (2) a glass rod previously moistened with concentrated hydrochloric acid. These are tests for ammonia gas. You thus have definite evidence that ammonia is in the gas phase above a solution of ammonium hydroxide. What equilibria must therefore be present in a bottle of ammonium hydroxide? 

What qualitative statement may be made about the solubility of ammonia in water? By what method, then, must a sample of

NITROGEN AND THE PHOSPHORUS FAMILY 

ammonia be collected? Does the gas combine with water or merely dissolve in it? Give reason for your answer. What is indicated concerning the stability of ammonium hydroxide? 

To determine whether ammonia is heavier or lighter than air, calculate the weight of one liter of ammonia (NH3) at standard conditions, and compare this with the value 1.29, the approximate weight of one liter of air under the same conditions. What method of collection will you employ for ammonia?

Grind together in a mortar a small quantity of ammonium chloride and a few drops of sodium hydroxide solution. Smell cautiously for ammonia. Account for its liberation, writing equations for the reactions involved. How may the liberation of ammonia be hastened? Try this on a dry mixture of ammonium chloride and calcium hydroxide.

B. The Phosphorus Family 

6. Reaction of the elements of the phosphorus family with nitric acid. (a) Warm a little red phosphorus in a casserole or large pyrex test tube with 15 cc. of dilute nitric acid until practically all of the phosphorus dissolves. Is the reaction energetic? Dilute the solution with about an equal volume of water and filter from any insoluble residue. Make the filtrate alkaline with ammonium hydroxide (litmus) and add 5 cc. of magnesia mixture. The white

NITROGEN AND THE PHOSPHORUS FAMILY 

precipitate which results is—in the absence of arsenates—a test for the phosphate ion. Write an equation to show the reaction of phosphorus with nitric acid. From the result of this experiment, is phosphorus to be classed as a metal or a non-metal? (b) Treat a few small pieces of arsenic with dilute nitric acid in just the same way as you did phosphorus. If dilute nitric acid does not react readily, add 2-3 cc. of the concentrated acid.

After the reaction seems complete, filter, make the filtrate alkaline with ammonium hydroxide, and test with magnesia mixture. 

The white precipitate in this case is a proof of the presence of the arsenate ion. (c) Boil some pieces of antimony with 5 cc. of dilute nitric acid, and then add a little concentrated acid to complete the reaction. 

In this case the antimony is oxidized to antimonic acid, which is insoluble and therefore precipitates. Can you detect any difference in the ease with which these three elements are attacked by nitric acid? (d) Obtain a few bismuth shavings. From the physical appearance of this element, would you classify it as a metal or as a nonmetal? Warm these shavings with nitric acid until they have dissolved, and the reaction appears to be complete. To a portion of this solution add sodium hydroxide to alkalinity. The white precipitate is bismuth trihydroxide. From this fact what conclusions can you reach regarding (1) the nature of the product formed by the reaction of bismuth with nitric acid, and (2) the valence of bismuth in this compound? What do these conclusions indicate about the chemical nature of bismuth? 

7. Amphoterism. To 3 cc. of antimony chloride solution add ammonium hydroxide until precipitation seems to be complete. 

What is the precipitate? Divide the suspended material into two portions, and add hydrochloric acid to one of the portions. Express the result by an equation. To the second half of the precipitate add excess sodium hydroxide, and again express the result by an equation. What properties must antimony trihydroxide possess to enable it to react with either an acid or a base? What are such compounds called? 

8. Hydrides of arsenic and antimony. The Marsh Test. Set up a hydrogen generator, using for the generator a large bottle equipped with a two-hole stopper carrying a thistle tube and a delivery tube drawn out into a coarse jet at the end. Cover the

NITROGEN AND THE PHOSPHORUS FAMILY 

bottom of the generator bottle to the depth of 2-3 cm. with mossy zinc, and generate hydrogen by adding dilute sulfuric acid through the thistle tube. Allow the generator to operate for a few moments, then have the assistant test the gas to determine whether or not all the air has been displaced. When the test indicates that such is the case, wrap a towel around the generator bottle and light the gas issuing from the jet. Pour down the thistle tube a few drops of a solution of arsenic acid, and after a moment note the change in the appearance of the burning hydrogen. Hold a clean dry porcelain dish in the flame for a moment and note the black stain of metallic arsenic which forms. Treat the stain with sodium hypochlorite solution. What is the result? Represent by equations all reactions occurring in this method of testing for the presence of an arsenic compound.

Clean the generator thoroughly and carry out a similar test with an antimony compound. Compare the appearance of the metallic stain in this case with that of arsenic, and note the result of treating the stain with sodium hypochlorite.

EXERCISE XXI EQUILIBRIUM IN SOLUTION 

When you read a statement to the effect that sodium fluoride and cupric nitrate are soluble salts, you merely take the statement for granted and read on hoping for something more interesting; and since in all probability you will never prepare a saturated solution of either of these salts, it will not be a matter of any concern that the solubility of cupric nitrate is about 60 times that of sodium fluoride. 

But if you fail to realize that the so-called "insoluble" salts exhibit just as great variation in their "insolubility," it is a matter of concern, for many of the ion separations of analytical chemistry take advantage of such differences. In this exercise some of these applications are illustrated, and you are introduced to the use of the solubility product idea as a quantitative method of expressing solubility. 

1. Precipitation conditions. Solubility product. In order to cause the precipitation of any given salt it is obvious that the proper ions must be brought together; but unless they are mixed in sufficiently high concentrations, no precipitate will result. The following experiments will demonstrate this. In each of three test tubes place 1 cc. of lead acetate solution. Add 5 cc. of water to the second tube, and 25 cc. to the third. Now add 5 cc. of ammonium chloride solution to each of the three test tubes. You have brought together lead ions and chloride ions in each of these solutions, but a precipitate of lead chloride is not obtained in all of them. Why not? Try adding five drops of dilute hydrochloric acid (thus increasing the concentration of the chloride ion) to one of the solutions from which no precipitate was obtained. Does this make any difference?

Add a few drops of ammonium sulfate solution to the solution from which no precipitate has yet been obtained. This precipitate is lead sulfate. What, therefore, must be the relative magnitude of the solubility products of lead chloride and lead sulfate? Confirm your statement by reference to a table of solubility product values. State accurately in terms of the solubility product concept 87

EQUILIBRIUM IN SOLUTION 

what conditions must be fulfilled in order to obtain a precipitate of any given salt. 

2. Precipitation of soluble salts. The terms, soluble and insoluble, are entirely relative. Probably no salt is absolutely insoluble, and those which we term soluble may be obtained as precipitates if the proper conditions are fulfilled. To demonstrate this add concentrated hydrochloric acid, dropwise, to 5 cc. of barium chloride solution. The solubility of barium chloride at 

20° is 37 g. per 100 cc. of water. Account for the fact that you are able to obtain a precipitate of this salt by adding hydrochloric acid. Is any barium chloride left in solution after adding hydrochloric acid? Devise and carry out an experiment to prove your answer. 

3. Solution conditions. In the saturated solution of any salt, such as barium chloride, the equilibria which exist reduce to the following expression:

BaCl2 (precipitate) - Ba++ + 2C1-; and, in terms of the solubility product idea: [Ba++] × [Cl−]² = S. P. BaCl2· 

From such an expression it should be obvious that, if the concentration of either ion or of both is made smaller, as can be done by adding water, or if the value of the solubility product can be increased, as is the case if the solution is heated, the equilibrium will be upset in such a way that the solid material must go into solution in order to restore the equilibrium. However, in the case of nearly all salts which are sufficiently insoluble to be of analytical importance, changes in ionic concentration produced by either of the means indicated above are too small to be of value. For this reason insoluble salts must usually be brought into solution by means of a chemical reaction which brings about a decrease in the concentration of one of the ions. The following experiments illustrate a number of such cases. (a) By mixing suitable pairs of reagents in separate test tubes, obtain small quantities of precipitates of calcium carbonate, zinc sulfide, ferric hydroxide, and silver phosphate. Allow the precipitates to settle somewhat, decant part of the clear solution, and to the precipitate remaining in the first tube add acetic acid; to that in the second tube, dilute sulfuric acid; to the third, hydrochloric

EQUILIBRIUM IN SOLUTION 

acid; and to the last, dilute nitric acid. Formulate each reaction in terms of the essential ions involved, and explain on the basis of the solubility product concept why each salt dissolves. Note that in each of these cases, the concentration of the negative ion is reduced, and that the metal ion does not enter into the reaction at all. (b) In separate test tubes prepare small amounts of precipitates of silver chloride, silver chromate, and silver phosphate. Let each precipitate settle as before, and decant most of the clear solution. 

Now add to each precipitate a few cubic centimeters of ammonium hydroxide, and note the result. Express each of these reactions in terms of the essential substances involved and explain, on the basis of the solubility product concept, why solution occurred in each case. Observe that in these examples it is the metal ion which enters into the reaction. (c) Complex ammonia ions, similar to the one obtained in (b), are fairly common, and not infrequently of value in analytical work. Precipitate separately the hydroxides of copper, cadmium, and nickel by adding a very little sodium hydroxide solution to a soluble salt of each of these metals, and then add several cubic centimeters of ammonium hydroxide to each precipitate. Note any outstanding characteristic of the complex ammonia ion which forms in each case.

Pass hydrogen sulfide for a moment into each of these ammoniacal solutions. Note the color of each sulfide and, from the results obtained, make a statement regarding the stability of each of these complex ions. (d) Complex cyanide ions also have considerable importance in chemical work, and some of these are remarkably stable, although there are marked differences in this respect. Prepare hydroxides of copper, cadmium, and nickel as before, and to each of these precipitates add 5 cc. of potassium cyanide solution. The reactions occurring may be expressed as follows: 2Cu+++8CN-2Cu(CN); + C₂N₂ Cd+++ 4CN-Cd(CN); Ni+++ 4CN- Ni(CN) Determine the relative stability of these cyanide complexes by passing hydrogen sulfide into each of the solutions. Are any of them more stable than the ammonia complexes studied in the

EQUILIBRIUM IN SOLUTION 

preceding section? Give a reason for your answer. Such complex cyanides are often used in electroplating solutions. (Cyanides are dangerously poisonous. Take care not to spill the solutions, and always wash them down the sink as soon as you have finished.) (e) In separate test tubes obtain, respectively, 3 cc. of solutions of a soluble salt of aluminum, zinc, and lead, and to each tube add, dropwise, sodium hydroxide solution until a precipitate results? Why can precipitates of these hydroxides be obtained? 

Now continue to add sodium hydroxide, stirring the solutions well, until each precipitate dissolves. What type of hydroxides are these? The reactions may be expressed as follows: 2 Al(OH)3 + OH- AIO₂ + 2H₂O Zn(OH)2 + 20H-ZnO2 + 2H₂O Pb(OH)2 + 20H-—— PbO₂ + 2H₂O. 

In terms of the solubility product concept explain why each of these hydroxides dissolves in an excess of hydroxide ion. Contrast this type of reaction with that illustrated in section (c).

EXERCISE XXII NITRATES. NITRITES 

Sodium nitrate is the only nitrate which occurs in nature in useful amount, and either this salt or nitric acid itself must serve as the source of all other nitrates. Since the nitrates are all soluble, their preparation involves recovery from solution by crystallization. An important reaction of nitrates is their decomposition when heateda reaction which leads to the use of nitrates as oxidizing agents. 

This reaction, as well as the preparation of potassium nitrate, is studied in this exercise. 

1. Preparation of potassium nitrate. The method employed for the preparation of potassium nitrate, a soluble salt, depends on the precipitation of this salt under properly controlled conditions. 

If sodium nitrate and potassium chloride are dissolved in water together, the solution will contain sodium, potassium, chloride, and nitrate ions; and, of the four salts which could result from combination of these ions, that one whose solubility product is first exceeded under any given set of conditions will crystallize first. The accompanying table indicates the number of moles of the various salts required to saturate 1000 g. of water at the temperatures indicated.

2. Effect of heating nitrates. Place about 5 g. of ammonium nitrate in a test tube provided with a one-hole rubber stopper carrying a delivery tube, and arrange to collect a gas over water.

This gas is nitrous oxide, N2O. Write an electronic equation for the thermal decomposition of ammonium nitrate. Collect two bottles of the gas and determine its color, odor, and ability to burn or support combustion.

The effect of heat on the nitrate of sodium or potassium may be demonstrated by heating a small quantity of one of these nitrates in an ignition tube. Test the evolved gas with a glowing splint. 

What is one product of the thermal decomposition of these two nitrates? When no further evolution of gas occurs, cool the residue and treat with dilute sulfuric acid. From the result, what is the residue?

The remaining nitrates are similar to one another in their behavior when heated. In separate test tubes heat about a gram of barium nitrate and of lead nitrate. What is the brown gas which is evolved? Hold the test tube in a horizontal position and, with the aid of a glowing splint, prove that the brown gas is not the only gaseous product. What residue is left in the tube?

3. Oxidizing action of sodium nitrate. In the preceding experiment the thermal decomposition of sodium nitrate was observed. What valence change is taking place in this transformation? Advantage may be taken of the decomposition of sodium nitrate by using it, under appropriate conditions, as an oxidizing agent. (a) Oxidation without a medium. In a dry porcelain crucible place 5 g. of sodium nitrate and about one-third as much of copper filings. Heat the crucible strongly until evolution of gas ceases. Pour the molten mass out of the crucible upon a clean spot on a porcelain plate (or the stone table top). Note that the solidified mass contains two products-a white substance and one which appears black. Treat the white solid with hydrochloric acid, and observe the color of the escaping gas. Account for the evolution of this gas. What has happened to the sodium nitrate? 

Add dilute hydrochloric acid to the black portion of the melt. 

To what is the color of the resulting solution due? Examine the residue at the bottom of the solution. What does it appear to be? 

Clean the crucible by soaking it in acid.

When copper reacts with sodium nitrate, what is the reduction product? the oxidation product? Write an electronic equation for the reaction. Has all of the copper been oxidized? Suggest a means of making the oxidation more efficient. The reduction of sodium nitrate by a metal constitutes the commercial method of preparing sodium nitrite. Lead is usually employed to bring about the reduction in this case, not only because of its relative cheapness, but also because the product, lead monoxide, is of value. (b) Oxidation in basic medium. In the preceding experiment no medium is employed. Most oxidation-reduction reactions will, however, proceed more smoothly if there is present some substance which can react with (and thereby remove) either the oxidation product or the reduction product. Obviously, to secure reaction, the medium must be of a chemical nature opposite to that of the substance with which it is to react. Thus, if a nonmetal is to be oxidized, a basic medium should be provided.

EXERCISE XXIII HALIDES AND SULFIDES 

Aside from the fact that they are salts of binary volatile acids, the halides bear little resemblance to the sulfides. The different halides, however, have many similar reactions, as might be expected when the similarity of the halogens themselves is recalled. Many of the reactions of the halides have been studied in connection with their acids (Ex. XIII). Consequently, this exercise will be devoted chiefly to a study of the halides and sulfides with the object of bringing out those differences in their reactions which may be applied in the detection of the respective ions.

A. Halides 1. Insoluble halides. To 1 cc. portions of solutions of silver nitrate, lead acetate, and mercurous nitrate, add a few drops of hydrochloric acid. Observe any differences which are evident in the physical appearance of the three precipitates, especially after they have been left standing for a few minutes. These insoluble chlorides, due to the metal ion, have certain differences which serve as a means of identifying these metal ions; such differences are studied in a later exercise (Ex. XXVII). 

In a similar way try to obtain precipitates of the bromides and iodides of silver, lead, and mercurous mercury, using solutions of potassium bromide and potassium iodide as the source of the halide ions, and record all your observations. 

2. Tests for the halide ions. (a) Solubility of the silver salt. 

In separate test tubes dilute a few drops of solutions of a chloride, bromide, and iodide with 8-10 cc. of water, and again precipitate the silver salts of these three halides. Decant about one-half of each suspension into clean test tubes. Determine the solubility of one set of precipitates in 10 cc. of dilute nitric acid, and of the second set in 10 cc. of ammonium hydroxide. Be sure to stir the precipitates well before coming to the conclusion that they are insoluble. Record the results in the accompanying table. 96

Why is the oxidation of the bromide ion more difficult? (d) Test for the chloride ion. The reactions which you carried out in (a) afford a means of testing for a chloride provided bromides are absent. (Why does the bromide ion interfere?) In the presence of bromides the following reaction may be employed to test for chlorides.

Mix 0.2 g. of dry sodium chloride with one gram of dry potassium dichromate by grinding them together in a mortar. Place all of this mixture in a dry pyrex test tube of appropriate bore, and cover with 5-6 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid. Moisten the inside of a Gooch (mushroom) trap with a little water and insert it into the top of the tube as illustrated in Fig. 12. 

Cautiously heat the contents of the tube until the brownish vapors cease to be evolved. Sufficient heat must be applied, however, to drive these fumes into the trap. Chromyl chloride, CrO2Cl2, distills out of the mixture, and the reactions for its formation may be considered to occur as follows:

FIG. 12.- 

Gooch Trap.

K2Cr2O7 + 2H2SO4 → 2CrO3 + 2KHSO4 + H2O NaCl + H2SO4 → HCl + NaHSO4 CrO3 + 2HCl → CrO2Cl2 + H2O.

The chromyl chloride is decomposed by the water in the trap (an example of hydrolysis):

CrO2Cl2+2HOH → H2CrO4 + 2HCl. 

Remove the trap from the test tube. Rinse the inner walls of the trap with not more than 5 cc. of water, catching the liquid in a clean test tube. The color (what is it?) of the solution is characteristic of the chromate ion. To the solution in the tube add 

3 cc. of ammonium acetate solution and a few drops of lead acetate solution. A yellow precipitate indicates the presence of chromate ion. This, however, could have appeared only as the result

of the intermediate formation of chromyl chloride. Hence the appearance of a yellow precipitate also serves as a test for the chloride ion in the original substance. 

Neither bromides nor iodides form a chromyl compound. An iodide, however, would be oxidized under the above conditions to free iodine, which could distill into the trap and later form a yellow precipitate of lead iodide. For this reason iodides must be removed before the test for chloride is carried out. 

3. Analysis of an unknown solution. After carefully studying the reactions which you have just carried out with the three halide ions, obtain an "unknown" solution from the assistant, and determine which halide ions it contains. 

B. Sulfides 

4. Reaction of sulfides with acids. (a) Test for sulfides. In separate test tubes place, respectively, 1 cc. of sodium sulfide solution, ammonium sulfide solution, and a very small quantity of solid ferrous sulfide. Add 5 cc. of dilute sulfuric acid to each tube and smell cautiously of the gas evolved. What is it? Hold over the mouth of each test tube a piece of filter paper moistened with lead acetate solution. What causes the dark color which develops on the filter paper?

Dilute 1 cc. of a solution of a soluble sulfide with 10 cc. of water, and precipitate silver sulfide from this solution. Note the color of this salt and determine its solubility in dilute nitric acid. Compare silver sulfide with the silver halides with respect to these two properties. (b) Removal of sulfides. Mix 5 cc. of a solution of a soluble sulfide with an equal volume of an iodide solution in a porcelain casserole, and add 10 cc. of acetic acid. Evaporate this solution to dryness in the hood, and dissolve the residue in 10 cc. of water. 

Using one-half of the solution for each test, try to obtain a test for a sulfide and for an iodide. Give two reasons to explain why this reaction removed the sulfide, but not the iodide. During any analysis it would be necessary to remove the sulfide by this means before testing for the halide ions. Why? 

5. Polysulfides. To 15 cc. of ammonium sulfide in a test tube add about a gram of powdered sulfur, stopper the tube loosely, and warm the mixture by dipping the test tube in a beaker of hot water until most of the sulfur dissolves. Note the orange or reddish

HALIDES AND SULFIDES 

color of the resulting solution. Ammonium polysulfide is now present in this solution. Save for use in the next experiment. 

6. Formation of a thio-salt. Obtain a small quantity of antimony sulfide by passing hydrogen sulfide from a generator into 10 cc. of antimony trichloride solution until precipitation seems to be complete. Filter the precipitate, scrape most of it from the filter paper, and add the precipitate to the polysulfide solution prepared in the preceding section. Warm the mixture in a water bath, if necessary, until the precipitate of antimony sulfide dissolves. Represent the reaction by an equation. 

1. Applying the solubility product idea, explain why a precipitate of AgCl dissolves in NH4OH, while AgI does not. 

2. Which halide ion is most easily oxidized to the free element? In which case is oxidation the most difficult? What experiments in this exercise support your conclusions? 

3. Represent by equations the reactions of NaCl, NaBr, and NaI, separately, with concentrated H2SO4. 

4. Write an equation to show what would happen if H2S were present when an iodide is treated with HOCI. 

5. Contrast the halogen acids with H2S as regards (a) strength and (b) solubility in water. What experiment in this exercise depends on these differences?

EXERCISE XXIV SULFATES. PHOSPHATES. CARBONATES. BORATES 

A number of the properties of the sulfate ion, and other negative ions of the salts studied in this section, have been encountered previously in the exercises dealing with corresponding acids. Consequently, in this exercise attention will be directed primarily to the properties of various specific salts, and to the analytical reactions by which the various anions are commonly identified. 

A. Sulfates 

1. Preparation of an acid sulfate. Weigh out 4 g. of potassium sulfate, pulverize the salt, and place it in a pyrex test tube. Add exactly 2 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid, warm the mixture gently-without boiling the acid-until the solid dissolves, and set the tube aside. Note the crystals which separate as the solution cools. What compound is this? Suggest a method by which it may be converted to the normal sulfate. 

2. Preparation and properties of potassium pyrosulfate. Transfer the acid sulfate prepared in the preceding experiment to a porcelain crucible, and heat this for some time in the hood. Why does the acid sulfate effervesce when heated? What remains when the effervescence stops? While the contents of the crucible are still molten, add a pinch of cupric oxide, and account for the color produced. Reheat, if necessary, to aid in the solution of the cupric oxide in the melt. Pour the molten contents of the crucible on a clean spot on a porcelain plate or stone table top and, when the mass has cooled, grind it in the mortar with 10 cc. of water. Filter from any undissolved cupric oxide, and observe the color of the solution. To what is this color due? Write equations for the thermal decomposition of potassium acid sulfate and the reaction of the product with cupric oxide. 

3. Plaster of Paris. Make a stiff paste of plaster of Paris [(CaSO4)2 H2O] and water. Place a coin on a piece of paper, cover the exposed face with a thin film of vaseline, then with the plaster paste. Allow this to remain undisturbed until the mass is hard. 101

SULFATES 

What reaction takes place upon setting? Break open the mass and examine the part next the coin. Suggest a use for plaster of Paris. 

4. Double sulfates. (a) A double sulfate will often crystallize on cooling a solution in which two sulfates have been dissolved in the proper molecular proportions. The class of double sulfates known as alums may be prepared in this way. Weigh out 9 g. of potassium sulfate, and also an equimolecular quantity of hydrated aluminum sulfate, Al2(SO4)3-18H2O. In calculating the weight of the latter do not overlook the water of crystallization. Pulverize both calts, dissolve each separately in the least possible amount of boiling water, pour the two solutions together and set the mixture aside to cool. Note the character of the crystals which make their appearance. If there is difficulty in getting crystallization started, scatter in the solution two or three tiny crystals of alum from the stock bottle to serve as nuclei. Filter off the crystalline product and dry the crystals by rolling them between filter paper. (b) Pulverize 10 g. of cupric sulfate and dissolve it in 20 cc. of hot water. Add to the hot solution one molecular equivalent of ammonium sulfate, stir until this dissolves, then set the solution aside to cool. Crystals of the composition (NH4)2SO4 CuSO4·6H₂O separate. Compare these crystals with those of (1) alum and (2) hydrated copper sulfate, and record the differences. Is this double salt an alum? Give reasons for your answer. 

5. Tests for sulfate and sulfite. (a) Sulfate. In separate test tubes obtain a few drops of a solution of a soluble phosphate, a soluble sulfate, a soluble sulfite, and a soluble carbonate. Dilute each with 10 cc. of water and add barium chloride solution to each of these solutions. Is a precipitate obtained in each case? 

Could you readily distinguish between the different precipitates?

Now add to each of the test tubes 5-10 cc. of dilute hydrochloric acid, taking care to stir the solutions thoroughly. Which precipitate is insoluble in hydrochloric acid? (Note: Barium sulfite is actually soluble in acids; any precipitate which may remain in the test tube containing sulfite is due to contamination by sulfate, arising from oxidation of the sulfite.) Now state the two steps required for the identification of a sulfate. (b) Sulfite. In separate test tubes place small amounts of solutions of the same salts used in (a), and dilute each solution with 10 cc. of water. Acidify each of these solutions with dilute sulfuric acid, and add to each a few drops of potassium permanganate

PHOSPHATES 

solution. In which case is the color of the potassium permanganate solution destroyed? Now test this solution for the presence of a sulfate. Account for the results.

Would the tests outlined here permit you to detect the sulfite and sulfate if both salts were present in the same solution? Test the correctness of your conclusion by testing an "unknown" provided by your neighbor.

B. Phosphates 

6. Thermal decomposition of phosphates. Heat gently in a porcelain crucible about a gram of disodium hydrogen phosphate. 

Note the effervescence and give a reason for it. When effervescence ceases, heat the crucible strongly for five minutes. What is the residue in the crucible? Give the equation for its formation.

Allow the crucible to cool, dissolve the residue in 10 cc. of cold water, and add to the solution a few drops of silver nitrate solution. 

Note and record the color of the precipitate. 

In a similar manner heat microcosmic salt (NaNH₁HPO₁). Hold a moistened strip of red litmus paper in the evolved gases. What is one product of the thermal decomposition of microcosmic salt? 

What substance finally remains in the crucible? Write an equation for its formation from microcosmic salt. Dissolve the residue in cold water and add to the solution a few drops of silver nitrate solution. Compare the color of this precipitate with that obtained in the preceding case.

Add a few drops of silver nitrate solution to a solution of microcosmic salt (an orthophosphate). Result? State how you might distinguish an orthophosphate from a pyrophosphate; from a metaphosphate? 

7. Phosphate bead tests. Make a small loop 2-3 mm. in diameter at one end of a clean platinum wire by wrapping the wire around the end of a pencil near the point. Heat the loop and, while hot, dip it into a little microcosmic salt on a watch glass and again heat until a clear bead results. What is the composition of this bead? Touch the clear bead while hot to a small fragment of a copper compound, and heat in the oxidizing part of the flame until the bead is again transparent. Allow it to cool and note the color. 

If the color is not sufficiently intense, repeat the heating with more of the copper compound. Write equations to account for the changes noted.

CARBONATES 

Repeat the above operations with compounds of cobalt and manganese, and note the color of the bead in each case. The formation of phosphate beads of characteristic color furnishes a method of detecting the presence of certain ions. 

8. Test for an orthophosphate. In separate test tubes place a few drops of a solution of a soluble phosphate and a soluble arsenate. Dilute each solution with 10 cc. of water, add 2 cc. of magnesia mixture, and then add ammonium hydroxide until the solutions are quite strongly alkaline. The white, sometimes slowforming, precipitates are, respectively, magnesium ammonium phosphate (MgNH,PO₁) and magnesium ammonium arsenate (MgNH4ASO4). Can you tell them apart?

Prepare a mixture containing a few drops each of a solution of a phosphate and of an arsenate in a large pyrex test tube, and dilute the mixture to 10 cc. with water. Add 5 cc. of concentrated hydrochloric acid, heat to boiling, and bubble a slow stream of hydrogen sulfide into the hot solution until no more precipitate forms. What is this precipitate? Filter the solution and boil down the filtrate to 5 cc. to remove hydrogen sulfide and some of the hydrochloric acid; then carry out the phosphate test in this solution. Result?

Note the result, and then continue to bubble the gas through the limewater until the precipitate first formed is dissolved. What compound is now present in the solution? Write equations representing the reactions which have occurred. Save the clear solution for the next two experiments. 

10. Effect of heat on bicarbonates. Heat a small quantity of the solution of the bicarbonate prepared in exp. 9. Account for the reappearance of the precipitate. 

Place a small amount of sodium bicarbonate in a test tube equipped with a one-hole rubber stopper, with the delivery tube extending into a test tube containing a few cubic centimeters of limewater. Heat the bicarbonate with a low flame. What gas is evolved? Proof? Continue heating until no more gas is driven off.

BORATES 

11. Hard water. To a few cubic centimeters of the bicarbonate solution reserved from exp. 9 add soap solution, a few drops at a time, shaking the tube after each addition, until suds are formed. 

Observe how much soap solution has to be added in order to obtain suds, and note the precipitate which forms during the addition of the soap. What objections are there to the use of water containing dissolved calcium salts? 

12. Test for a carbonate. Place 5 cc. of the test solution of carbonate ion in a pyrex test tube, of such bore that a Gooch trap fits in the end (Fig. 12). Moisten the inside of the trap with limewater. Make the carbonate solution just acid with sulfuric acid, and immediately insert the trap. Heat the solution carefully, so as not to drive any of it into the trap. Do not heat for more than thirty seconds after the solution begins to boil. Observe the condition of the limewater in the trap and account for the result. 

What would be the result if an excess of carbon dioxide is passed into limewater?

D. Borates 

13. Borax bead tests. Make a loop on the end of a clean platinum wire, and heat until it glows. Bring the hot loop in contact with some powdered borax and reheat until the borax adhering to the loop forms a clear glassy bead. Then dip the bead into a solution of a cobalt salt, and again heat it strongly in the oxidizing flame. 

Cool the bead and note its color. If the color is not sufficiently pronounced, dip the bead again into the solution, and reheat. 

Make another borax bead and touch this, while still hot, to a small amount of cupric oxide. Reheat as before, and observe the color of the cool bead. These beads of characteristic color may serve to identify particular ions. Upon what property does this particular reaction of borax depend? Of what practical value is the reaction? 

14. Test for a borate. Place 5 cc. of a solution of borax in a large pyrex test tube, and make definitely acid with dilute hydrochloric acid. Dip a piece of turmeric paper into this solution, wet it thoroughly, and dry it by placing it on a watch glass above a beaker of boiling water. (A reddish-brown color usually develops.) 

Moisten the dry paper with a few drops of sodium hydroxide. If the paper develops a dark olive-green color (shade and intensity will vary somewhat) a borate is indicated.

BORATES 

15. Analysis of unknowns. Obtain from the assistant solutions of unknown content, and determine which one of the ions studied in this exercise is present. It will also be interesting to examine certain commercial substances for the presence of these ions. 

Among materials suitable for testing here are: water softeners; bath salts; solid paint removers, such as "Double-X" and "Oakite"; and concentrated fertilizers. A small quantity of the latter should be extracted with hot water, filtered from inert material, and the test carried out on the filtrate. 

1. Using examples differing from those studied in this exercise, write several equations showing: (a) the conversion of normal salts to acid salts; (b) the effect of heating acid salts; (c) the conversion of acid salts to normal salts. 

2. Give evidence to indicate that double salts are true compounds, and not merely mixtures of the original components. 

3. Explain why soluble phosphates are frequently used as water softeners. 

What other compounds are also used for the same purpose? 

4. Show by equations how a precipitate of As2S5 could be treated in order to obtain from it an arsenate. 

5. Purchase a bottle of bath salts, noting the weight of the contents and the price you pay. Determine the chief constituent of this commodity, and look up its price in a trade journal.

EXERCISE XXV SEPARATION OF METAL IONS INTO ANALYTICAL GROUPS 

This exercise and the two immediately following it are designed to demonstrate to the student the application of certain principles studied in the classroom. In this exercise the emphasis is upon the procedure which may be employed for the separation of the common metal ions into groups. In the two exercises following this, the emphasis is directed upon the outstanding principles behind such a separation, and the extension of these principles in bringing about the separation of ions within certain of the groups. 

Two students may work together in this exercise. For satisfactory results, it is recommended that the test solutions of metal ions in the concentrations specified for Part II (Qualitative 

Analysis) be used.

In separate test tubes pour one or two cubic centimeters of test solutions of the following ions: aluminum, ammonium, antimony, arsenic, barium, bismuth, cadmium, calcium, chromium, cobaltous, cupric, ferrous, lead, magnesium, manganous, mercuric, mercurous, nickelous, potassium, silver, sodium, stannous, strontium, and zinc. To avoid confusion label each tube with the name of the ion it contains. 

1. The Hydrochloric Acid Group. Add to each tube a few drops of dilute hydrochloric acid. Note which ions yield a precipitate under these conditions. Describe the color of each precipitate and its appearance, such as curdy or crystalline. This set of metal ions constitutes the Hydrochloric Acid Group, and the group reagent is dilute hydrochloric acid. This group is distinguished by the insolubility of its chlorides in water. Discard the solutions in which precipitation occurred. 

2. The Hydrogen Sulfide Group. Add 5 cc. of hydrogen sulfide water to each of the remaining solutions (which of course still contain hydrochloric acid). Note which of the ions produce precipitates under these conditions, and record their colors and any other 107

ANALYTICAL SEPARATIONS 

characteristic appearance. The group reagent is hydrogen sulfide in acid solution. 

3. The Hydroxide Group. Boil off all hydrogen sulfide from the solutions which did not yet give precipitates. The complete removal of hydrogen sulfide is indicated when a piece of filter paper moistened with lead acetate solution is not blackened when held in the escaping vapors. After the complete removal of hydrogen sulfide, add a little dilute nitric acid to the solution containing the ferrous ion and boil to oxidize this ion to the ferric condition. 

What would have been the result if hydrogen sulfide were still present when nitric acid was added? Equation? Also write the equation for the oxidation of the ferrous ion to the ferric condition. Now add to each solution about 5 cc. of ammonium chloride solution, and then ammonium hydroxide until the solution is strongly alkaline. Boil for a moment. Note which ions yield precipitates under these conditions. These comprise the Hydroxide Group. Discard them. 

Note: Because of atmospheric oxidation, a precipitate may appear in the tube containing the manganous ion. Manganese, however, does not belong to this group. Take a fresh portion of a test solution of manganous ion for the next section. 

4. The Ammonium Sulfide Group. To the remaining solutions add a few drops of ammonium sulfide solution and note the ions now yielding precipitates, and the color and appearance of each. 

These ions constitute the Ammonium Sulfide Group. Discard these solutions. 

5. The Ammonium Carbonate Group. Add a little more ammonium chloride to the remaining solutions, and then a few drops of ammonium carbonate solution. Boil for just a moment. Note which ions appear as precipitates. This is the Ammonium Carbonate Group, and the group reagent is ammonium carbonate solution in the presence of ammonium chloride. 

6. The Alkali Group. Note which of the metal ions have failed to yield precipitates with the various group reagents previously used. Note that these are magnesium and the metals of the Alkali 

Group. There is no group reagent for this set of ions. 

1. Give reasons, different in each case, why H2SO4, HNO3, or HC₂H₂O₂ are not desirable for acidifying the solution previous to the precipitation of the 

Hydrogen Sulfide Group.

ANALYTICAL SEPARATIONS 

2. On the basis of the solubility product concept, explain why the members of the Ammonium Sulfide Group do not precipitate with the Hydrogen Sulfide Group.
