EXERCISE XXVI METALS I 

In the preceding exercise the cations of the common metals were divided into a number of analytical groups by the systematic application of a series of group reagents. The present exercise is designed to demonstrate a few of the principles upon which the separation of ions may be based, and to emphasize the importance of controlling conditions when carrying out such separations. 

1. Lead ion (Pb++). To 5 cc. of a strong solution of a lead salt, add ammonium chloride solution until no further precipitation occurs. What is the precipitate? Equation? Has all of the lead ion been removed from the solution? Explain in terms of the solubility product concept. Decant some of the clear liquid from the precipitate and to this add hydrogen sulfide water. Result? 

How does the solubility product value for the insoluble lead salt formed in this case compare with that for lead chloride? In what two groups may the lead cation make its appearance in the course of an analytical separation? 

2. Zinc ion (Zn++). To 5 cc. of a test solution of zinc ion, add 15 cc. of hydrogen sulfide water. What is the precipitate?

Now acidify 5 cc. of the test solution of zinc ion with an equal volume of dilute hydrochloric acid and endeavor to precipitate zinc sulfide from this acid solution by the addition of hydrogen sulfide water. Why is the presence of hydrochloric acid necessary in precipitating the Hydrogen Sulfide Group? Employ the solubility product concept in explaining the effect of the acid. What other cations behave similarly to zinc? 

3. The cations: zinc, magnesium, and aluminum. A method of obtaining each of these metals in the form of a separate precipitate from a mixture of the three cations can be devised from a study of the property of each cation separately. (a) To 2 cc. of a test solution of magnesium ion, add a little ammonium hydroxide. What is the precipitate? Account for its formation in terms of its solubility product. To a second portion 110

METALS I 

of this test solution add, first, an equal volume of ammonium chloride solution, and then ammonium hydroxide. Explain the result in terms of the common ion effect and the solubility product concept. Try the effect of adding sodium hydroxide to a solution of a magnesium salt which contains ammonium chloride. 

Account for the result. (b) Add ammonium hydroxide to one portion of a test solution of aluminum ion. To a second portion add an excess of sodium hydroxide, carrying out the addition a few drops at a time. What is the precipitate which forms in each case? Explain its disappearance upon the addition of excess sodium hydroxide. (c) To 5 cc. of a test solution of zinc ion add ammonium hydroxide, a few drops at a time, until the solution is definitely ammoniacal. Account for the disappearance of any precipitate produced by the first drops of the base. Add sodium hydroxide to a second portion of the test solution, and explain the disappearance of the precipitate in an excess of this reagent. (d) Prepare a mixture containing the cations, aluminum, magnesium, and zinc, by mixing 5 cc. portions of test solutions of these ions. Devise and carry out a scheme by which each of these cations is obtained in the form of a separate precipitate. Receive the assistant's approval of the method, then apply it to the detection of the cations present in an "unknown" solution obtained from him. (e) Explain why ammonium chloride is used with ammonium hydroxide to precipitate the Hydroxide Group. Why is ammonium hydroxide used instead of sodium hydroxide to separate aluminum from magnesium and zinc? 

4. The cations: chromium and aluminum. To a 5 cc. portion of a test solution of chromium ion add sodium hydroxide dropwise until no further reaction is evident. What is the first precipitate? 

Why does it dissolve upon the addition of an excess of sodium hydroxide? Now heat this alkaline solution to boiling. Account for the resulting precipitate, which is chromium hydroxide. 

Treat 5 cc. of a test solution of aluminum ion in the same manner as above, first adding sodium hydroxide solution until the initial precipitate is dissolved, then heating this alkaline solution until it begins to boil. In what respect does this alkaline solution differ from that of the chromium salt? In what respect are the hydroxides of chromium and aluminum similar?

METALS I 

Devise a scheme by which you can obtain the cations, chromium and aluminum, as separate precipitates from a mixture containing these two ions, and try it upon such a mixture. 

5. The cations: iron and aluminum. To test solutions of ferric ion and aluminum ion, in separate test tubes, add sodium hydroxide solution, a few drops at a time, until an excess of the base is present. 

Explain why the aluminum compound which first precipitates dissolves upon further addition of sodium hydroxide. To this solution add hydrochloric acid, drop by drop, until the solution becomes acid; then make it slightly alkaline with ammonium hydroxide and heat to boiling. What aluminum compound have you now?

Filter off the precipitate obtained upon the addition of sodium hydroxide to a test solution of ferric ion, dissolve this in a little hydrochloric acid, and divide the solution into two portions. To one portion add potassium thiocyanate solution; to the second, a solution of potassium ferrocyanide. The results obtained in each case serve as tests for the ferric ion. Write equations for all the reactions performed.

Devise a scheme for the separation and identification of the ferric ion in a mixture containing both ferric and aluminum ions, and try your scheme on such a mixture. 

6. The cations: calcium and magnesium. Using 5 cc. portions of test solutions of calcium and magnesium ion respectively, in separate test tubes, try the effect of adding a few cubic centimeters of sodium carbonate solution. Is a precipitate obtained in each case?

To fresh portions of the test solutions of these ions add an equal volume of ammonium carbonate solution. Explain with the aid of the solubility product concept and the principles of hydrolysis why there is a precipitate in one case and not in the other. 

Which of the two carbonates, calcium carbonate or magnesium carbonate, has the lower solubility product? 

7. The cations: magnesium and the metals of the Alkali Group. 

There is no group reagent for these cations, therefore a separate test is made for each ion. 

To 5 cc. of a test solution of magnesium ion add an equal volume of ammonium chloride reagent, and a few drops of a solution of microcosmic salt. Make the mixture strongly alkaline with ammonium hydroxide. The precipitate which forms is magnesium

METALS I 

ammonium phosphate (MgNHPO4). This reaction serves as a test for the magnesium ion. What is the purpose of the ammonium chloride?

The sodium ion may be identified as follows. Bend the end of a piece of platinum wire into a small loop. Hold this in the outer edge of the Bunsen flame until it ceases to impart any color to the flame. Then dip the loop into a test solution of sodium ion, again hold the loop in the outer edge of the flame, and note the color imparted to the flame.

Although the potassium ion also produces a characteristic flame color, the color is so easily obscured by the presence even of small traces of sodium ion that it is of little value for test purposes, and potassium ion is better identified in the following manner. To 2 cc. of the test solution of potassium ion, add a few drops of a solution of sodium cabaltinitrite [NasCo (NO2)6]. The yellow precipitate K₂NaCo (NO2)-indicates the presence of potassium ion.

EXERCISE XXVII METALS II 

The metal ions constituting each analytical group fall into that group because they possess certain similar properties. In effecting separations of the ions within a given group we must, however, look for characteristic differences in their reactions. It is the purpose of this exercise to show, in several of the analytical groups, how such differences may be applied in accomplishing the separation and identification of each ion from the others of its group. 

1. The Hydrochloric Acid Group. Prepare separate precipitates of lead, silver, and mercurous chlorides by adding hydrochloric acid to 5 cc. of test solutions of these ions. Allow each precipitate to settle somewhat, then decant most of the supernatant liquid. (a) Effect of hot water. To the residue in each tube add 15 cc. of boiling water. Which precipitate dissolves? Explain in terms of solubility product. Divide the solution of the one which dissolves in the hot water into two portions. To one portion add dilute sulfuric acid; to the second, potassium chromate solution. Result? 

For what ion is either of these results a test? Describe a method by which you can separate and identify this ion in a mixture of the three insoluble chlorides. (b) Effect of ammonium hydroxide. Add ammonium hydroxide to the two precipitates which did not dissolve after treatment with hot water. Which one now disappears? Why? What happens to the other? To the ammoniacal solution of the one which has disappeared, add nitric acid. What is the precipitate? Show its formation by means of an equation, and explain the part played by the nitric acid. 

(c) Separation of the ions of the Hydrochloric Acid Group. Pre pare the following mixtures by taking small portions of the appropriate test solutions, mixing these, and then adding hydrochloric acid to the mixture: (1) silver chloride and lead chloride; (2) silver chloride and mercurous chloride; (3) lead chloride and mercurous chloride. Filter each, and discard the resulting filtrate. 114

Then prepare a mixture of these three ions and carry out the separation and identification of each ion according to your outline. Finally obtain from your neighbor an “unknown" solution, containing one or more of these ions. How do the sulfates of these ions compare in solubility with the sulfate

of lead? State how you might accomplish a separation of lead ion from any of the other three ions discussed. (d) Test for cupric ion. Note the color of solutions of copper salts.

EXERCISE XXVIII COMPOUNDS OF CHROMIUM AND MANGANESE 

As elements, chromium and manganese play an important part in the production of alloy steels; and several of their compounds are of importance in the laboratory as oxidizing agents. In this exercise we shall study the changes in valence which these elements undergo, and the conditions which are essential in bringing about these changes. 

A. Compounds of Chromium 

1. Nature of chromic hydroxide. Observe the color of a solution of chromic chloride. From 5 cc. of this solution precipitate chromic hydroxide by the addition of ammonium hydroxide. Divide the suspension of this precipitate into two portions and test its solubility in hydrochloric acid and in sodium hydroxide. Express each reaction by an equation. What do you conclude to be the nature of this hydroxide? 

2. Oxidation of Cr³+ to Cr6+. This oxidation may be carried out either in solution or by fusion, but in either case a basic medium must be employed. Why? To 3 cc. of chromic chloride solution add an equal volume of sodium hydroxide, then 10 cc. of sodium hypochlorite solution, and boil for a few minutes. Filter off any solid remaining and save the filtrate. Note and account for the color change of the solution. To a few drops of this solution add a little barium chloride solution. What is the precipitate? 

For what ion is it a test? What is the oxidation product of the reaction of Cr3+ with sodium hypochlorite? Derive an equation for the reaction by the electronic method. 

3. Chromate dichromate relation. Note the colors of the stock solutions of potassium chromate and potassium dichromate. To what ions are these colors due? Acidify a few cubic centimeters of potassium chromate solution with dilute sulfuric acid. Make a portion of potassium dichromate solution alkaline with ammonium hydroxide. Observe all changes and represent them by equations. 

Note that these changes are not due to a change in the valence number of chromium. 118

Since the basic character of the amphoteric chromic hydroxide is the more prominent, the reduction of Cro+ to Cr3+ is usually carried out in the presence of an acid medium. 2 cc. of carbon disulfide and a drop or two of potassium iodide solution. Shake the tube. Explain the color of the carbon disulfide bead. Write an electronic equation for the reaction of potassium dichromate with potassium iodide in acid solution.

Note that the reaction proceeds of its own accord after it is started.

Derive an electronic equation for the reaction.

EXERCISE XXIX ELECTRICAL ENERGY FROM CHEMICAL REACTIONS 

A study of the problem of obtaining electrical energy from chemical reactions may be condensed into a consideration of three issues: (1) the characteristics of reactions suitable for use as sources of electrical energy; (2) the arrangement of apparatus required to get electrical energy from such reactions; and (3) the factors which determine the intensity of the electrical energy, or voltage. topics are developed in the following experiments. 

It is suggested that in this exercise students work in pairs. These 

1. Electrical energy from reactions. Secure a small porous cup. Place in this a solution of chlorine water, which has been made conducting by the addition of a few cubic centimeters of sodium chloride solution. Set the cup in a beaker containing a solution of potassium iodide. Now insert a carbon rod into each solution, and connect each carbon rod to a terminal of a voltmeter. Is there any indication of a flow of electricity? Replace the potassium iodide solution with a solution of ferrous ammonium sulfate, and repeat the experiment. Then discard the chlorine water, rinse out the cup, and half-fill it with dilute nitric acid. 

Set the cup in the beaker containing the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution, and determine whether electrical energy is obtained from this reaction. What characteristic is common to the three reactions just used? In what form is the energy of these three reactions usually manifested? What must be done in order to obtain electrical energy from such a chemical reaction? 

2. Potential differences between metals. Secure clean strips of zinc, lead, and copper. Measure the potential difference between pairs of these metals in the following manner. Insert the pair, the potential difference of which is to be measured, in a solution of sodium sulfate; make sure that they are not in contact with each other, and then connect each electrode to a terminal of the voltmeter. Three such pairs are to be measured. By comparison of the meter readings, arrange the metals in the order of decreasing 122

potentials.

Compare this order with the order of these metals in the Electromotive Series. 

3. Electric couples. (a) Prepare a dilute solution of sulfuric acid by cautiously adding 10 cc. of the concentrated acid to 200 cc. of water. In half of this solution place a strip of zinc and one of copper, but do not let them touch each other. At what metal is hydrogen evolved? Connect the two strips with a wire and account for what you now observe. (b) With the remainder of the dilute sulfuric acid set up a second cell, using as the electrodes copper and an amalgamated strip of zinc. The latter is prepared by placing the zinc for several minutes in a dilute solution of mercuric chloride. Observe the behavior of the amalgamated zinc strip both before and after being connected to the copper, compare with your observations upon the zinc strip in the cell of (a) and account for the difference. Are the cells of (a) and (b) polarizing or non-polarizing cells? (c) Connect the cells of (a) and (b) in series by joining the zinc strip of one cell with the copper strip of the second. Insert the tips of the remaining wires into a little dilute sulfuric acid. Observe the evolution of a gas (hydrogen) at the tip of one wire. Account for its liberation at this terminal. Replace the acid with a few cubic centimeters of a solution of sodium chloride to which a few drops of phenolphthalein solution have been added. Note what happens, and explain. 

4. Corrosion. Select three bright iron nails. Wrap firmly around one a small piece of tin wire; around the second, copper wire; and around the third, zinc wire. Place the nails in separate test tubes, cover them with water, and let them stand a week. 

Then note in which cases corrosion has occurred, and to what extent. Explain.

Set the cup in a beaker containing a solution of copper sulfate into which there extends a copper electrode. Connect the copper and zinc electrodes with a wire and let the cell run for several minutes. Do you observe any action at either electrode? Is the cell a polarizing or a nonpolarizing cell?

(b) Now break the connection between the two electrodes, connect each one to the terminals of a voltmeter, and read the deflection of the needle. Return the zinc chloride solution to its bottle. Clean the porous cup and the copper electrode by immersing them in dilute nitric acid and warming until the deposit has dissolved. 

6. The lead storage cell. Determine the polarity of the wires leading to the direct current outlet as follows. Moisten a piece of filter paper with a solution of sodium chloride to which a few drops of phenolphthalein have been added. With the ends of the wires about a centimeter apart, bring them in contact with the moistened filter paper. Explain the color change taking place at one terminal. Which pole is this? You thus have a means of identifying which wire is positive, and which negative.

Compare the direction of the flow of electricity during the charging operation with its direction during the discharge of the cell. In storage cells in what form is energy stored?

EXERCISE XXX HYDROCARBONS. FUEL GASES 

Most of our fuel gases—and the liquid fuels as well—contain combustible hydrocarbons, sometimes mixed with inorganic combustible substances. In this exercise some of the sources of the common fuel gases are noted, and the properties of the individual components of such gases examined. 

1. Preparation and properties of carbon monoxide. Place about 

5 g. of oxalic acid crystals in a large pyrex test tube, and cover them with 15 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid. Provide the test tube with a one-hole rubber stopper and delivery tube. Clamp the tube to a ring stand and apply a very gentle heat. When the evolution of gas is brisk, bubble it into limewater for a moment. 

What is one product of the decomposition of oxalic acid? Light the gas as it issues from the delivery tube and let it burn for a moment only. Note the appearance of the flame. What constituent of the gas burns?

Spread out about 0.5 g. of cupric oxide in a thin layer in a large bore hard glass tube. Connect this tube to the delivery tube of the carbon monoxide generator. Develop and maintain a good flow of carbon monoxide over the surface of the cupric oxide and at the same time heat the oxide strongly. Since carbon monoxide is toxic, prevent its escape into the room by igniting the gas at the end of the hard glass tube. Note and explain any change in the appearance of the cupric oxide. What property of carbon monoxide has been illustrated? Let the test tube generator cool before attempting to discard the sulfuric acid. 

2. Preparation of methane. Grind together 0.5 g. of anhydrous sodium acetate and twice this weight of soda-lime (a mixture of calcium oxide and sodium hydroxide). Place the well pulverized mixture in a hard glass test tube, and fit the tube with a rubber stopper and delivery tube. Clamp the tube in a horizontal position and arrange to collect a gas over water. Fill one small receiving bottle entirely with water and a second only half full. Heat the 126

HYDROCARBONS. FUEL GASES 

mixture in the tube and, after the air has been expelled from the apparatus, displace the water from both bottles with a stream of the gas. You now have one bottle containing methane only, and a second containing a mixture of methane and air. Ignite the gas in the first of these (the one with methane only) and observe the nature of the flame. Bring a lighted match to the mouth of the second bottle. Show the connection between this experiment and explosions in a coal mine. 

3. Reaction of saturated hydrocarbons with a halogen. To 10 cc. of gasoline (largely a mixture of the hydrocarbons of the methane series ¹) add 5 cc. of a 5% solution of bromine in carbon tetrachloride. Divide the mixture into two portions and set one portion aside in a dark place in your desk. Expose the second portion to direct sunlight for 10-15 minutes, then compare the solutions in the two tubes. The fading of the color of the solution exposed to sunlight is the result of a substitution reaction between the bromine and the hydrocarbons present in the gasoline. Note that sunlight greatly aided this reaction. Represent the reaction by an equation, using CH 16 as a representative hydrocarbon. 

4. Preparation and properties of acetylene. Fit a 100 cc. bottle with a two-hole rubber stopper carrying a delivery tube and a straight glass tube extending far down into the bottle. With a rubber connector attach a funnel to this tube, support the funnel with a ring of the stand, and close the connector with a pinch clamp. Place several pieces of calcium carbide in the bottle and insert the stopper. Then by partially opening the pinch clamp, admit water from the funnel, drop by drop. Displace all of the air first, and then collect a bottle of the gas over water. Take the bottle to the hood and there set fire to the gas. Compare the flame of burning acetylene with the appearance of the flames of burning methane and carbon monoxide. From a knowledge of the composition of each gas, account for the gradation. 

Pass a slow stream of acetylene for some time into a few cubic centimeters of strong bromine water and note the loss of color of the bromine water. Such an addition of a free halogen is characteristic of unsaturated compounds, such as acetylene, and is often used as a test for the presence of such compounds.

EXERCISE XXXI DERIVATIVES OF THE HYDROCARBONS 

In this exercise you are introduced to a few of the enormous number of derivatives of the hydrocarbons. No elementary text can pretend to teach very much about the chemistry of carbon compounds, but it is hoped that from this exercise you may gain some idea of the importance of these compounds in everyday life. 

1. Esterification. Note the odor of ethyl alcohol and of acetic acid. Warm gently a mixture of 5 cc. of ethyl alcohol with an equal volume of glacial acetic acid and 2 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid. Note the odor of the product of the reaction. What is formed? What part is played by the sulfuric acid?

To 5 cc. of sulfuric acid add an equal volume of amyl alcohol (C5H11OH), and 2 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid. Heat the mixture for a few minutes. What ester is formed in this case? 

Describe the odor.

Mix about 0.5 g. of salicylic acid (HOC,H,COOH), 5 cc. of methyl alcohol, and 1 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid. After warming the mixture, note and describe the odor of the ester formed in this reaction. Suggest a use for such compounds. 

To what class of inorganic compounds may the alcohols be compared on the basis of the above reactions? 

2. Hydrolysis of esters. Saponification. The hydrolysis of an ester results in the formation of the corresponding alcohol and acid. Contrast this reaction with those carried out in exp. 1. 

When the decomposition of the ester is brought about by an alkali, forming a salt of the corresponding acid, the reaction is called saponification (soap making).

Weigh out 20 g. of lard into a beaker. Prepare a 50% solution of sodium hydroxide by adding 5 g. of the solid to 5 cc. of water. 

Stir this into the lard, and heat the mixture cautiously for an hour by dipping the beaker into a larger one containing boiling water. Stir the mixture until it hardens. This is crude soap. 

Cut it into fine pieces and dissolve it in about 20 cc. of hot water. 130

DERIVATIVES OF THE HYDROCARBONS 

Add 100 cc. of a saturated sodium chloride solution and set aside until the next exercise. Remove a portion of the top layer, moisten with water, and rub on your hands. What is it? Account for its presence on the surface. What part does the sodium chloride play in soap making? 

3. Oxidation product of alcohols. Acidify 5 cc. of a solution of potassium dichromate with dilute sulfuric acid. Add 5 cc. of methyl alcohol, and warm the tube gently until the color of the contents changes. Note the odor of the oxidation product, formaldehyde, which is rather volatile. What is the reduction product? Oxidize ethyl alcohol in a similar manner. What is the oxidation product in this case? 

4. Reduction by aldehydes. Since the aldehydes may be converted into the corresponding acids by further oxidation, one of the properties of aldehydes is their reducing action. 

Note its odor. (a) Clean a test tube by placing 15 cc. of sodium hydroxide solution in it and heating the tube until the solution begins to boil. Rinse thoroughly with water, and then place in the tube about 5 cc. of a dilute solution of silver nitrate. Add a dilute solution of ammonium hydroxide, drop by drop, until the precipitate which first appears is just dissolved. Now add a cubic centimeter of dilute formaldehyde solution and put the tube into warm water. What substance forms a mirror on the tube? What is the other product of this reaction? The reflecting surfaces of mirrors are usually prepared by reactions similar to this. (b) Mix half a cubic centimeter of copper sulfate solution with 5 cc. of a solution of sodium potassium tartrate, and make the solution strongly alkaline with sodium hydroxide. The precipitation of cupric hydroxide is prevented by the presence of the tartrate, which reduces the concentration of the cupric ion by the formation of a complex ion. Heat this solution nearly to boiling, and add a few drops of glucose solution. After a few minutes again heat the solution to boiling. A precipitate of cuprous oxide appears, formed by the reduction of cupric ion in alkaline solution. 

Glucose is a sugar which contains an aldehyde group (-CHO), and hence is a "reducing" sugar.

Determine in the same way whether cane sugar is a reducing sugar. Now dissolve about one gram of cane sugar in 10 cc. of water, add 5 drops of concentrated hydrochloric acid, and bring the solution gradually to the boiling point. Allow it to cool, and

DERIVATIVES OF THE HYDROCARBONS 

then neutralize the acid by adding, drop by drop, a solution of sodium carbonate until the resulting mixture is just alkaline to litmus. Test this in the same manner as you did the glucose solution. Explain the result. 

5. Conversion of starch into sugar. Starch is a carbohydrate which is more complex than either sugar or glucose. When boiled with an acid, it is converted into carbohydrates of lower molecular weight, the final product being glucose. (a) Grind a gram of starch with 20 cc. of water until a smooth paste results; pour this into 200 cc. of boiling water and heat for a few minutes. Test a little of this starch paste for the presence of a reducing substance in the same way as in exp. 4(b). Result? 

Now acidify 50 cc. of the starch paste with an equal volume of concentrated hydrochloric acid, place the mixture in a flask, and boil very gently for about 45 minutes, taking care not to boil off much of the water. At the end of this time remove 10 cc. of the liquid, neutralize with sodium hydroxide solution, and determine if a reducing sugar is now present. (b) Chew a piece of soda cracker for several minutes, then test the chewed mass for the presence of a reducing sugar. The saliva contains an organic compound (known as an enzyme) which has the ability to convert starch to sugar. 

6. Nitration of benzene. In a small flask cautiously add 10 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid to 10 cc. of concentrated nitric acid. 

To this mixture of acids add, drop by drop, 5 cc. of benzene, shaking the flask after each addition. Support the flask in a beaker of warm water for 15 minutes, then add 5 cc. more of concentrated sulfuric acid, and again warm the flask as before for 15 minutes. 

Finally pour the mixture into a large volume of water. After a short time crystals of di-nitro-benzene should appear. Note the odor of the contents of the beaker. This odor is due to the formation of some mono-nitro-benzene. Of what value is the nitration of benzene? Why is sulfuric acid used in nitration reactions? 

7. Preparation of methyl orange, a "coal tar dye." Prepare the following solutions: (a) 12 g. of dimethyl aniline in 12 cc. of concentrated hydrochloric acid and 40 cc. of water. (b) 5 g. of anhydrous sodium carbonate in 200 cc. of water. (c) 7 g. of sodium nitrite in 40 cc. of water.

DERIVATIVES OF THE HYDROCARBONS 

Weigh 20 g. of sulfanilic acid and dissolve it in the solution of sodium carbonate. Do not heat in order to effect solution. To this solution add the solution of sodium nitrite and cool the whole solution by immersing the beaker in a freezing mixture of ice and salt. To the cold solution add, a drop at a time, 8 cc. of concentrated hydrochloric acid. Stir the solution constantly and try to keep the temperature below 10°. Add the dimethyl aniline dissolved in hydrochloric acid. Make the solution just alkaline (litmus) with sodium hydroxide, and add 20 g. of sodium chloride to "salt out" the dye. After half an hour filter the precipitated solid by suction, wash with a little cold water, and dry at room temperature. The dye may be purified by a crystallization from a very little hot water.

Dissolve a pinch of the solid in a few cubic centimeters of water and divide the solution into two parts. To one add a few drops of some acid, and to the other a few drops of a base. Could this dye be used as an indicator for acids and bases?

SECTION II 

QUALITATIVE ANALYSIS

ELEMENTARY QUALITATIVE ANALYSIS 

Qualitative analysis is a systematic examination of a substance to determine its composition. Such analysis should show what metals and non-metals are present, and should afford some indication of their relative proportions. However, before chemical tests may be applied, the substance must usually have been dissolved in suitable reagents. Hence, although the original material may have contained a number of free elements (as in the case of a metal alloy), the analysis becomes a pursuit of the various ions present in solution.

For each ion there have been assembled a limited number of preliminary experiments, demonstrating certain of the chemical reactions of that ion. Intelligent performance of these experiments will result in a better understanding, not only of the steps involved in the systematic analysis of a substance containing the ions in question, but also of the reasons for the particular procedure employed.

Solutions of ions used in preliminary experiments should, for satisfactory results, be of the concentrations specified in the Table of Laboratory Reagents (Appendix). Hereafter such solutions will be designated as "test solutions." 3

PART I DETECTION OF THE NON-METAL IONS 

The anions, or the corresponding acids, may be classified according to several schemes, the simplest of which depends upon the different solubilities of their silver and barium (or calcium) salts. As this course will include only those anions which occur most commonly and whose detection is not too involved, a list of such anions must necessarily be incomplete. For convenience in analysis, these may be divided into three groups. 

Silver Group (Group I) 

Those acids, or anions, whose silver salts are insoluble in water and in nitric acid. The following members of this group will be encountered: Sulfide Ion; Cyanide Ion; Chloride Ion; Bromide 

Ion; Iodide Ion. The acids corresponding to these negative ions have the further common characteristic of being volatile and at least reasonably stable.

Barium Group (Group II) 

Those acids, or anions, whose barium or calcium salts are insoluble in neutral or alkaline solution. To this group belong the 

Sulfate Ion, Sulfite Ion, Carbonate Ion, Phosphate Ion, Arsenate Ion, Borate Ion, and Fluoride Ion. 

Soluble Group (Group III) 

Those acids, or anions, whose silver and barium salts are soluble in water. Of this group only the Nitrate and Nitrite Ions will be studied.

In studying the so-called "acidic analysis", the student should keep constantly in mind that the tests given are tests for the acidic ion, from any source, and not merely from the free acid itself. 

Further, it must be noted that the material to be tested should contain no metal ions other than sodium or potassium, although the methods of obtaining solutions free from interfering cations is beyond the scope of these exercises.

THE  (GROUP I) 

S¯; CN-; Cl-; Br−; I- 

PRELIMINARY EXPERIMENTS 

Chloride Ion, Cl- 

1. Reaction with silver nitrate. To 2 cc. of the test solution of 

Cl- add two or three drops of silver nitrate solution. Note the color of the precipitate. Divide this into two portions. Test the solubility of one portion in 5 cc. of nitric acid; of the other, in 

5 cc. of ammonium hydroxide. Describe the results. 

2. Reaction with concentrated sulfuric acid. Place one gram of sodium chloride in a test tube; then add 5 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid, pouring the acid carefully down the side of the tube. 

Observe the action of the escaping gas on moistened blue litmus paper. Blow across the mouth of the tube, and compare the result with that observed when the breath is blown over an open bottle of concentrated hydrochloric acid. What gas is formed by the action of concentrated sulfuric acid on sodium chloride? Is this reaction of any industrial importance? Write the equation for the reaction. 

3. Reaction with manganese dioxide and sulfuric acid. Mix half a gram of sodium chloride with an equal weight of manganese dioxide. Place the mixture in a test tube and add 2 cc. of sulfuric acid. Heat gently, and note the color and odor of the evolved gas. 

What is the gas? Represent the reaction by an electronic equation. 

4. Reaction with potassium dichromate and sulfuric acid. Mix 

0.2 g. of dry sodium chloride with one gram of dry potassium dichromate by grinding them together in a mortar. Place all of this mixture in a dry pyrex test tube of appropriate bore, and cover with 5-6 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid. Moisten the inside of a Gooch (mushroom) trap ¹ with a little water and insert into the top of the tube as illustrated in Fig. 1. Cautiously heat the contents of the tube until the brownish vapors cease to be evolved. 

1 If such a trap is not available for this experiment (and later ones in which a trap is specified), a 50 cc. distilling flask may be substituted for the pyrex test tube. To collect the distillate place a test tube, the inside of which has been moistened with water, over the side arm of the flask.

Sufficient heat must be applied, however, to drive these fumes into the trap. Chromyl chloride, CrO2Cl2, distills out of the mixture, and the reactions for its formation may be considered to occur as follows: 

K2Cr2O7+ 2H2SO4 → 2CrO3 + 2KHSO4 + H2O. NaCl + H2SO4 → HCl + NaHSO4. 

CrO3 + 2HCl → CrO2Cl2 + H2O. 

The chromyl chloride is decomposed by the water in the trap (an example of a reaction known as hydrolysis):

CrO2Cl2 + 2HOH → H₂CrO4 + 2HCl. 

Remove the trap from the test tube. Rinse the inner walls of the trap with not more than 5 cc. of water, catching the liquid in a clean test tube. The color (what is it?) of the solution is characteristic of the chromate ion. 

To the solution in the tube add 3 cc. of ammonium acetate solution and a few drops of lead acetate solution. A yellow precipitate indicates the presence of the chromate ion. This, however, could have appeared only as the result of the intermediate formation of chromyl chloride. Hence the appearance of a yellow precipitate also serves as a test for the chloride ion in the original substance. 

Bromide Ion, Br- 

1. Reaction with silver nitrate. To 2 cc. of the test solution of Br- add a few drops of silver nitrate solution. Compare the color of this 

FIG. 1.-Gooch precipitate with that of the silver chloride previously obtained. Continue the comparison by testing the solubility of this precipitate in 5 cc. of nitric acid and in 5 cc. of ammonium hydroxide. Trap. 

2. Reaction with concentrated sulfuric acid. Place half a gram of potassium bromide in a test tube; then pour 3 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid carefully down the side of the tube. Blow across the mouth of the tube. What evidence is there that hydrogen bromide is present in the escaping gases? To what is the color due? Ac-

count for the formation of the colored gas, and write an electronic equation for the reaction which produces it. 

3. Displacement of bromine by chlorine. Dilute 2 cc. of the test solution of Br- with 10 cc. of water, add 3 cc. of carbon disulfide (or carbon tetrachloride) and 2-5 drops of chlorine water. Shake the tube and, after the carbon disulfide has settled, observe the color of the bead. Explain what you observe, and represent the reaction electronically. 

Iodide Ion, I- 

1. Reaction with silver nitrate. To 2 cc. of the test solution of of I add a few drops of silver nitrate solution. Note the color of the precipitate, and determine the effect (if any) of 5 cc. of nitric acid and of 5 cc. of ammonium hydroxide on separate portions of the precipitate. Compare your observations with those made on similar reactions with the chloride and bromide ions. 

2. Reaction with concentrated sulfuric acid. To a small crystal of potassium iodide in a test tube cautiously add 3 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid. Observe the color of the evolved gases. To what is the color due? Hold in the path of the gases a filter paper moistened with lead acetate. In order to interpret the results in this case, perform the following test tube experiments: (a) to one portion of a solution of lead acetate add a few drops of potassium iodide solution; (b) to a second portion add hydrogen sulfide. 

Note the result in each case, compare with what you observe on the filter paper, and draw your own conclusions. Account for the formation, as a result of the reaction of the iodide with concentrated sulfuric acid, of all the products indicated by your observations. 

3. Displacement of iodine by chlorine. Dilute 2 cc. of the test solution of I- with 10 cc. of water. Add 3 cc. of carbon disulfide, then a few drops of chlorine water, and shake. Note the color of the bead when the carbon disulfide has settled. Compare this with the bromine-carbon disulfide solution. 

Cyanide Ion, CN Hydrogen cyanide and cyanides are extremely poisonous; therefore, handle all cyanide solutions with the greatest care. All experiments with cyanides in which a gas is evolved must be performed in a well-ventilated hood. Dilute sulfuric acid, when added

to a cyanide solution, will (with the exception of mercuric cyanide solution) cause the evolution of hydrogen cyanide. Why? Should you expect hydrochloric acid to act similarly? Do NOT attempt to perform the experiment. 

1. Reaction with silver nitrate. To 2 cc. of the test solution of

CN-add 1 cc. of water and half a cubic centimeter of silver nitrate solution. Observe the color of the precipitate. Divide this solid into two portions, and determine the solubility of one portion in 5 cc. of ammonium hydroxide. Which halide ion does the cyanide ion resemble most closely? To the second portion of the precipitate add an excess of potassium cyanide solution. What you observe is a general characteristic of cyanides, and the reaction which takes place may be represented as follows:

AgCN Ag+ + CN- Ag++2CN-Ag(CN)7. 

The formation of complex cyanides is further demonstrated in the next experiment. 

2. Formation of Prussian Blue. Prussian Blue is formed by reaction between ferric salts and a ferrocyanide. For this to apply to the cyanide ion, the latter must be transformed into the ferrocyanide ion. Take 2 cc. of the test solution of CN-, add 1 cc. of ferrous sulfate solution, then 1 cc. of sodium hydroxide. Warm the solution for a minute, then carefully acidify with concentrated hydrochloric acid and add one drop of ferric chloride solution.

A blue (or greenish blue) color is an indirect test for the presence of the cyanide ion. This test depends upon the formation of a complex ion, the reactions being: Fe(OH)2 Fe+++ 20H- Fe+++6CN-→→ Fe(CN)ō 4Fe++++ 3Fe(CN) →Fe4[Fe(CN)6]3(blue). 

Although the formation of the ferrocyanide ion does not require the presence of alkali, its formation is more rapid when the ferrous ion is in the form of ferrous hydroxide. A large excess of alkali is, however, to be avoided. The solution of ferrous ion usually contains sufficient ferric ion to produce the blue precipitate, upon addition of acid, without the further addition of ferric chloride.

Sulfide Ion, S 

1. Reaction with silver nitrate. Add a few drops of silver nitrate solution to 1 cc. of the test solution of S. What is the color of the precipitate? Compare with the colors of the silver salts of the other anions of the Silver Group. Test the solubility of the precipitate in 1 cc. of dilute nitric acid. 

2. Reaction with sulfuric acid. In separate test tubes observe the effect of dilute sulfuric acid on 5 cc. of the test solution of S and on a piece of ferrous sulfide. Cautiously note the odor of the escaping gas. What gas is this? Moisten a piece of filter paper with lead acetate solution and hold this at the mouth of the test tube. Note the black (or brown) coloration and account for its formation. This is a good test for the gas and for those sulfides soluble in acids. The sulfuric acid may be replaced by hydrochloric acid, but not by nitric acid. Why not?

Prepare a summary of the reactions of the anions of the Silver Group by filling in the accompanying table. Whenever a precipitate appears as the result of a reaction, the color of that precipitate must also be recorded. Have the assistant check your statements, and then proceed with the systematic analysis of a solution for the anions of the Silver Group.

SYSTEMATIC ANALYSIS OF THE SILVER GROUP 

The beginner should learn to follow directions exactly, but not blindly. Read over the complete directions for a test, and then try to perform the test without further reference to the manual. 

Form the habit of thinking why each step of the analysis is taken. 

Use very small quantities of reagents, exactly as directed. These analytical tests are, for the most part, far more delicate than is usually realized, and the use of large amounts of solutions often ruins the procedure completely. Work carefully. 

From the experiments performed with the anions of the Silver 

Group, note: (a) that the group reagent is silver nitrate, producing in each case a precipitate insoluble in nitric acid; and (b), that cyanide and sulfide ions interfere with the tests for the halide ions and should therefore be removed before testing for the latter. 

Give two reasons for the interference.

Prepare a practice solution by mixing 2 cc. each of the test solutions of the ions of this group.

NOTES

1. Tests for Cyanide and Sulfide Ions. Prove the presence of cyanide ion in this practice solution by testing 1 cc. of the mixture in accordance with the directions given for the formation of Prussian Blue (exp. 2 under the discussion of the Cyanide Ion). With 

2 cc. of the practice solution test for the sulfide ion as directed in the study of the behavior of this ion toward sulfuric acid (exp. 2 under the discussion of the Sulfide Ion). 

Since the analysis of the solution for the halide ions can be carried out only in the absence of both of the above ions, their removal from the remainder of the solution is accomplished as directed in the next experiment. 

2. Removal of Cyanide and Sulfide Ions. Take the remainder of the practice solution to the hood, add 5 cc. of acetic acid, and evaporate to dryness. Heat the solid residue for a moment. Cool, and take up the residue in 10 cc. of water. With 2 cc. portions of this solution test for the cyanide and sulfide ions. Reserve the remainder for the halide ion tests. Explain how cyanide and sulfide are removed by this treatment. State why acetic acid is used in place of (a) hydrochloric acid, (b) sulfuric acid, (c) nitric acid. 

3. Test for the Iodide Ion. To 2 cc. of the solution reserved from the preceding experiment, add 5 cc. of dilute sulfuric acid and an equal volume of potassium dichromate solution. Add a little carbon disulfide and shake the contents of the tube. Observe the color of the carbon disulfide bead by transmitted light. To what is the color due? Under the conditions of the experiment the iodide ion only is oxidized, and the reaction may be represented by the following equation:

Cr₂07 +61 + 14H+ → 2Cr+++ + 31½ + 7H₂O. 

4. Test for the Bromide Ion. The color of a bromine-carbon disulfide solution, when this halogen is obtained in the free state by the action of an oxidizing agent on a bromide in the presence of carbon disulfide, is a test for the bromide ion. If, however, the iodide ion is also present, free iodine will be produced at the same time, as demonstrated in the preliminary experiments. Further, the intense color of the free iodine will mask the color of the bromine. Hence the following means is employed to detect the bromide ion.

With 5 cc. of dilute sulfuric acid, acidify 2 cc. of the solution

reserved after removal of cyanide and sulfide, and add 3 cc. of carbon disulfide. Dilute some sodium hypochlorite solution with at least an equal volume of water, and add this diluted solution drop by drop to the acidified halide solution, shaking the tube after each addition. Note the first color of the carbon disulfide and the change of color which follows. By the action of the hypochlorous acid, the following valence changes are accomplished successively: 21+ HClO + H+ → I2 + Cl + H2O, 2Br+ HClO + H+ → Br₂ + Cl− + H2O, I2 + 5HCIO + H₂O → 2103 + 5Cl− + 7H+. 

If too much hypochlorite is added,

Br2 + 5HCIO + H2O → 2Br0+ 5Cl-+7H+; and the test sought for is lost. Therefore, add the hypochlorite solution very carefully.

Note that the above method accomplishes the detection of the bromide ion, even in the presence of iodide, because of the greater ease of oxidation of the latter ion. Give two additional examples which demonstrate this difference between the two ions. 

5. Tests for the Chloride Ion. When either or both the iodide and bromide ions are present, the test for the chloride may be made through the intermediate formation of chromyl chloride. Bromides and iodides do not form corresponding chromium compounds, but are oxidized to the free state. The iodide ion does, however, interfere with this test for chloride. When known to be present, iodide may be removed in the following manner. To a portion of the solution from which cyanide and sulfide ions have been removed, add sufficient bromine water to oxidize all of the iodide to the free state. Evaporate this to dryness in a porcelain casserole, treat the residue as directed in the preliminary experiment on the formation of chromyl chloride (exp. 4 under the discussion of the Chloride 

Ion), and observe the indirect test for the chloride ion. Explain: (a) why the iodide ion, if not removed, would destroy the effectiveness of the above test for the chloride ion; (b) how the conditions employed accomplish the removal of the iodide ion. 

Before proceeding with the formation of chromyl chloride, it is essential that no chlorate be present; otherwise explosive oxides of chlorine will be formed. Methods of removing the chlorate ion before testing for the chloride ion are beyond the scope of this

manual. If required, consult advanced texts on analytical procedure.

After the removal of sulfide and cyanide, and in the absence of iodide and bromide, the presence of the chloride ion will be indicated by the formation, upon the addition of a few drops of silver nitrate solution, of a white precipitate soluble in ammonium hydroxide, but insoluble in dilute nitric acid. 

6. Analysis of an unknown solution. Procure from the assistant a solution the contents of which are to be determined and, by following the procedure just outlined, determine what anions of the Silver Group are present in the unknown. For this purpose 

10 cc. of the solution will be sufficient. If cyanide or sulfide are found, remove these from the entire unknown as directed; then use portions of this prepared solution for the halide tests. The choice of procedure for the detection of chloride will be governed by results of the tests for iodide and bromide.

As you proceed with the analysis, keep a record of the observations from which you infer the composition of the "unknown." 

REVIEW OF THE SILVER GROUP (GROUP I) 

1. For each of the following pairs suggest a procedure, involving one step only, by which a separation may be accomplished: (a) S= and Cl¯; (b) I¯ and Cl-; (c) CN and Cl¯¯; (d) I¯and Br¯; (e) AgCl and AgI. 

2. Suggest a procedure for proving the presence of chloride in silver chloride. 

3. In terms of the solubility product concept, explain why silver chloride is the last to precipitate when silver nitrate solution is added to a solution containing a chloride, a bromide, and an iodide. 

4. In terms of the solubility product concept, explain why silver chloride is the first to dissolve when dilute ammonium hydroxide is added to a mixture of the chloride, bromide, and iodide of silver.

THE  (GROUP II)

PRELIMINARY EXPERIMENTS 

Sulfate Ion, SO In separate test tubes place 1 cc. of test solutions of the anions of this group. Make each solution alkaline, if not already so, by adding a drop or two of sodium hydroxide. To each add a few drops of the special reagent, barium chloride-calcium chloride solution (prepared according to the directions in the Table of 

Laboratory Reagents of the Appendix). Note the result. Then add a little hydrochloric acid to each tube and mix the acid thoroughly with the contents of the tube. On the basis of your observations, devise a test for the presence of the sulfate ion. Give the purpose of each step in the test. 

Note: The sulfites of barium and calcium are actually soluble in hydrochloric acid. The result obtained in the case of the sulfite ion may be explained by the fact that sulfites, because of oxidation by the air, are contaminated with sulfate. 

Carbonate Ion, CO 1. Reaction with acids. Observe the action of dilute sulfuric acid on small portions of different carbonates. Account for the formation of a gas. To produce this gas, may other acids be substituted for sulfuric acid? Why? 

2. Test for carbon dioxide and carbonates. Place 5 cc. of the test solution of CO in a pyrex test tube, of such bore that a Gooch trap fits in the end. Moisten the inside of the trap ¹ with limewater (calcium hydroxide solution). Make the carbonate solution just acid with sulfuric acid, and immediately insert the trap. Heat the solution carefully, so as not to drive any of it into the trap. Do not heat for more than 30 seconds after the solution begins to boil. Observe the condition of the limewater in the trap 

1 If a 50 cc. distilling flask is substituted for the pyrex test tube, use a test tube filled with sufficient limewater so that the end of the side arm of the distilling flask may be just under the surface of the limewater. 14

and account for the result. What would be the result if an excess of carbon dioxide is passed into limewater? Why are other acids not so suitable as sulfuric acid for this method of identification of a carbonate? Save the trap and contents.

Sulfite Ion, SO 1. Reaction with acids. Observe the action of dilute sulfuric acid on 5 cc. of the test solution of SO. Cautiously smell the evolved gas. Compare its properties with those of carbon dioxide. 

2. Action of sulfur dioxide upon calcium carbonate. Place 10 cc. of the test solution of SO, in a pyrex test tube, and acidify with sulfuric acid. Insert the Gooch trap with its calcium carbonate content (reserved from exp. 2 of the discussion of the Carbonate 

Ion), and warm the test tube. Do not boil the liquid over into the trap. Is there any evidence of reaction between the calcium carbonate and sulfur dioxide? Explain. Why is the method of testing for a carbonate, as presented in exp. 2 under the Carbonate Ion, not satisfactory in the presence of sulfite? A method of detecting the carbonate ion in the presence of the sulfite ion will be developed in the following experiments. 

3. Reducing action of sulfurous acid. Add sulfurous acid to a few cubic centimeters of bromine water in a test tube. Explain the loss in color. Divide the resulting solution into two portions. Test one for the presence of sulfate ion; to the second add nitric acid and a few drops of silver nitrate solution. What is the reduction product? Represent electronically the reaction of sulfurous acid and bromine water.

Note the effect of adding sulfurous acid to a few cubic centimeters of (a) potassium permanganate solution, and (b) dilute nitric acid. Test each solution for sulfate ion and account for the presence of this ion. 

4. Test for sulfur dioxide and sulfites. Prepare a dilute solution of potassium permanganate by adding one drop of the permanganate solution to 10 cc. of water. Wet the inside of a Gooch trap with this diluted solution. Place 10 cc. of the test solution of SO in a pyrex test tube, and acidify with sulfuric acid. Insert the trap containing a small amount of the permanganate solution, and heat the test tube. Be careful not to drive any of the liquid into the trap. Observe the loss of color of the permanganate solution in

the trap, and explain. Rinse the inside of the trap with 5 cc. of water, catching the liquid in a clean test tube, and test this liquid for the presence of sulfate ion. 

Note: The disappearance of the color in the permanganate solution is not necessarily due to the action of sulfur dioxide. Other reducing agents may be evolved from the acid mixture and decolorize the permanganate solution. Hence this observation must be supplemented by a sulfate test in order to be a positive means of identifying a sulfite. 

5. Detection of Carbonate Ion in the presence of Sulfite Ion. 

Place in a pyrex test tube 5 cc. each of the test solutions of CO and SO. To this mixture add potassium permanganate solution, a little at a time, until the color persists after thorough mixing. 

A moderate excess will do no harm. What is the function of the permanganate? Now acidify the solution with sulfuric acid and immediately insert into the test tube a Gooch trap, the inside of which has previously been moistened with limewater. Heat, with the same precautions given previously. The formation of a precipitate in the trap will now be a reliable indication of the presence of carbonate. Why?

Phosphate Ion, PO 

1. Reaction with silver nitrate. To 2 cc. of the test solution of

PO add a few drops of silver nitrate solution. What is the precipitate? Note its color. Divide the precipitate into two portions. 

Test the solubility of one portion in nitric acid; that of the other in ammonium hydroxide. Explain your results. These two reactions are characteristic of the insoluble silver salts of this group of anions (Barium Group). 

2. Test for the Phosphate Ion. Acidify 1 cc. of the test solution of PO with an equal volume of dilute nitric acid, and add the acidified solution to 5 cc. of ammonium molybdate, (NH4)2M0O4 (see Appendix). The final mixture must be acid to litmus. Note the formation of a yellow precipitate, the formula of which is (NH4) 3PO4-12MoO3. Its formation is usually slow, and may require warming if only a trace of phosphate is present. Always add the phosphate solution to the ammonium molybdate of volume at least equal to that of the phosphate solution, and be certain that the final mixture is acid.

Arsenate Ion, AsO 

1. Reaction with silver nitrate. Add a few drops of silver nitrate solution to 2 cc. of the test solution of AsOF. Compare this precipitate with that obtained when a phosphate was used, as to color and solubility in (a) nitric acid and (b) ammonium hydroxide. 

2. Test for the Arsenate Ion. Treat ammonium molybdate solution with the test solution of AsO exactly as directed in testing for phosphate. Can you distinguish the result from that obtained for the phosphate test? Obviously the reaction with ammonium molybdate is a delicate test for phosphate only in the absence of the arsenate ion. The same is true of other tests for the phosphate ion. Normally the presence of arsenic is detected during metal ion analysis and, when arsenate is known to be present, it must be removed before making the phosphate test. 

When this is required, consult advanced texts on qualitative procedure. 

Borate Ion, BO.

Place 5 cc. of the test solution of BO in a large pyrex test tube. Make definitely acid with dilute hydrochloric acid. Dip a piece of turmeric paper into this solution, wet it thoroughly, and dry it by placing it on a watch glass above a beaker of boiling water. (A reddish-brown color usually develops.) Moisten the dry paper with a few drops of sodium hydroxide. If the paper develops a dark olive-green color (shade and intensity will vary somewhat), a borate is indicated.

Fluoride Ion, F- 1. Test for the Fluoride Ion. A delicate test for the presence of the fluoride ion may be performed in the following manner. Mix 

0.5 g. of calcium fluoride with an equal bulk of potassium acid sulfate and powdered glass by grinding them together in a mortar. 

Place some of the mixture in an ignition tube at least 15 cm. long. 

Heat the mixture slowly. Note the sublimate which deposits on the walls of the cooler portion of the tube. The reactions by which this sublimate is produced may be expressed as follows: 2 CaF2+2KHSO4 → H₂F₂+ CaSO4 + K2SO4; SiO2 + 2H2F2 → SiF₁ ↑ + 2H2O; 

SiF₁ + H₂F₂ ↔ H₂SiF6.

Allow the tube to cool, and cut off the lower end of the tube just below the white sublimate. Run a little water through that portion of the tube which has the sublimate, and then heat it. Note that the white solid no longer sublimes. The following equations express what has taken place:

SiF4+3HOH → H¿SiO3 + 2H₂F2; 

HSiO, (heated) →SiO2 + H2O. 

Compare the fluoride ion with the other halide ions as to (a) the solubility of the silver salt, and (b) ease of oxidation. 

2. Etching. Warm a glass plate by holding it in a luminous flame for a few minutes. Rub the upper surface of the hot glass plate with a small piece of paraffin until the surface is completely covered. Warm the plate again, tilting it about to obtain uniform distribution of the paraffin. Allow to cool, and with the point of the file or knife blade cut out a design through the paraffin. Make the cuts deep enough to expose the glass. In a lead dish make a paste of a small amount of calcium fluoride with concentrated sulfuric acid, stirring the paste with a splint. Warm the dish cautiously in the hood until the evolution of gas is sufficient to cause the formation of white fumes when a glass rod previously dipped in ammonium hydroxide is held over the dish. Then place the plate, with paraffined side down, over the dish, and allow to stand in the hood for an hour or longer. Finally remove the paraffin with the aid of hot water and note the effect on the glass where it was not protected. Explain.

Before passing to the systematic analysis of a solution for the anions of the Barium Group, fill out that portion of the accompanying chart which deals with the anions of this group. Give colors of all precipitates. When a given reagent has no effect, specify this by the term "none.”

SYSTEMATIC ANALYSIS OF THE BARIUM GROUP 

Note that the group reagent for this group of anions is a mixture of barium chloride and calcium chloride solutions. The use of the mixture is more advantageous than the use of either reagent alone, because in some cases the most insoluble salt of the anion is the barium salt; whereas in other cases it is the calcium salt which is the most insoluble. By combining reagents the maximum precipitating action is obtained

IN NEUTRAL SOLUTION Soluble Group (Group III)

With separate portions of a substance or solution to be analyzed, carry out the tests indicated in your preliminary experiments.

In testing for the sulfate ion, it is advisable to acidify the solution first, then to add barium chloride. The order in which the ions may be detected is optional, except in those cases in which interferences may occur. For example, test for the sulfite ion before testing for the carbonate ion, since the choice of procedure for detecting the latter depends upon the presence or absence of the former.

If the solution being analyzed also contains ions of the Silver Group (Group I), the ions of this group may act as possible interferences. Normally the sulfide and cyanide ions would be removed before testing for the ions of the Barium Group. The method by which these ions are removed, however, will also result in the removal of carbonate and possibly the sulfite ions. Why? Hence tests for sulfite and carbonate must be made on portions of the unknown originally issued.

THE ANIONS OF THE SOLUBLE GROUP (GROUP III) 

NO3; NO₂

PRELIMINARY EXPERIMENTS 

Nitrate Ion, NO3 

1. Test for the Nitrate Ion. To 1 cc. of the test solution of

NO3 cautiously add 5 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid, and cool the mixture by running tap water over the test tube. Incline the tube and carefully run in, from a second test tube, 5 cc. of freshly prepared ferrous sulfate solution. With reasonable care, this solution will form a layer over the heavier sulfuric acid. A brown (or black) color, the formation of which may require a few minutes, at the junction of the two layers is a delicate test for the nitrate ion. The substance, of indefinite composition and usually indicated as FeSO4xNO, is the product of the reaction of nitric oxide with the ferrous ion. Express by equations the reactions by which nitric oxide is formed. Why will the use of dilute sulfuric acid not result in a satisfactory test? 

2. Interferences in the test for the Nitrate Ion. Prepare a mixture containing iodide, bromide, and nitrate ions, by taking 2 cc. of each of the test solutions of these ions. To a small portion of this mixture cautiously add a few cubic centimeters of concentrated sulfuric acid. Account for the result. Could you detect the brown ring of the nitrate test in the presence of bromide and iodide?

Obviously their removal is essential to the subsequent success of the test for the nitrate ion. 

To the remainder of the mixture containing iodide, bromide, and nitrate, add 5 cc. of the special reagent, ammoniacal silver sulfate. Then acidify with sulfuric acid. How does the use of the special reagent accomplish the removal of iodide and bromide ions? 

Filter off the precipitate, and test the filtrate for the nitrate ion. Result? 

Nitrite Ion, NO₂

To 5 cc. of the test solution of NO2 add a few drops of ferrous sulfate solution. What happens? The solution of ferrous sulfate 20

ANIONS OF THE SOLUBLE GROUP 

is acidic, due to hydrolysis. With this in mind, interpret your experimental observation. With concentrated sulfuric acid the reaction, although more vigorous, would be the same. Why is the formation of the substance of brown color possible through the use of dilute acid (such as is present in the ferrous sulfate solution)? Compare with the nitrate test.

Before proceeding to the group analysis, supply the information requested in the table facing p. 18, for the nitrate and nitrite ions. 

SYSTEMATIC ANALYSIS OF THE SOLUBLE GROUP 

Analysis of a solution containing the anions of this group involves the detection of nitrate in the presence of nitrite. The preliminary experiment on the nitrite ion indicates that this ion must be removed in order to test for the nitrate ion. 

Acidify 5 cc. of the test solution of NO2 with an equal volume of dilute sulfuric acid, and boil for a few minutes. Why does this procedure cause the removal of nitrite ion? Cool the solution thoroughly, then make a test for nitrate ion. Result? Methods of removing nitrite result in the formation of some nitrate, so that this ion will be detected even though none was originally present. 

There is no satisfactory method of making a nitrate test in the presence of nitrite.

When testing for nitrate ion in the absence of nitrite, do not overlook the possibility of the presence of interfering ions and the necessity of their removal, as directed in the preliminary experiments.

PROCEDURE FOR SYSTEMATIC ANION ANALYSIS 

An idea of the anions present in a general unknown may be gained from the results obtained upon application of the experimental steps outlined in the review of the Silver and Barium Groups. A complete determination of the anions present can, however, be obtained only by following the recommendations given in the directions for the systematic analysis of each group. 

Attention is again directed to the fact that examination for acid constituents is made on a solution containing only sodium and potassium as the cations. A common method of establishing this condition, when ions of the heavy metals are present, is as follows. 

Add to the solution sufficient concentrated sodium carbonate solution to produce an alkaline effect, and boil until the odor of ammonia (if the ammonium ion is present) can no longer be detected. Replace the water lost by evaporation during the boiling, and filter. The filtrate may now be designated as the "prepared solution"; and the tests described in the systematic analysis for each group of anions are made on this "prepared solution." 

Do not overlook the fact that carbonate has been introduced into the "prepared solution." A direct test for this ion may be made on a small portion of the original solution. For a more general treatment of the preparation of substances for anion analysis, refer to advanced texts on qualitative procedure. 22 22

PART II THE SYSTEMATIC CATION ANALYSIS 

In elementary cation analysis tests are made usually for about twenty of the more common metals, in the form of their ions. 

Rarely is it possible to apply specific tests for a single ion directly to a solution which contains many of the other ions. Instead, ions of somewhat similar characteristics are segregated into comparatively small groups by the action of group reagents. To the group precipitates so obtained may then be applied a proper sequence of reactions to bring about a separation of the individual ions of the group in such a way that each may be readily identified. The cation analysis is, in general, much more systematic than was the case with the anion analysis, in that only one portion of the substance is used for the identification of nearly all of its metallic components, instead of employing a different portion for each test. 

Consequently reasonable skill in manipulation must be developed in order to obtain satisfactory results. 

Many different systems have been devised for grouping the metal ions, and a satisfactory one for beginners divides these ions into the following groups:

The Silver Group (Group I) 

Those metal ions whose chlorides are insoluble in water and in dilute acids.

Silver Ion; Mercurous Ion; Lead Ion 

Group Reagent: Hydrochloric Acid The Copper Group (Group II) 

Those metal ions which form soluble chlorides, but whose sulfides are precipitated by hydrogen sulfide from dilute acid solution and remain undissolved by ammonium polysulfide. 

Cupric Ion; Mercuric Ion; Lead Ion; Bismuth Ion; Cadmium Ion 

Group Reagent: Hydrogen Sulfide, in acid solution 23

THE SYSTEMATIC CATION ANALYSIS The Tin Group (Group III) 

Those metal ions which form soluble chlorides, but whose sulfides are precipitated by hydrogen sulfide from dilute acid solution and are soluble in ammonium polysulfide. 

Stannous Ion; Stannic Ion; Arsenious Ion; Antimony Ion 

Group Reagent: Hydrogen Sulfide, in acid solution (This group is dissolved by ammonium polysulfide) It should be noted that both the Copper Group and the Tin Group will be precipitated at the same time, and subsequently separated by ammonium polysulfide. 

The Iron Group (Group IV) 

Those metal ions which are not precipitated by the reagents used for the preceding groups, but whose hydroxides may be precipitated by ammonium hydroxide in the presence of ammonium chloride.

Ferrous Ion; Ferric Ion; Aluminum Ion; Chromic Ion 

Group Reagent: Ammonium Hydroxide, in the presence of ammonium chloride

The Nickel Group (Group V) 

Those metal ions not precipitated under the conditions employed for the preceding groups, and whose sulfides are completely precipitated only from alkaline solution. 

Nickelous Ion; Cobaltous Ion; Zinc Ion; Manganous Ion 

Group Reagent: Hydrogen Sulfide, in ammoniacal solution

The Calcium Group (Group VI) 

Those metal ions whose carbonates may be precipitated by ammonium carbonate in the presence of ammonium chloride. 

The chlorides, sulfides, and hydroxides of this group are sufficiently soluble so as not to have precipitated with earlier groups. 

Calcium Ion; Strontium Ion; Barium Ion 

Group Reagent: Ammonium Carbonate, in the presence of ammonium chloride

THE SYSTEMATIC CATION ANALYSIS The Alkali Group (Group VII) 

Those metal ions not precipitated in any of the preceding groups under the conditions employed. 

Sodium Ion; Potassium Ion; Lithium Ion; Magnesium Ion; 

Ammonium Ion

No Group Reagent 

During any systematic analysis, each of these groups must be removed in the above order, since the successful analysis of any of the groups requires the complete absence of ions belonging in the preceding groups. However, the analysis of any group may be studied as a separate entity and, since it seems a simpler approach, we shall study the groups in just the reverse of the order given above. Later, when the systematic analysis for all the cations is undertaken, the groups must be precipitated in the order presented above. Successful precipitation of a particular group requires the absence of cations of the preceding groups. 

Within a given group, however, the method of analysis will remain unchanged.

THE ALKALI GROUP (GROUP VII) 

Na+; K+; Li+; Mg++; NH†

PRELIMINARY EXPERIMENTS 

Sodium Ion, Na+ 1. Flame coloration. Clean a platinum wire by dipping it in concentrated hydrochloric acid and holding it in the flame of the Bunsen burner until no color is imparted to the flame. Dip the tip of the wire into a portion of the test solution of Na+ and introduce it into the lower, outside part of the Bunsen flame. The yellow flame is an extremely delicate test for sodium ion. During analysis, unless the color is intense and persists for several seconds, it should be considered as due to a contamination of the solution by sodium ion. 

Sodium forms no insoluble compounds suitable for tests in a beginning course.

Potassium Ion, K+ 

1. Precipitation by sodium cobaltinitrite. To 2 cc. of the test solution of K+ add a few drops of a solution of sodium cobaltinitrite-Na3Co(NO2)6. Avoid a large excess of the reagent, for this colors the solution and masks the appearance of the precipitate. What is the color of the precipitate-K₂NaCo(NO2)6?

Dilute 1 cc. of the test solution of K+ with 15 cc. of water and try to obtain the precipitate from this diluted solution. Recall this result when you apply this test for potassium ion later on in the systematic analysis. 

Lithium Ion, Li+ 

1. Flame test. Try a flame test with the test solution of Li+ in the same manner as for sodium ion. Result? This is usually a sufficient identification of the presence of lithium ion. 

Mix 3 cc. of the test solution of Li+ with 3 cc. of the test solution of Nat. Can you get a distinct flame test for lithium ion under these conditions? 26 26

THE ALKALI GROUP 

2. Separation of Lithium Ion from the Ions of Sodium and 

Potassium. In a small pyrex beaker evaporate just to dryness a mixture of 3 cc. each of test solutions of Na+, K+, and Li+. When the residue is cool, add 8 cc. of amyl alcohol. Stir the mixture for some time, and finally decant the alcohol through a small dry filter into a porcelain dish. Ignite and burn off the alcohol in the hood, dissolve the residue in the casserole in 1 cc. of water, and try a flame test with this solution. Which ion has been extracted by the alcohol?

Dissolve the residue remaining on the filter paper in 2 cc. of water and test for potassium ion by adding sodium cobaltinitrite solution. Result? What other ion remained with the potassium? 

Ammonium Ion, NH 

1. Reaction with sodium cobaltinitrite. To 3 cc. of the test solution of NH add a few drops of sodium cobaltinitrite solution. 

The yellow precipitate is ammonium cobaltinitrite. This precipitate cannot be distinguished in appearance from the corresponding potassium compound. Hence ammonium salts must be removed before testing for potassium ion. 

2. Removal of ammonium salts. In a pyrex test tube, evaporate 

3 cc. of the test solution of NH to dryness and heat the residue strongly for a few minutes. Note, and account for, the sublimate which collects on the cooler portions of the tube. 

Now take a fresh portion of the test solution of NH‡, add 5 cc. of concentrated nitric acid, then evaporate and ignite as before. Is there any residue? Would a single evaporation with nitric acid completely free the solution of ammonium ion? 

3. Reaction with strong bases. Place 5 cc. of the test solution of 

NH+ in a test tube and drop in a very small piece of solid sodium hydroxide. Warm the solution and hold above it a strip of wet red litmus wrapped around a glass rod. Do not let the paper touch the solution or the wall of the test tube. Describe the result. 

4. Test for Ammonium Ion. Place a gram of solid calcium hydroxide in the smallest beaker. Pour over this 2 cc. of the test solution of NH+. Attach a moist red litmus paper to the under side of a watch glass, set this over the beaker for a few minutes, and again note the effect on the litmus. If much ammonium salt is

THE ALKALI GROUP 

present, its presence may be detected by the odor of the evolved gas. Try this. Indicate the reactions on which this test depends. 

Magnesium Ion, Mg++ 1. Action of soluble bases. To 3 cc. of the test solution of Mg++, add 1 cc. of sodium hydroxide reagent; to a second 3 cc. portion add an equal volume of ammonium hydroxide. What is the precipitate in each case? By reference to the text, compare the solubility of magnesium hydroxide with that of calcium hydroxide and of aluminum hydroxide. 

2. Effect of ammonium salts on the precipitation of magnesium hydroxide. To 3 cc. of the test solution of Mg++ add an equal volume of ammonium chloride reagent, then 5 cc. of ammonium hydroxide. Compare the result with that obtained by the use of ammonium hydroxide alone, and account for the difference. 

Add sodium chloride solution to a fresh portion of the test solution of Mg++, and then add sodium hydroxide solution. Why did not the sodium chloride produce the same effect as the ammonium chloride in the previous experiment?

Why does magnesium ion not appear in the Iron Group? (Refer to p. 24 for the reagents used to precipitate the Iron Group.) 

3. Magnesium carbonate. To 3 cc. of the test solution of Mg++ add an equal volume of sodium carbonate reagent. On a similar portion try the effect of ammonium chloride and ammonium carbonate reagents. Now explain why magnesium ion does not precipitate in the Calcium Group. 

4. Hydrolysis of magnesium salts. In a small pyrex beaker evaporate 10 cc. of the test solution of Mg++ to complete dryness. 

Hold moist blue litmus in the last of the escaping vapors. Cool the beaker and test the solubility of the residue in (a) water and (b) a few drops of concentrated hydrochloric acid. Explain what has happened. This behavior of magnesium salts is very troublesome during any ignition. 

5. Test for Magnesium Ion. To 1 cc. of the test solution of 

Mg++ add 2 cc. of ammonium chloride reagent. Filter into this 

1 cc. of a solution of a phosphate-such as microcosmic salt, and then make the mixture quite strongly alkaline with ammonium hydroxide. The white precipitate is magnesium ammonium phosphate (MgNHPO4). Write an equation for its formation.

THE ALKALI GROUP SYSTEMATIC ANALYSIS OF THE ALKALI GROUP 

Prepare a practice solution by mixing about 8 cc. of test solutions of each of the ions, Mg++, NH, K+, Na+, and Li+. Lithium is an uncommon element, and rarely encountered in ordinary work. 

1. Test for Ammonium Ion. This is usually the first test carried out, because the presence of this ion makes it impossible to test for potassium ion until the ammonium salts have been removed. 

Likewise, lithium ion cannot be effectively separated unless ammonium compounds have been removed. Using about 5 cc. of your practice solution, carry out the test as directed in the preliminary experiments. Unless a distinct color change of the litmus is observed within one minute, this ion should not be reported. 

Any test obtained after a longer interval than one minute is probably due to contamination or to vapors of ammonia from your neighbor's desk. 

2. Test for Magnesium Ion. Using about 10 cc. of your practice solution, carry out the test as directed in the preliminary experiments. Magnesium ammonium phosphate is frequently slow in forming. A negative test should not be reported until you have waited twenty minutes. 

3. Tests for the Ions of Sodium and Potassium. To the remainder of your solution add 5 cc. of concentrated nitric acid and evaporate to dryness. Heat the residue to low redness for a minute or two, using your smallest casserole (or beaker), and being careful to heat the entire casserole so that the last traces of ammonium salts may be removed. What test may be spoiled if this is not done? The residue of the alkali salts usually melts, and is then rather slow to dissolve in the next step. If lithium ion is to be identified, extract this residue with amyl alcohol, as directed in the preliminary experiments, allowing plenty of time for solution to occur. If lithium ion is not to be tested for, omit the amyl alcohol treatment and, instead, take up the residue in not over 5 cc. of water to which one drop of hydrochloric acid has been added. Try the flame test for sodium ion and, following that, add sodium cobaltinitrite solution to identify potassium ion.

THE CALCIUM GROUP (GROUP VI) 

Ca++; Sr++; Ba++

Group Reagent: Ammonium Carbonate, in the presence of ammonium chloride

The ions of barium, strontium, and calcium-all members of the same family of the periodic table-form a very closely related analytical group. Since the properties of strontium compounds are usually intermediate between those of barium and calcium, there is some difficulty in obtaining sharp separations. A study of the following reactions will bring out the similarity of the relations in this group, and at the same time point out a means of identifying each ion. 

PRELIMINARY EXPERIMENTS 

1. Carbonates. Take 5 cc. portions of test solutions of each of the three ions. To each add 5 cc. of ammonium chloride reagent, and make alkaline with a drop or two of ammonium hydroxide solution.
