Now add to each solution 1 cc. of ammonium carbonate reagent and heat to boiling for a moment to coagulate the precipitate. What is the precipitate in each case? Save all three. Refer to the previous group and state how magnesium ion would behave under these conditions. Give a reason for the use of ammonium chloride in precipitating the group. What is the function of the ammonium hydroxide? Note that the reaction with ammonium carbonate serves as a means of separating this group from the Alkali Group (Group VII), which remains in solution; but we still have to separate the ions of this group from each other. 

2. Chromates. Decant the liquid from the three carbonate precipitates obtained above, and dissolve the residue by warming with 3 cc. of acetic acid. To each solution add 5 cc. of sodium acetate reagent and a few drops of potassium chromate solution. 

Note which of the three ions yields a precipitate under these conditions. What is the color of this precipitate? Explain the function of the sodium acetate. 

3. Sulfates. To 3 cc. portions of test solutions of each of the three ions, add 1 cc. of ammonium sulfate reagent. Which of the 30

THE CALCIUM GROUP 

sulfates appears least soluble? Check your answer by the following experiment.

To 10 cc. of saturated calcium sulfate solution add strontium chloride solution, drop by drop, until precipitation is complete. 

What is the precipitate? Filter, and to the filtrate add a few drops of barium chloride reagent. Result? Arrange these three sulfates in the order of increasing solubility. 

4. Oxalates. Dilute 3 cc. of test solutions of each of the three ions with 15 cc. of water, and add 5 cc. of dilute acetic acid. Now add to each solution a little ammonium oxalate reagent. From the results of these experiments, state which of the three ions forms the least soluble oxalate. Which ion would be likely to interfere in the test for calcium ion with this reagent? 

Now fill out the following table, stating for each compound its relative solubility in (a) water, and (b) any acid you applied. 

CARBONATE 

Calcium Strontium Barium

CHROMATE SULFATE OXALATE SYSTEMATIC ANALYSIS OF THE CALCIUM GROUP 

Note: During a complete cation analysis, this group would be sought for in the filtrate from the Nickel Group (Group V). Before the carbonates of calcium, strontium, and barium can be brought down by the ammonium carbonate group reagent, it is necessary to remove the ammonium sulfide employed for the precipitation of the Nickel Group, then to evaporate the solution to dryness and heat the residue to volatilize most of the ammonium salts which have accumulated during the analysis. 

A study of the table just prepared will show that the barium ion can be precipitated as the chromate without interference from the other ions, provided the proper conditions are obtained. Then the strontium ion may be precipitated as the sulfate, without interference from the calcium ion under the conditions selected. 

Finally, the presence of calcium ion can be proved by forming the oxalate. Since barium ion also forms an insoluble sulfate, and strontium ion an insoluble oxalate, it should be obvious that these

THE CALCIUM GROUP 

separations must be made in the proper order; i.e., barium first, and calcium last.

Prepare a practice solution by taking 10 cc. of test solutions of each of the three ions of this group. (Or dilute 10 cc. of an unknown solution to a volume of 25 cc.) This group should always be precipitated from fairly dilute solution to lessen the chances of precipitating any magnesium or lithium ion from the next analytical group. The precipitation of this group is best carried out in an Erlenmeyer flask of appropriate size. 

1. The group precipitation. Make the solution slightly alkaline with ammonium hydroxide and add 8 cc. of ammonium chloride reagent. Heat nearly to boiling, and add ammonium carbonate reagent in small portions, with stirring, until precipitation appears complete. Avoid too great an excess of this reagent. When time permits, the flask containing the precipitate should be loosely stoppered and allowed to stand in a hot (not boiling) water bath for thirty minutes. Decant the liquid through a filter, test the filtrate for complete precipitation, and wash the flask once with hot water, pouring the washings through the filter. 

2. Identification and separation of the Barium Ion. Warm 10 cc. of acetic acid in the flask just used for the group precipitation, and pour the warm acid over the precipitate on the filter paper. 

Usually it is necessary to pour the acid repeatedly through the filter to effect complete solution. Do not use additional acid. 

Dilute with water to a volume of 30 cc. Take 3 cc. of this acetate solution, and make a side test for barium ion by adding an equal volume of sodium acetate solution and a few drops of potassium chromate reagent. A yellow precipitate of barium chromate indicates the presence of barium ion. If barium ion is absent, go directly to section 4.

If barium ion is found in this side test, heat the remainder of the solution to boiling, add 10 cc. of sodium acetate solution, then potassium chromate reagent until the solution becomes colored, indicating an excess of the chromate. Filter, making sure that none of the precipitate runs through the filter paper. (If the filtrate is turbid, heat to boiling for five minutes and filter again on the same paper.) 

3. Reprecipitation of the Ions of Strontium and Calcium. If barium ion was not present, this step is not carried out; but if chromate had to be added to separate barium ion, the excess of

THE CALCIUM GROUP 

this reagent would interfere with subsequent tests and, consequently, must be removed.

To the yellow filtrate from the barium chromate separation add sodium hydroxide solution until alkaline, heat nearly to boiling, and precipitate with ammonium or sodium carbonate. The precipitate is calcium or strontium carbonate (or both), and is actually white. Filter, and wash with hot water until the filtrate runs through colorless. Then dissolve the precipitate in not more than 10 cc. of hot acetic acid and dilute to 30 cc. 

4. Test for the Strontium Ion. To this acetate solution (or, if barium ion was absent, to the solution from section 2) add 8 cc. of ammonium sulfate reagent, warm slightly, and let stand for five minutes. A white precipitate-strontium sulfate-indicates the presence of strontium ion in the unknown. This precipitate is often quite slow in forming. Although it is quite possible for calcium ion to precipitate as the sulfate at this stage, this could happen only if relatively very large concentrations of this ion are present a condition not likely to be encountered in beginning work. 

5. Identification of Calcium Ion. Filter out the precipitate of strontium sulfate, if obtained, and to the clear filtrate add 5 cc. of ammonium oxalate solution. The presence of calcium ion is indicated by the formation of a white precipitate-calcium oxalate. 

The quantity of this oxalate precipitate should always be noted, and a very slight turbidity viewed with suspicion, as it is very easy to introduce a trace of calcium ion from the glass in which the reagents are kept.

REVIEW OF THE CALCIUM GROUP (GROUP VI) 

1. In separating barium ion from the ions of strontium and calcium, explain: (a) the purpose of having the solution acid; (b) the harm of having too high a concentration of acid; (c) the purpose of the sodium acetate. 

2. Why is the original group precipitate (the carbonates of calcium, barium, and strontium) dissolved in acetic acid instead of in hydrochloric acid? 

3. A solution contains Ca++ and Sr++. If both CrO and CO are added, in equal amounts, will the precipitate consist of the carbonates or the chromates of calcium and strontium? Explain your answer, referring to your text for the necessary solubility product data. 

4. Explain what may happen if calcium carbonate is boiled for some time with a strong solution of ammonium chloride. What bearing has this on the regular analytical procedure?

(GROUP V) 

Ni++; Co++; Mn++; Zn++ 

Group Reagent: Hydrogen Sulfide, in alkaline solution

PRELIMINARY EXPERIMENTS 

1. Nature of the hydroxides. (a) To 2 cc. of the test solution of each ion of this group add sodium hydroxide reagent a few drops at a time, with shaking, until a definite excess is present. Record the colors of the precipitates formed. In case any of the precipitates dissolve in excess sodium hydroxide solution, record the fact and explain. Note, and account for, the slow darkening in the case of manganese. (b) To 2 cc. of the test solution of each ion add ammonium hydroxide, a few drops at a time, until a considerable excess is present. Compare the effects of ammonium hydroxide with sodium hydroxide and explain the difference. (c) To 2 cc. of a test solution of Mn++ add an equal volume of ammonium chloride reagent, and make alkaline with ammonium hydroxide. Compare with the action of ammonium hydroxide alone, and explain. What other ion behaves in a manner similar to manganous ion? 

On the basis of these experiments, what reasons can you give for the failure of the ions of this group to precipitate in the Iron 

Group (Group IV)? 

2. Nature of the sulfides. (a) In separate test tubes obtain 3 cc. of the test solution of each of the four ions of this group. Acidify each with a little dilute hydrochloric acid, and pass hydrogen sulfide into each solution for five seconds. Note whether any of the ions form precipitates under these conditions. (b) Obtain a fresh 3 cc. portion of the test solution of each ion, acidify each with a few drops of acetic acid, and again treat with hydrogen sulfide. Note which ion yields a precipitate in this feebly acid solution. What is the color of this precipitate? This reaction is applied later during the systematic analysis. (c) To a fresh 3 cc. portion of the test solution of each ion add 

1 cc. of ammonium hydroxide, and then pass in hydrogen sulfide 34

for five seconds. Note that precipitates are now obtained in all cases, and that zinc sulfide is the only white sulfide. Which of the sulfides appear black? (d) Allow each precipitate to settle, decant the liquid as well as possible, and to each residue add 3 cc. of dilute hydrochloric acid. 

Observe which sulfides dissolve appreciably within a short time. 

This apparent difference in solubility is applied in subdividing the group during analysis. The difference is actually in the rate of solution, and is apparently due largely to a difference in the state of subdivision of the sulfide precipitate. All would dissolve if sufficient time is allowed. 

Nickelous Ion, Ni++ 1. Oxidation of the ion. Make 3 cc. of the test solution of Ni++ alkaline with sodium hydroxide solution and add bromine water. 

The black precipitate is nickelic hydroxide, Ni(OH)3. Represent the reaction by an equation. 

2. Test reaction. Obtain two drops of the test solution of Ni++, dilute with 5 cc. of water, and add 1 cc. of dimethylglyoxime. The scarlet precipitate is an exceptionally delicate test for nickel. The equation is:

CH-C-N—OH 2 +Ni+++2NH,→ CH-C-N-OH CH-C-N-OH -O-N-C-CH3 Ni +2NH+.

CH3-C-N—O HO-N-C-CH3 (Nickel dimethylglyoxime)

Compare the result with that obtained when cobaltous ion is treated in the next section with this same reagent. 

Cobaltous Ion, Co++ 1. Oxidation of the ion. Try the effect of sodium hydroxide and bromine water on 3 cc. of the test solution of this ion in the same way as was done for nickelous ion. Is the result the same in this case? 

2. Effect of dimethylglyoxime. Using 1 cc. of test solution, try the dimethylglyoxime reaction as with nickel. Note that while no precipitate is obtained, the solution may turn dark. If this occurs

when testing for nickelous ion, a few more drops of the reagent must be added. 

3. Borax bead test. Bring a gram of powdered borax to your desk. Make a loop, about 2 mm. in diameter, on the end of a platinum wire. Heat this red hot and, while hot, plunge it into the borax so that some will adhere to the loop. Heat again, take up more borax if needed, and repeat the heating until a thin clear bead results in the loop. Cool and make sure that the bead is colorless. 

Prepare a little cobalt sulfide, place a small amount of the sulfide on the bead, and heat again in the hottest (oxidizing) part of the Bunsen flame. Use the full flame and allow plenty of time for the reaction to occur. Note the deep blue color of the bead after cooling. The following changes may be considered to take place: 2CoS +302 → 2CoO +2SO₂ ↑ ; 

Borax (Na2B4O7) may be written as (NaBO2)2 B203; (NaBO2)2 B2O3 + CoO→ Co(BO2)2 + 2NaBO2. (Cobalt metaborate)

Manganous Ion, Mn++ Recall from an earlier section the slow darkening of manganous hydroxide in air. The ease with which low valence manganese compounds oxidize is characteristic, and is taken advantage of in several tests for this ion. 

1. Sodium carbonate bead test. Mix thoroughly about a gram each of sodium carbonate and sodium nitrate. Bring a small amount of this mixture on to the loop of a platinum wire as you did in forming the borax bead, but heat only long enough to sinter the mass. Prepare a small quantity of manganous hydroxide, touch the bead to this, and heat for a few seconds in the Bunsen flame. 

Avoid heating this bead more than a few seconds. On cooling, a blue-green color (shade and intensity vary) will develop in the bead, affording a very delicate test for manganese. The valence number of this element has been raised from 2+ to 6+. Write an equation for the reaction. What is the purpose of the sodium carbonate? 

Zinc Ion, Zn++ 1. Refer to the preceding sections and record here the following information concerning zinc ion:

(a) Nature of the hydroxide; (b) Color of the sulfide; (c) Solubility of the sulfide in dilute hydrochloric acid; (d) Ability of the sulfide to precipitate from a solution acid with acetic acid.

The separation and identification of zinc ion during analysis is based on the chemical behavior which you summarize above. 

SYSTEMATIC ANALYSIS OF THE NICKEL GROUP 

Note: During the complete cation analysis, this group would be sought for in the filtrate from the Iron Group (Group IV). This filtrate would already be alkaline with ammonium hydroxide and would contain ammonium chloride. The Nickel Group (Group V) 

may, therefore, be precipitated by passing hydrogen sulfide into this filtrate without any other treatment. During the general analysis this should be done at once, else manganese will be oxidized to some extent by oxygen from the air, and later tests will be rendered unsatisfactory.

For analysis the four sulfides, obtained by passing hydrogen sulfide into the alkaline solution, are subdivided by taking advantage of the fact that two (MnS, ZnS) dissolve readily in dilute hydrochloric acid. From this solution manganous ion is precipitated as the hydroxide, while zinc ion remains in solution due to the amphoterism of its hydroxide. The borax bead test identifies cobalt in the residue from the hydrochloric acid extraction. After dissolving this residue in aqua regia, nickelous ion may be identified by its reaction with dimethylglyoxime. 

Prepare a practice solution containing about 8 cc. of the test solution of each ion. If an unknown is being analyzed, a 10 cc. portion should be diluted to 40 cc. 

1. Precipitation of the group. In working with hydrogen sulfide, every effort should be made to keep the gas out of the room, for it is not merely unpleasant, but poisonous. To carry out a precipitation with hydrogen sulfide, select an Erlenmeyer flask of suitable size and fit it with a one-hole rubber stopper. Pass through the stopper a glass delivery tube, slightly drawn out at the lower end and arranged so as not to enter the solution when the stopper is inserted in the neck of the flask (Fig. 2).

Place the practice solution (or diluted unknown) in the Erlenmeyer flask. Add 10 cc. of ammonium chloride reagent, and heat to

boiling. Add ammonium hydroxide to alkalinity (use litmus), but avoid a large excess. Now, in the hood, attach the rubber delivery tube of a hydrogen sulfide generator to the glass delivery tube of your flask. Keeping the stopper loose, turn on the gas slowly, allowing it to run five seconds to displace the air in the flask. Then push the stopper down to close the flask.

Agitate the contents of the flask until precipitation is complete, usually in one and a half minutes. Let the precipitate settle, filter on a carefully made paper, and wash with hot water. The filtrate is tested for complete precipitation. 

2. Subdivision of the group. Prepare a well running filter. Wash the sulfide precipitate into a beaker by means of less than 15 cc. of cold water. Prepare about 20 cc. of 3 N hydrochloric acid by mixing 10 cc. of the 6 N acid with 10 cc. of water. Pour into the suspended sulfides an equal volume of this 3 N acid, stir for fifteen seconds, and filter on the previously prepared filter paper. The black residue will contain the sulfides of nickel and cobalt, while the filtrate contains manganese and zinc as their chlorides. The separation is not perfect, being based on a time reaction, but it is adequate.

FIG. 2.-Precipi tation with Hydrogen Sulfide. 

3. Identification of Cobalt Ion. Test the black sulfide on the filter paper with a borax bead. Be sure to heat the bead for a considerable time in the upper (oxidizing) flame of the Bunsen burner. A blue bead shows the presence of cobalt. In the absence of this ion, the bead may appear brownish because of the presence of nickel. This color, however, does not constitute a satisfactory test for nickel, and cannot be seen at all in the presence of cobalt. 

4. Identification of Nickel Ion. Dissolve the remaining sulfides in 5 cc. of warm aqua regia (prepared by mixing one volume of concentrated nitric acid with three volumes of concentrated hy- ¹ At times a very persistent colloid of nickel sulfide forms, which passes through the filter. This may usually be corrected by carefully making the filtrate just acid with acetic acid, warming for a moment, and filtering through a fresh filter.

drochloric acid).

The reaction may be considered to occur as follows: 

3HCl + HNO3 → Cl2 + NOC1 + 2H₂O; NiS + Cl2 Ni++ + 2Cl− +S↓. 

Indicate the electron changes which occur in the above reactions. 

What happens to the cobalt sulfide? Evaporate (hood) the resulting solution just to dryness to remove the excess of acid. Try to account for the color changes during this evaporation. If the residue is black, it has been overheated. In this case, dissolve it in a few drops of concentrated hydrochloric acid and evaporate more carefully. Take up this residue in 8 cc. of water, add ammonium hydroxide to some excess, and filter if not clear. Test the clear filtrate for nickel ion by adding 1 cc. of dimethylglyoxime. 

What result is obtained if nickel ion is present? How would the solution appear if only the cobalt ion is present? (Consult the preliminary experiments.) 

5. Separation and identification of Manganous Ion. The hydrochloric acid filtrate from the separation in (2) is boiled down to about 8 cc. to drive out hydrogen sulfide and to remove some of the excess acid. Now neutralize with sodium hydroxide solution and add about 5 cc. in excess. Filter. The precipitate will contain manganese, if present, as the hydroxide, which usually darkens in air; but obtaining a precipitate here does not prove the presence of manganous ion. (What else can appear here?) Use the sodium carbonate-sodium nitrate bead to make the final test for manganese, as described in the preliminary experiments. 

6. Identification of Zinc Ion. The filtrate from (5) contains any zinc as zincate ion. Place a small piece of litmus in this solution and acidify with acetic acid. Note that sodium acetate is formed during the neutralization. This makes it unlikely that too much acetic acid will be used to prevent the next reaction. Explain why. 

Now pass hydrogen sulfide into the solution for a few seconds. 

A white precipitate, appearing almost at once, is zinc sulfide, proving the presence of zinc ion. 

REVIEW OF THE NICKEL GROUP (GROUP V) 

1. Which ions of this group form complexes with ammonia? Give a typical formula for such a complex ion. Which complex ion has a distinctive color? 

2. Which ions of this group can be oxidized to a higher valence? What kind of medium is essential in order to carry out this oxidation? Why?

3. Explain what harm results from a failure to remove hydrogen sulfide from the solution before adding sodium hydroxide to precipitate manganous hydroxide. 

4. A solution contains (only) the ions: Fe+++, Mn++, Ni++, Zn++, Co++. 

Show by balanced equations the effect of adding to this solution, separately, the following reagents. (Underline all precipitates.) (a) Sodium hydroxide to excess. (b) Excess ammonium hydroxide. (c) Hydrogen sulfide, after previously acidifying with hydrochloric acid. 

5. During analysis of a solution for the Nickel Group, the following observations are made: (a) Hydrogen sulfide produced a black precipitate. (b) This precipitate did not entirely dissolve when extracted with 1.5 N hydrochloric acid. (c) The hydrochloric acid extract just obtained yields a precipitate with sodium hydroxide, and this precipitate darkens on standing. 

Considering these reactions as a whole, state which ions of the Nickel Group are shown with reasonable certainty to be present, and which (if any) are proved absent. State the reasons for your conclusions. 

6. Show by equations how to examine properly the black residue from (b) of the preceding question. 

7. The separation of Mn++ and Zn++ by dilute hydrochloric acid, is not sharp, and some Ni++ and Co++ nearly always dissolve. Show what happens to the Ni++ and Co++ during the subsequent procedure. Do they interfere?

(GROUP IV) 

Fe++; Fe+++; Al+++; Cr+++ 

Group Reagent: Ammonium Hydroxide, in the presence of ammonium chloride

This group and the Nickel Group, which you have just completed, are frequently brought down as a single large group by the use of ammonium hydroxide and ammonium sulfide at the same time. This large group is then split into subdivisions and analyzed by methods not very different from those which we shall apply to the individual groups. Although combining the two groups has certain analytical advantages, the procedure is less cumbersome when the groups are considered as separate entities. 

The student should note here that neither of these methods of analysis is applicable in the presence of phosphates (and certain other anions). Special procedure for such cases will be given later. 

PRELIMINARY EXPERIMENTS 

1. Character of the hydroxides. To 3 cc. portions of test solutions of Fe++, Fe+++, Al+++, and Cr+++ add ammonium hydroxide, dropwise, with shaking, until definitely in excess. 

Record your observations, and compare the behavior of these ions with that shown by the ions of the Nickel Group under similar conditions. 

Using fresh portions of the test solutions of these four ions, add to each an excess of sodium hydroxide solution, and again compare the behavior of these ions with that of the ions in the Nickel Group. For any case in which the precipitate dissolved in an excess of the base, add hydrochloric acid, dropwise, to the solution and note the result. 

2. Hydrolysis of salts. (a) Note the effect of the test solutions of Fe+++, Al+++, and Cr+++ on litmus paper, and explain your observations. Evaporate 5 cc. of the test solution of Fe+++ to dryness in a small casserole. Hold wet blue litmus paper in the vapors as the solution approaches dryness, and account for the result. Try to dissolve the residue, first in water, then in a little concentrated hydrochloric acid. What is the residue? What ef- 41

fect does heat have on the hydrolysis of a ferric salt? Would you expect the test solutions of Al+++ and Cr+++ to exhibit similar behavior? (b) Prepare sodium metaluminate, NaAlO2, and sodium metachromite, NaCrO2, from 5 cc. of the test solution of each of the appropriate ions. Heat each solution to boiling; note and explain the difference. This difference is important later in effecting the separation of chromic ion from aluminum ion. (c) In a large test tube place 3 cc. of the test solution of Fe+++ and add 5 cc. of sodium acetate solution. Does any precipitate form? Now add 10 cc. of water and heat to boiling for a moment. 

The precipitate is basic ferric acetate, Fe(OH)2C2H3O2. Why did it form when the diluted solution was heated? 

Ferrous Ion, Fe++; Ferric Ion, Fe+++ 

1. Tests for Ferrous and Ferric Ions. To 1 cc. of a test solution of Fe+++ add potassium ferrocyanide solution; to a second portion add a solution of potassium thiocyanate. Describe the results, noting carefully whether precipitates are obtained, and represent each reaction by an equation. These reactions afford very delicate tests for iron in the ferric condition.

Now try the effect of either of these reagents on the test solution of Fe++. What impurity is present in this solution? Account for its presence.

Prepare a solution of potassium ferricyanide by dissolving a tiny crystal of the solid in 5 cc. of water. Add this reagent to a small portion of the test solution of Fe++. Describe the result and express the reaction by an equation. Potassium ferricyanide does not give a blue precipitate with Fe+++, and hence affords a test for iron in the ferrous condition. 

2. Oxidation of ferrous compounds. To 3 cc. of a test solution of Fe++ add 3 cc. of concentrated nitric acid and boil for several minutes. Dilute to 20 cc. and try to obtain a test for Fe++. Write an equation to express the effect of nitric acid. What reagents other than nitric acid will accomplish a similar result? 

3. Reduction of ferric compounds. (a) Warm 5 cc. of a test solution of Fe+++ for several minutes with a small iron nail, and a second portion with a small piece of aluminum. Test each portion for the presence of ferrous ion and, if found, write equations to account for its formation.

(b) Pass hydrogen sulfide for a few seconds into 5 cc. of a test solution of Fe+++. You will observe a white precipitate. What is it? Now make the solution alkaline with ammonium hydroxide. 

The black precipitate is ferrous sulfide. Account for the formation of this sulfide, recalling that you had ferric ion originally, and explain why the sulfide did not precipitate until the solution was made alkaline.

Chromic Ion, Cr+++ 1. Oxidation of Chromic Ion. (a) To 3 cc. of a test solution of 

Cr+++ add sodium hydroxide solution until the original precipitate just dissolves. Now add 8 cc. of sodium hypochlorite solution and boil until the solution becomes yellow, indicating the presence of chromate ion. Express these reactions by equations. (b) In a small beaker place 5 cc. of a test solution of Cr+++, add excess sodium hydroxide reagent, and then slowly stir in about 0.5 g. of sodium peroxide. Heat to boiling and account for the changes. The reaction is as follows. 2NaCrO2 + 3Na2O2 + 2H2O → 2Na2CrO4 + 4NaOH. 

Make a portion of this yellow solution acid with acetic acid and add a few drops of lead acetate solution. What is the yellow precipitate? 

2. Reduction of hexavalent chromium. To 2 cc. of a solution of potassium dichromate add an equal volume of concentrated hydrochloric acid, warm, and treat with hydrogen sulfide for a short time. What is the precipitate? Note the color of the solution, first filtering if necessary, and write an equation to express the change.

SYSTEMATIC ANALYSIS OF THE IRON GROUP 

Note: During the complete cation analysis, this group would be sought for in the filtrate from the acid hydrogen sulfide groups (the Copper and Tin Groups). Before proceeding with the precipitation of the Iron Group, it is necessary to boil down this filtrate in order to remove the hydrogen sulfide. (Why must this be removed here?) Ferric ion, if originally present, will have been reduced to the ferrous state; and effective removal of the latter ion is not possible by the use of ammonium hydroxide, since ferrous hydroxide is too soluble to be precipitated completely. Ferric hydroxide, however, has a smaller solubility product. Hence the

ferrous ion is oxidized by boiling the solution, freed from hydrogen sulfide, with 10 cc. of dilute nitric acid.

If the concentration of the magnesium ion and the ions of the Nickel Group is large, some of these ions will precipitate in the Iron Group. This difficulty may be overcome by dissolving the group precipitate in dilute hydrochloric acid, diluting to 50 cc. and reprecipitating the group in the usual manner. The two filtrates are then combined for subsequent analysis. 

A study of the preliminary experiments will indicate that aluminum ion may be dissolved out of the precipitated hydroxides by hot sodium hydroxide reagent. The residue is then treated with sodium peroxide, thereby oxidizing trivalent chromium to the chromate ion, which is soluble, and usually recognized by its color. Ferric hydroxide remains as an insoluble residue. 

The valence of iron is determined by applying the tests to separate portions of the original unknown. Remember, when carrying out these tests, that many ions form precipitates with potassium ferrocyanide-many of them colored-but only ferric ion forms a deep blue precipitate. This is especially significant when a general unknown is being tested for iron. 

Prepare a practice solution containing 10 cc. of each of the test solutions of Fe++, Al+++, Cr+++. Add 10 cc. of dilute nitric acid, boil for three minutes, and dilute to 50 cc. before continuing with the group precipitation. 

1. Precipitation of the group. Although ammonium salts must be present (why?), none need be added, since the neutralization of the excess nitric acid will produce a sufficient amount. After oxidizing the iron as directed, heat the diluted solution to boiling and add ammonium hydroxide, a little at a time, with stirring, until the odor of the hot solution indicates that a distinct excess of the reagent is present. (Why does the Nickel Group not precipitate under these conditions?) Filter the hot solution at once, and wash the precipitate with hot water. 

2. Separation and identification of Aluminum Ion. Scrape the washed precipitate of the hydroxides into a large pyrex test tube. 

If necessary, a little water may be used to assist the transfer. Add 

8 cc. of sodium hydroxide solution and boil gently for a full minute. 

Filter hot through a well running filter, for if the solution cools much, the separation of chromium is incomplete and, consequently, a false indication of aluminum ion is obtained. Acidify the filtrate

with dilute hydrochloric acid (use litmus), then heat and make alkaline with ammonium hydroxide. A flocculent white precipitate of aluminum hydroxide indicates the presence of aluminum ion. Any very small particles of precipitate should not be considered a test, as traces of silicic acid from the reagents sometimes appear here. 

3. Identification of Chromic Ion. The residue from the previous extraction consists of the hydroxides of iron and chromium. Scrape or wash this precipitate into a small beaker, add 5 cc. of sodium hydroxide, and carefully stir in one gram of sodium peroxide, in small portions. Finally boil for a minute to decompose the excess peroxide, and filter the solution. A yellow filtrate indicates that trivalent chromium has been oxidized to chromate. A confirmatory test may be made as directed in the preliminary experiments, but it is rarely necessary. 

4. Determination of the valence of iron. Although the presence of ferric ion in the residue should be demonstrated by dissolving this residue in hot dilute hydrochloric acid and adding potassium ferrocyanide solution, this test would afford no indication of the original valence of iron. Why? Separate tests for each valence must be made on the original solution, and the results indicated in your report.

REVIEW OF THE IRON GROUP (GROUP IV) 

1. Explain, on the basis of solubility product: (a) why the ions of nickel, cobalt, and zinc do not precipitate with the Iron Group; (b) why the magnesium ion is not a member of the Iron Group. 

2. What is the color of each hydroxide of the Iron Group? Which did you find to be amphoteric? Are these hydroxides strong or weak bases? 

3. Indicate by balanced equations, together with a statement of conditions, how a separation in one step may be made of each of the following pairs of ions. More than one method is possible in several cases. (a) Fe+++— Al+++; (b) Al+++— Co++; (c) Cr+++— Mg++; (d) Cr+++ Fe+++. 

4. Suppose that potassium dichromate were present in an unknown received for general analysis. By means of equations show what would have happened to this compound before the Iron Group was reached. 

5. A solution contains only Fe+++, Cr+++, Zn++, Ni++. Indicate by equations the effect of adding, separately: (a) excess sodium hydroxide; (b) excess ammonium hydroxide; (c) passing hydrogen sulfide into the previously acidified solution. 

6. Give two reasons why sodium hydroxide cannot replace ammonium hydroxide in the precipitation of the Iron Group, even if sodium ion were not to be tested for later.

SEPARATION OF  IN THE PRESENCE OF PHOSPHATE. THE BASIC ACETATE PROCEDURE 

Reference to a table of solubilities will indicate that the phosphates of all the metals except the alkalies are insoluble in water. (The same is true for the salts of several other anions, most of which, however, are not very common.) These phosphates, although insoluble in water, are soluble in acids, and thus do not interfere with the precipitation of the Silver, Copper, and Tin Groups, because these groups are precipitated from an acid solution. But, when the solution is made alkaline to precipitate the 

Iron Group (Group IV), the conditions become favorable for the formation of insoluble phosphates and, if sufficient phosphate ion is present, nearly all of the ions of the Nickel and Calcium Groups will fall, as phosphates, along with the Iron Group, and no satisfactory separation can be made. A special procedure, known as the 

Basic Acetate Separation, has been devised to meet this difficulty. 

This special method takes advantage of the fact that ferric phosphate is the most insoluble of any of the phosphates which can form at this point. This phosphate is so insoluble, in fact, that it can be brought down in a feebly acid solution, and under these conditions the phosphates of the other ions cannot form. 

Hence, by the addition of an excess of ferric ion, and carefully adjusting the acidity of the solution, all of the phosphate ion can be precipitated as ferric phosphate; and the filtrate may then be tested for the remaining groups without further interference. 

The excess of ferric ion, above that required to combine with the phosphate ion, is precipitated at the same time as basic ferric acetate, from which the procedure derives its name. 

It should be noted that, since this entire procedure is carried out in acid solution, it affords an excellent means of keeping manganese from precipitating with the Iron Group. Hence this special separation is often employed even when no phosphate ion is present. 

The Analytical Procedure 

The acid filtrate from the hydrogen sulfide groups is boiled free of dissolved hydrogen sulfide, filtered free from any turbidity, 46

THE BASIC ACETATE PROCEDURE 

and any ferrous ion oxidized by the addition of nitric acid, exactly as in the regular procedure. A side test for phosphate, with ammonium molybdate reagent, gives a positive result. Make a side test for iron at this point. 

Place the solution (the volume should be about 25 cc.) in a small beaker. Add ammonium hydroxide in small portions, with constant stirring, until a very small amount of precipitate forms and remains undissolved after stirring for two minutes. (In case too much ammonium hydroxide is accidentally added, dissolve the precipitate in hydrochloric acid, and repeat the neutralization until the correct result is obtained.) Finally dissolve this trace of precipitate in a single drop of concentrated hydrochloric acid. 

The solution is now very feebly acidic. 

Transfer this solution to a beaker of at least 500 cc. capacity. 

Add 30 cc. of ammonium acetate solution, and then ferric chloride solution in 1 cc. portions, until the solution develops a deep red color. This color is caused by ferric acetate, and indicates that there is now an excess of ferric chloride in solution. Dilute the solution to 350-400 cc. with boiling water and boil for two minutes. 

Let the precipitate settle, and filter while still hot, using a fluted filter. The reactions may be summarized as follows: 

FeCl3 + (NH4)2HPO4 + NHÂC2H3O2 → FePO₁ ↓ + 3NH4Cl + HC2H3O2; Fe(C2H3O2)3 + 2HOH → Fe(OH)2C2H3O2 ↓ + 2HC2H3O2. 

This precipitate, due to the conditions employed, also contains the ions of aluminum and chromium, and is analyzed for these ions in the same way as if the group had been precipitated in the usual manner. The filtrate is boiled down and analyzed as usual for the following groups.

REVIEW OF THE BASIC ACETATE PROCEDURE 

1. If the Iron Group is precipitated by ammonium hydroxide in the presence of the phosphate ion, of what may the precipitate consist? What cations will be in the filtrate? 

2. Explain the purpose of the ferric chloride added in the Basic Acetate procedure. 

3. Give two reasons for the relatively large amount of ammonium acetate used in this separation. 

4. Why is the precipitation of basic ferric acetate carried out in a hot, dilute solution?

THE ACID HYDROGEN SULFIDE GROUPS 

 (GROUP II). Cu++; Hg++;

Pb++; Bi+++; Cd++ 

 (GROUP III). Sn++; Sn++++; Sb+++; As+++ 

Group Reagent: Hydrogen Sulfide, in acid solution

The cations which fall in these groups are those whose sulfides are precipitated by hydrogen sulfide from a slightly acid solution. 

Since the conditions for the precipitation of the two groups are identical, they precipitate together, and are then separated by the action of ammonium polysulfide. The sulfides of the Tin 

Group dissolve in this reagent; those of the Copper Group do not. 

PRELIMINARY EXPERIMENTS INVOLVING BOTH GROUPS 

1. The precipitation of the groups. In separate labelled test tubes place 5 cc. of the test solution of each ion of the two groups. To each add 5 drops of dilute hydrochloric acid and dilute to 10 cc. with hot water. If a precipitate forms on diluting any solution do not be concerned at present; an explanation can be found later. 

Pass hydrogen sulfide into each test tube for a few seconds, keeping the contents of the tube well agitated; note, and record, the color of each sulfide. In one or two cases the color of the precipitate may change during the operation, because of the intermediate formation of double salts. In such cases record the final color. 

If any of the sulfides form colloidal precipitates, they may, usually, be coagulated by warming. 

2. Effect of changes in hydrogen ion concentration. In separate test tubes place 2 cc. portions of test solutions of Bi+++, Cd++, and Sn++. To each add 1 cc. of concentrated hydrochloric acid.

Pass in hydrogen sulfide for a moment. Explain carefully why any of the sulfides now fail to precipitate. What harm is there in having too low a concentration of hydrogen ion? 48

3. Separation of the Tin Group from the Copper Group. To il lustrate the reaction by which the separation of the groups is brought about, mix 10 cc. of the test solutions of Hg++ and Sb+++. 

Dilute to 60 cc., heat, and precipitate the sulfides with hydrogen sulfide, using an Erlenmeyer flask as in the precipitation of the Nickel Group. Filter and wash the precipitate once with hot water.

Scrape the precipitate into a test tube and add 3 cc. of ammonium polysulfide solution, (NH4)2Sx. Warm for thirty seconds by dipping the test tube into warm water (do not heat over a flame) and filter on a small paper. 

Make the filtrate just acid (litmus) with dilute hydrochloric acid. By the color of the precipitate thus obtained, and of the residue on the filter, determine which of the sulfides is soluble in ammonium polysulfide. If all of the sulfides of this group are similarly treated with this reagent, it will be found that the sulfides of arsenic and of tin also are soluble. Write equations for the reaction of each of these sulfides with the ammonium polysulfide.

THE TIN GROUP (GROUP III) 

Sn++; Sn++++; Sb+++; As+++ (Sulfides which are dissolved by ammonium polysulfide) 

PRELIMINARY EXPERIMENTS 

Arsenious Ion, As+++ 

1. Insolubility of the sulfide. Precipitate arsenic trisulfide from 

5 cc. of the test solution of As+++. Decant the liquid and test the solubility of the sulfide in 12 N hydrochloric acid. Result? This affords a means of separating arsenious ion during the systematic analysis. 

2. Identification of Arsenious Ion. The treatment during a systematic analysis converts any arsenic present into arsenic acid.

Hence we test for the negative arsenate ion, AsO, in order to identify the original arsenious ion. Decant the hydrochloric acid from the arsenic trisulfide prepared in the preceding experiment, add 8 cc. of concentrated nitric acid, boil until reaction seems to be over-usually several minutes-and filter the solution. To the

filtrate add 2 cc. of "magnesia mixture" (essentially a solution of magnesium chloride), and add ammonium hydroxide to some excess (use litmus). The white precipitate which, like that of magnesium ammonium phosphate, is often slow in forming, is an indication of arsenic. Express these reactions by equations. 

Antimony Ion, Sb+++ 1. Hydrolysis of antimony salts. Dilute 3 cc. of the test solution of Sb+++ (SbCl) with small portions of cold water until a precipitate is produced. This is the oxychloride, SbOCI. 

2. Solubility of the sulfide. Pass hydrogen sulfide into the suspension of the oxychloride obtained in the preceding experiment and note the ready conversion into the characteristic orange sulfide. The color of this sulfide is so distinctive that it usually serves as a sufficient test for antimony ion. Decant the liquid and determine the solubility of the sulfide in concentrated hydrochloric acid.

Stannous Ion, Sn++; Stannic Ion, Sn++++ 

1. Nature of stannous hydroxide. Try the effect of excess sodium hydroxide solution on a 5 cc. portion of the test solution of 

Sn++. List all the hydroxides you have studied which show a similar behavior. 

2. Sulfides of tin. Precipitate stannous sulfide from 5 cc. of the test solution of Sn++. Note the color of the precipitate and determine its solubility in concentrated hydrochloric acid. Similarly, precipitate stannic sulfide from 5 cc. of the test solution of Sn++++. 

Note the distinct difference in color between the sulfides in the different valences. Determine the solubility of the stannic sulfide in concentrated hydrochloric acid. 

3. Reducing action of stannous compounds. To 3 cc. of a test solution of Sn++ add mercuric chloride solution, drop by drop. 

The white precipitate of mercurous chloride, or the blackish suspension of free mercury, affords a good test for the stannous ion during the later analysis. Would you expect the stannic ion to show a similar behavior? Why?

Warm 5 cc. of the test solution of Sn++++ with an iron nail for several minutes; then filter the solution into 2 cc. of mercuric chloride reagent. From the result of this test, state what effect

the iron produced on the stannic ion. Express all these reactions by equations.

SYSTEMATIC ANALYSIS OF THE TIN GROUP 

Note: During a systematic analysis, this group is sought for, along with the Copper Group, in the filtrate from the Silver Group (Group I). Ordinarily, the hydrochloric acid employed to precipitate the Silver Group will be sufficient to provide the degree of acidity required for the hydrogen sulfide groups. 

The method of analysis to be applied in the separation of the ions of this group takes advantage of the differences in the relative solubilities of the three sulfides. The sulfide of arsenic is insoluble in hot concentrated hydrochloric acid, and is filtered out, while the other two dissolve readily. If the resulting solution of antimony and tin chlorides is diluted to the correct concentration of acid, antimony sulfide can be precipitated, leaving stannic ion in solution. This ion is identified, after reduction to the low valence, by adding mercuric chloride. 

These separations, depending on the control of sulfide ion concentration, will be entirely satisfactory if carried out with care. 

Volumes must be measured accurately, and losses by spilling or other careless manipulation must be avoided.

If the acidity of the solution becomes too low, some stannic sulfide precipitates with the antimony sulfide. This is especially noticeable when antimony is absent. Traces of copper sulfide, dissolved by the polysulfide in separating this group from the Copper Group, frequently discolor the precipitate of antimony sulfide. The student must be on his guard to interpret these observations and not be misled by them. 

1. Preparation of the solution. Before attempting the precipitation, it is most important that the acidity of the solution be adjusted to as near 0.3 N as possible. This is not difficult to carry out by the use of the indicator, methyl violet. 

Dilute 10 cc. of your unknown (or a practice solution containing 

10 cc. of test solution of each ion) to a volume of 100 cc. Neglect any precipitate which may form. Now moisten the outside of a clean casserole with water and make a series of streaks with an ordinary violet copying pencil (which contains methyl violet). 

Dip a stirring rod into your diluted unknown and wet one of the streaks with the solution. The proper acidity is indicated by the

development of a blue-green color by the indicator. If the solution is too acid, the color will be yellow-green or yellow, and may be corrected by adding a few drops of ammonium hydroxide to the solution. If not acid enough, a blue or violet color is obtained; in that case add concentrated hydrochloric acid until the proper shade appears.

An alternative method by which the acidity of the solution may be adjusted is as follows. Exactly neutralize 10 cc. of your solution by adding ammonium hydroxide drop by drop. To the neutral solution add exactly 5 cc. of dilute (6 N) hydrochloric acid, and dilute the solution to a volume of 100 cc. 

2. Precipitation of the group. Place this adjusted solution in an 

Erlenmeyer flask of appropriate size, heat nearly to boiling, and precipitate with hydrogen sulfide, keeping the contents of the flask agitated during the process. Let the precipitate settle, decant a small portion of the liquid through a filter, add a little water, and again pass hydrogen sulfide into this portion to determine whether or not the precipitation has been complete. If additional precipitate is formed, treat the entire solution in a similar fashion. Decant as much as possible of the clear liquid through the filter and finally wash the precipitate with hot water. 

In a general analysis, this precipitate will contain the sulfides of both the Tin Group and the Copper Group. 

3. Separation of the Tin Group. Transfer the washed precipi tate to a large pyrex test tube and add about 8 cc. of ammonium polysulfide solution. Warm the tube by dipping it into a beaker of warm water, but do not heat over a free flame. Stir well, and after one or two minutes filter on a small paper. The chief reason for caution in this separation is the fact that copper sulfide is slightly soluble in ammonium polysulfide. A trace will cause no trouble, but if heating is prolonged, much copper passes into the Tin Group. If copper is known to be absent, much less care need be used.

The residue on the filter will contain the Copper Group-HgS, PbS, CuS, Bi2S3, CdS. The filtrate contains the Tin Group in solution as the thio-salts-(NH4) 3ASS4, (NH4) 3SbS4, (NH4)2SnS3. 

If the solution of the thio-salts is allowed to stand, it usually becomes turbid and develops a precipitate. This does no harm. 

In order to recover the sulfides of the Tin Group, add to this filtrate, with stirring, enough dilute hydrochloric acid to make the

solution just acid (use litmus). Avoid much excess acid. (Why?)

Warm for a moment to coagulate the precipitate, filter, and let the precipitate drain well. 

4. Separation and identification of Arsenic Ion. Scrape (do not wash) this precipitate of the three sulfides into a small flask. In a dry graduate measure exactly 10 cc. of concentrated hydrochloric acid (12 N) and add this to the precipitate in the flask. Warm in a hot (not boiling) water bath for several minutes, keeping the contents well agitated to break up any lumps of precipitate. It is well to keep the flask loosely stoppered during this heating so as to prevent, so far as possible, the loss of hydrochloric acid in the vapors.

Measure carefully 40 cc. of boiling water. Add 5 cc. of this to the hydrochloric acid digestion, and filter hot. When all of the acid solution has run through the filter, wash the precipitate and paper with the remaining 35 cc. of water, catching it all in the filtrate. (The volume of the filtrate is now 50 cc. What is the approximate normality of the acid?) This filtrate may become turbid if it cools; this is quite normal. 

The residue on the filter will contain arsenic pentasulfide, if arsenic ion is present, but this residue is always contaminated with considerable sulfur, and the presence of arsenic ion must be confirmed as directed in the preliminary experiments. 

5. Identification of Antimony Ion. Heat the 50 cc. filtrate from the previous separation nearly to boiling. (If a precipitate has formed on standing it should largely dissolve on warming.) Pass hydrogen sulfide into this hot solution for a few seconds. A bright orange-red precipitate of antimony sulfide is ample proof of the presence of antimony ion. (This is the trisulfide of antimony. 

The pentasulfide is quite unstable, and probably breaks up to some extent when the ammonium thio-antimonate is decomposed by acid.) 

2(NH4) 3SbS4 + 6HCl → 6NH4Cl + Sb₂S3 + 2S + 3H2S. 

In the acid concentration now present in the solution, stannic sulfide cannot precipitate so long as the solution is kept hot, but if it cools much, a good deal of this sulfide may fall with the antimony. 

6. Identification of Stannous Ion. If a precipitate of antimony sulfide was obtained in the preceding step, filter it out while the

G 

solution is still very hot. Place a couple of iron nails in this filtrate (or in the solution which gave no precipitate) and boil down this solution to a volume of 10-15 cc. Filter directly into 5 cc. of mercuric chloride solution. A white precipitate, which may turn gray, is a test for stannous ion. 

REVIEW OF  (GROUP III) 

1. Why should the solution be slightly acidic during the precipitation of the sulfides of the Tin and Copper Groups? Explain the effect if the acidity rises much above 0.3 N; if it becomes much lower than this value. 

2. What is ammonium polysulfide? Compare by equations the action of normal ammonium sulfide and of ammonium polysulfide, separately, with arsenic trisulfide and with stannous sulfide. 

3. Which sulfide of the Copper Group dissolves to some extent in the polysulfide? What will become of this stray ion during the analysis of the Tin Group? 

4. Select a reagent, or combination of reagents, which will bring about a separation in one step of the following pairs of substances. In each case state the concentration of the reagents if this is significant, and express all reactions by equations. (a) As2S3-Sb2S3; (b) SnS-HgS; (c) SbCl;-SnCl2; (d) SnCl4-ZnCl2. 

5. Write balanced equations showing the action of nitric acid with each of the following: (a) As2S3; (b) As2S5; (c) H₂S. 

6. If a solution of arsenic trichloride is first made alkaline with sodium hydroxide, hydrogen sulfide will not cause the precipitation of arsenic trisulfide, although the sulfide ion concentration is higher than in an acid solution. Explain why this sulfide fails to precipitate from the alkaline solution.

THE COPPER GROUP (GROUP II) 

Cu++; Hg++; Pb++; Bi+++; Cd++ (Sulfides insoluble in ammonium polysulfide) 

PRELIMINARY EXPERIMENTS 

Reaction of the sulfides with nitric acid. In separate test tubes prepare the sulfides of all the members of this group by passing hydrogen sulfide for a moment into 3 cc. portions of the test solution of each ion. Let each precipitate settle, decant the supernatant liquid as well as possible, and add to each 5 cc. of dilute nitric acid. Warm each test tube for a moment and observe which

sulfides dissolve. (A whitish suspension of sulfur will of course remain after the reaction.) Express each reaction by an equation.

Mercuric Ion, Hg++ 1. Nature of the hydroxide. To 2 cc. of the test solution of Hg++ add sodium hydroxide. Mercuric hydroxide is too unstable at room temperature to exist. What is this precipitate? 

2. Reaction of mercuric sulfide with aqua regia. To the suspension of mercuric sulfide in nitric acid (saved from the preceding experiment), add 1 cc. of concentrated hydrochloric acid and warm. This combination of nitric and hydrochloric acids forms aqua regia, which may be considered to react as follows: 3HCl + HNO3 → 2H2O + NOCI + Cl2. 

HgS + Cl2 → HgCl2 + S. 

3. Identification of Mercuric Ion. Add stannous chloride reagent dropwise to 3 cc. of the test solution of Hg++. Note the formation of a white precipitate which darkens as excess stannous chloride is added. Represent these reactions by equations. 

Lead Ion, Pb++ 1. Nature of the hydroxide. Add sodium hydroxide slowly, with shaking, to 2 cc. of the test solution of Pb++, until an excess is present. Equations? What other hydroxides have you encountered which behave in a like manner? 

2. Solubility of lead chloride. To 3 cc. of the test solution of

Pb++, add an equal volume of ammonium chloride reagent. Similarly, add ammonium chloride solution to 3 cc. of lead acetate reagent (a fairly concentrated solution). Explain the formation of a precipitate of lead chloride in one case and not in the other. 

Pass hydrogen sulfide into the solution from which no precipitate was obtained. Which is more soluble, lead sulfide, or lead chloride? If lead ion is found in the Silver Group (Group I), will it also appear in the Copper Group? Why? 

3. Formation of lead sulfate. To 5 cc. of the test solution of 

Pb++ add 5 cc. of dilute sulfuric acid, and evaporate in a small casserole (hood) until dense fumes of sulfuric acid and sulfur trioxide come off. Cool, and add 10 cc. of water. What is the precipitate? Filter, and pour 10 cc. of warm ammonium acetate solution over the precipitate. To the filtrate add a few drops of

potassium chromate reagent. The yellow precipitate is lead chromate. Explain why lead nitrate is converted to the sulfate by the above operation, and why the sulfate is soluble in ammonium acetate solution.

Bismuth Ion, Bi+++ 1. Hydrolysis of bismuth salts. To 3 cc. of the test solution of 

Bi+++ (BiCl3) slowly add hot water until a precipitate forms. 

This is bismuth oxychloride-BiOCl. It will usually form when a solution containing a bismuth salt is diluted preparatory to precipitation with hydrogen sulfide, but it does no harm, for it is rapidly changed into the sulfide. Which ion of the Tin Group does this resemble?

Add concentrated hydrochloric acid to the precipitate of the oxychloride and account for the disappearance of the precipitate. 

2. Nature of the hydroxide. To two 2 cc. portions of the test solution of Bi+++ add excess ammonium hydroxide and sodium hydroxide solution, respectively. Does the precipitate dissolve in an excess of either base? Compare this hydroxide with the hydroxides of lead and antimony. 

3. Reduction of bismuth hydroxide. A test for Bismuth Ion. 

Filter the bismuth hydroxide obtained above by the action of ammonium hydroxide. Prepare fresh sodium stannite solution by taking 1 cc. of stannous chloride reagent and adding to it sodium hydroxide solution until the first precipitate dissolves. Pour this reagent over the precipitate of bismuth hydroxide on the filter paper. Note the characteristic glossy, jet black appearance of this precipitate, which is metallic bismuth. SnCl2 + 2NaOH → Sn(OH)2 ↓ + 2NaCl. Sn(OH)2+2NaOH → Na₂SnO2 + 2HOH. 2Bi(OH)3 + 3Na2SnO2 → 2Bi + 3Na2SnO3 + 3H2O. 

These reactions afford a delicate test for bismuth ion. In what other case has di-valent tin been employed as a reducing agent? 

How did the conditions differ?

Cupric Ion, Cu++ 1. Action of bases. Add sodium hydroxide solution to 3 cc. of the test solution of Cu++. The precipitate is cupric hydroxide. 

Heat the suspension to boiling for a moment and account for the

change in color of the precipitate. What other ion of this family forms an unstable hydroxide? 

To 3 cc. of the test solution of Cu++ add ammonium hydroxide to excess. Account for the solution of the first precipitate and the resultant deep blue color of the solution. This color often serves as a test for cupric ion. With this reaction in mind, suggest a means of separating the ions of bismuth and copper. 

To a portion of this deep blue ammoniacal solution add hydrogen sulfide. From the fact that you get a precipitate of cupric sulfide, what conclusion can you draw about the stability of the copper-ammonia complex ion? 

2. Ferrocyanide test for Cupric Ion. Dilute 1 cc. of the test solution of Cu++ to 10 cc., and add a few drops of potassium ferrocyanide reagent. The precipitate of copper ferrocyanide affords an extremely delicate test for the cupric ion. 

3. Reaction with potassium cyanide. Remember that cyanides are dangerously poisonous. Take care not to spill the solutions, and wash them down the sink as soon as you have finished. 

To 3 cc. of the test solution of Cu++ add an equal volume of potassium cyanide solution. Note the disappearance of the blue color, and realize that this means the removal of copper ions from the solution because of the formation of a complex cuprocyanide ion. 2Cu+++4CN-→2CuCN+ C₂N2. CuCN+2CN− → Cu(CN)5. (Cupro-cyanide ion) Pass hydrogen sulfide into this cyanide solution for a few seconds. 

Does any sulfide precipitate?

Cadmium Ion, Cd++ 1. Reaction with ammonium hydroxide. To a 3 cc. portion of the test solution of Cd++ add an excess of ammonium hydroxide. 

Cadmium forms a complex ion with ammonia just as copper does. 

In what respect do the two complex ions differ? 

2. Reaction with potassium cyanide. To a portion of the ammoniacal cadmium solution add an equal volume of potassium cyanide solution and pass in hydrogen sulfide for a few seconds. 

Compare the result with that obtained in the case of copper. 

Cadmium ion does not form a stable complex with cyanide ion.

How can you take advantage of this fact to effect a separation of copper and cadmium ions?

Cadmium has, until recently, been a metal of few uses. At present, however, considerable cadmium plating is being used, making its analytical detection of more importance than formerly. 

SYSTEMATIC ANALYSIS OF THE COPPER GROUP 

Note: During a complete cation analysis, the Copper Group would have been precipitated along with the Tin Group, and subsequently isolated as the residue left when the Tin Group was dissolved in ammonium polysulfide. 

The analysis of this group affords an excellent example of the application of specific chemical reactions. Mercuric ion is first separated by taking advantage of the insolubility of its sulfide in dilute nitric acid. From the solution containing the remaining four ions, lead ion is eliminated as the insoluble sulfate. Addition of ammonium hydroxide to the solution of the ions of bismuth, copper, and cadmium, throws down insoluble bismuth hydroxide, while the copper and cadmium ions form soluble complexes and pass into the filtrate, where the intense blue of the copper complex ion affords evidence of the presence of copper ion. Finally, copper ion may be removed by the addition of potassium cyanide, and cadmium ion identified by the precipitation of the bright yellow sulfide. Suitable confirmatory tests may be applied when necessary.

Prepare a practice solution containing 10 cc. of the test solution of each ion of this group. Dilute to 100 cc., adjust the acidity and precipitate the group exactly as directed in the Tin Group (p. 51). Filter the precipitated sulfides and wash them with hot water. Test the filtrate for complete precipitation. 

1. Separation and identification of Mercuric Ion. Wash the precipitated sulfides into a casserole. Estimate the volume of the wash water used (which should not exceed 10 cc.) and add as much dilute (6 N) nitric acid as there is wash water. With a low flame heat the sulfides just to boiling and hold them there for a full minute. Filter.

The residue from this treatment will contain any mercuric sulfide present in the group precipitate. Since there is always a residue of free sulfur-usually blackish because of occluded sul-

fides the presence of mercury must be confirmed. Dissolve the residue in a little aqua regia and boil until the chlorine is removed, but do not go to dryness. To the solution add stannous chloride reagent, a little at a time, until a considerable excess has been added. A white or gray precipitate proves the presence of mercuric ion. 

2. Separation and identification of Lead Ion. To the nitric acid solution from the preceding separation, add 2 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid and evaporate in a casserole under the hood until dense white fumes of sulfur trioxide appear. It is necessary to carry the evaporation to this point to insure the complete removal of nitric acid, which would otherwise dissolve some lead sulfate. Cool, and add 10 cc. of water and 5 cc. of dilute sulfuric acid to replace the acid lost during evaporation. By the use of a limited amount of water, and the addition of sulfuric acid, extensive hydrolysis of bismuth sulfate is avoided. Transfer this diluted solution to a beaker. Why? A fine, heavy white precipitate is lead sulfate. Filter. It is well to confirm the precipitate by washing it and pouring over the paper 10 cc. of warm ammonium acetate solution. Repeat the extraction several times with the same portion of solution, then add a few drops of potassium chromate reagent to the filtrate. The presence of lead ion is shown by a bright yellow precipitate. 

3. Separation and identification of Bismuth Ion. To the sulfuric acid filtrate (from which lead sulfate has been removed) add ammonium hydroxide, with stirring, until just alkaline (use litmus) and then 8-10 cc. in excess. If bismuth ion is present there will be a white precipitate of bismuth hydroxide, but since this precipitate is often hard to see, the solution should always be filtered. 

Wash any precipitate on the paper, and confirm bismuth with freshly prepared sodium stannite solution, as directed in the preliminary experiments. Remember that the precipitate of metallic bismuth formed in this test is a glossy, jet black. Any gray or brownish stain on the paper is not a bismuth test. 

4. Identification of Copper Ion. If the ammoniacal filtrate from the separation of bismuth hydroxide is blue, this is due to the copper-ammonia complex ion. Usually no further confirmation is needed, but if desired, a portion of the filtrate may be acidified with acetic acid and tested with potassium ferrocyanide solution as directed in the preliminary experiments.

5. Identification of Cadmium Ion. If the copper ion is absent, cadmium may be directly identified by passing hydrogen sulfide into the ammoniacal filtrate for not over ten seconds. The bright yellow precipitate of cadmium sulfide is a good test. However, if copper ion is present, the black copper sulfide will obscure the cadmium sulfide. This may be avoided as follows:

To the blue ammoniacal solution add strong potassium cyanide solution, with stirring, until the blue color completely disappears. 

Admit hydrogen sulfide for five or ten seconds (not longer). A yellow precipitate is cadmium sulfide, proving the presence of cadmium ion. Wash this cyanide solution down the sink at once. 

After completing unknowns on the Tin and Copper Groups separately, an analysis should be carried out which involves the separation of the two groups.

THE SILVER GROUP (GROUP I) 

Ag+; Hg+; Pb++

Group Reagent: Hydrochloric Acid 

Silver ion is the only member of this group of much analytical importance. Mercurous compounds are not commonly encountered, and lead ion falls in this group only when present in fairly high concentrations. Recall that lead ion was one of the members of the Copper Group. Its detection in that group is of more importance, analytically, than it is here. 

PRELIMINARY EXPERIMENTS 

The Group Reagent. In separate test tubes place 2 cc. of the test solution of each of the three ions of this group, and to each add dilute hydrochloric acid. Note the appearance of each precipitate. Repeat, using first ammonium chloride and then sodium chloride solution. It should be apparent that any source of chloride ion will precipitate this group. Hydrochloric acid is most frequently used. Why? 

Silver Ion, Ag+ 

1. Solubility of the chloride. Using ammonium chloride solution as the precipitating agent, prepare silver chloride from 3 cc. of the test solution of Ag+. Decant the liquid and test the solubility of the precipitate in 20 cc. of hot water. Does any of the chloride appear to dissolve? 

Mention four other 

Acidify the ammo Decant the hot water and add ammonium hydroxide to the residue. The solubility of the chloride in this reagent is due to the formation of a silver-ammonia complex ion. metal ions which form similar complex ions. niacal solution with nitric acid. Explain why the precipitate of silver chloride reappears. 

Mercurous Ion, Hg+

Note carefully that this ion is mono-valent. In what group does di-valent mercury (Hg++) fall? 62

THE SILVER GROUP 

1. Nature of the chloride. Using ammonium chloride solution as the precipitant, precipitate mercurous chloride from 3 cc. of the test solution of Hg+. Decant the liquid and try to dissolve the precipitate in hot water. Compare with silver chloride.

To a very small portion of mercurous chloride add chlorine water (or aqua regia). Why does the precipitate dissolve? This should again emphasize the fact that, of the two ions of mercury, only mercurous ion falls in this group. 

2. Reaction of the chloride with ammonium hydroxide. Prepare a fresh sample of mercurous chloride and add ammonium hydroxide to it. The reaction which occurs may be represented as follows: 

2HgCl + 2NH4OH → Hg ↓ + Hg(NH2)Cl ↓ + NHÂCl + 2H₂O. 

Contrast this behavior with that shown by silver chloride. 

Lead Ion, Pb++

To secure satisfactory results, the test solution of Pb++ used for the following experiments should be three times that concentration used elsewhere. 

1. Solubility of the chloride. To 2 cc. of the test solution of

Pb++ add ammonium chloride dropwise until precipitation seems complete. Decant, or filter, and add potassium chromate solution to the filtrate. How insoluble is lead chloride, even in cold water?

Heat 2 cc. of the test solution of Pb++ and, while hot, add ammonium chloride solution. Account for the result.

Dilute 2 cc. of the test solution with three volumes of water and try to precipitate lead chloride by adding ammonium chloride reagent.

From the results of these experiments state the proper conditions for the precipitation of this group. Will lead be completely removed even if these conditions are observed? 

2. Effect of ammonium hydroxide on lead chloride. Precipitate a small amount of lead chloride, decant the liquid, and add ammonium hydroxide to the precipitate. Does the lead chloride dissolve? Does it blacken?

SYSTEMATIC ANALYSIS OF THE SILVER GROUP 

Note: Although the analytical groups have been studied in the reverse order, during a general cation analysis the Silver Group is the first group sought for. Because of the comparatively high

THE SILVER GROUP 

solubility of lead chloride, the unknown should not be diluted before attempting the precipitation if the identification of lead ion in this group is expected. Since a small amount of lead ion would not be precipitated here, and since the ion would appear again in the Copper Group, the precipitation of the Silver Group is often carried out in a hot solution, and no lead ion test is made here. 

This must not, however, be done except during a general analysis.

A review of the preliminary experiments will indicate that the ions of this group are very readily separated by the application of a few simple reactions, provided these are applied in the proper order. Lead chloride is readily soluble in hot water. If ammonium hydroxide is then added to the residue of silver and mercurous chlorides, silver chloride dissolves; and mercurous chloride is partially converted into free mercury, which appears black. Confirmatory tests are then applied to the two filtrates to identify lead and silver ions.

For analysis take 10 cc. of an unknown solution, or prepare a practice solution containing 5 cc. of the test solution of each of the three ions in this group. 

1. Precipitation of the group. To the solution for analysis, add dilute hydrochloric acid dropwise. After each addition, stir and let the precipitate settle for a moment. Then add more acid to the supernatant liquid and note if any further precipitate forms. 

Filter, test the filtrate for complete precipitation, and wash the precipitate twice with small portions of cold water. 

2. Identification of Lead Ion. Heat 10 cc. of water to boiling and pour it over the washed precipitate, collecting it as it runs through the filter. Add a few drops of potassium chromate reagent to the extract. A yellow precipitate proves the presence of lead ion. If this is found, continue to wash the precipitate remaining on the paper with hot water until a sample of the washings shows practically no lead ion test. 

3. Separation of Silver Ion and identification of Mercurous Ion. 

Pour 10 cc. of ammonium hydroxide over the washed precipitate, passing the filtrate back through the same filter repeatedly until the solution is perfectly clear. The black precipitate on the paper is a decisive test for the mercurous ion. 

4. Identification of Silver Ion. Cautiously acidify the clear ammoniacal extract with dilute nitric acid. Use litmus to show

THE SILVER GROUP 

that it is acid. The appearance of a white precipitate of silver chloride proves the presence of silver ion. 

REVIEW OF THE SILVER GROUP (GROUP I) 

1. Fill out the information requested in the following chart. Represent all chemical reactions by balanced equations. 

REACTION WITH SOLUBILITY OF THE CHLORIDE ION CHLORIDE IN HOT WATER 

Pb++ Ag+ Hg+ 

REACTION OF THE CHLORIDE WITH AMMONIUM HYDROXIDE 

2. On the basis of the solubility product concept, explain why lead chloride is soluble in hot water. 

3. Select a reagent, or combination of reagents, which will bring about a separation in one step of each of the following pairs. Express all reactions by balanced equations. (a) AgCl—PbCl2; (b) AgCl—HgCl; (c) HgNO3— 

Hg(NO3)2; (d) PbCl2-HgCl.

It may appear that the complete, systematic analysis for all of the cations involves nothing but the carrying out, in proper sequence, of the individual group procedures whose study has just been completed. Although the analysis of the group precipitates does not change, it is, nevertheless, necessary to carry out certain additional operations because of the fact that each group is now precipitated from the filtrate of the preceding one. Hence the solution will contain the reagent by which the preceding group was precipitated. Before commencing a complete analysis the student should study carefully the general discussions which have been included in presenting each group. Recall especially the following points: 

(1) The filtrate from the Iron Group (Group IV) is alkaline, and under these conditions manganese is rapidly oxidized by the air. If the Nickel Group (Group V) cannot be precipitated at once, this filtrate should be acidified with hydrochloric acid. (2) The filtrate from the Nickel Group is also alkaline, and contains ammonium sulfide from the group reagents employed. 

If this solution remains in this condition long, some sulfate ion is formed (by the oxidation of sulfide ion) and this causes the loss of barium and strontium ions. Why? Furthermore, the large quantity of ammonium salts which is usually present by the time this group is reached, greatly hinders the precipitation of the 

Calcium Group (Group VI). To avoid these difficulties, promptly acidify the Nickel Group filtrate with hydrochloric acid, evaporate to a small volume to drive out hydrogen sulfide, and filter from any precipitated sulfur. Finally add nitric acid, evaporate to dryness, and heat the residue until most of the ammonium salts have been decomposed. After taking up the residue in water (plus a drop of concentrated hydrochloric acid) the Calcium Group is precipitated in the usual manner. (3) Since the ions of the Calcium Group are not entirely removed by precipitation as carbonates, the traces of these ions remaining interfere with the test for the magnesium ion. The interference may be avoided in the following manner. To the portion of the 66

solution to be tested for the magnesium ion, add 3 cc. of ammonium sulfate solution and a like amount of ammonium oxalate solution; make alkaline with ammonium hydroxide, filter from any precipitate, and then complete the test as usual. 

GENERAL MANIPULATION 

1. Precipitation. Before trying to precipitate a group, take a small side portion of the unknown and test it with the group reagent. If the group is present, the test portion may be returned and the entire solution treated with the proper reagents. If a negative test results, the test sample is discarded. In this way no time is unnecessarily lost in removing the ions of the group reagent. Use reagents sparingly. At the same time, remember that the group precipitations must be complete or the analysis of the later groups is ruined. The filtrate from a group precipitation must be tested to prove that the group has been removed completely, before going further. 

Always wash the group precipitates thoroughly-usually with hot water and never allow such a precipitate to stand from one period to the next. It will dry out, and then fail to react as expected.

PRELIMINARY TESTS 

1. Ammonium Ion. Since the ammonium ion is added several times during the analysis, its presence must be determined before the precipitation of the Iron Group (Group IV). Usually the regular test may be applied directly to a 10 cc. portion of the original unknown. In this case use a large excess of solid calcium hydroxide and mix thoroughly, in order to neutralize the free acid often present in the unknown. If the test is not obtained within half a minute, ammonium ion should not be reported. 

2. Ferrous and Ferric Ions. If the valence of iron is to be reported, appropriate tests must be made on side portions of the original unknown. Why not wait until the Iron Group is precipitated? Remember that there are many insoluble ferrocyanides some colored-but only the ferric ferrocyanide is deep blue.

3. Anions present. A little reflection will remind the student that certain anions and cations are incompatible in the same solution. It is well to test small portions of a general unknown for the presence of chloride ion and sulfate ion. (Use barium nitrate reagent in the latter test.) Which cations are eliminated if sulfate ion is present? Which ions if chloride ion is found? A consideration of other anions whose presence may give rise to difficulties during the systematic cation analysis is beyond the scope of this manual.

REVIEW OF GENERAL ANALYSIS 

1. The following tests, with the results indicated, are made according to the usual analytical procedure on a solution received for analysis. Prepare a table showing those ions whose presence is definitely indicated and also those which are proved to be absent. Do not list any ion about which there is no definite information. (a) Hydrochloric acid produces a white precipitate, completely soluble in ammonium hydroxide. (b) Hydrogen sulfide in the filtrate from (a) produces a dark precipitate. 

This is treated with ammonium polysulfide, and a black residue remains. (c) The polysulfide extract, when acidified with dilute hydrochloric acid, yields a bright orange-red precipitate, completely soluble in concentrated hydrochloric acid. (d) The black residue from (b) is treated with dilute nitric acid. A reaction occurs, but considerable black residue remains undissolved. (e) Excess ammonium hydroxide added to the nitric acid solution from (d) produces a deep blue solution free from suspended precipitate. (f) The filtrate from the precipitation of the hydrogen sulfide groups is treated with ammonium hydroxide in the usual manner without result. (g) Hydrogen sulfide in this ammoniacal solution produces a whitish precipitate completely soluble in dilute hydrochloric acid. (h) When sodium hydroxide is added to the hydrochloric acid extract of (g) a whitish precipitate falls, which darkens considerably on exposure to air. (i) Ammonium carbonate reagent, added to the filtrate from (g) produces a white precipitate. This dissolves in acetic acid, and from this acid solution, potassium chromate throws down a yellow precipitate. After filtering out this precipitate, ammonium carbonate no longer produces any precipitate. (j) The filtrate from (i) imparts a brilliant crimson color to the Bunsen flame. 

2. Point out, during the general analysis: (a) Two instances of the application of the common ion effect. In each case, state what effect is produced, and how it was obtained.

(b) Five cases where an oxidation-reduction reaction leads directly to the identification of a metal ion. (c) Two instances in which the separation or identification of an ion depends, at least in part, on the amphoterism of the hydroxide. (d) An instance where the principles of hydrolysis are applied to effect the separation of an ion, or of a group of ions. (e) Three cases, at different stages of the analysis, where the formation of a complex ion assists in the separation or identification of one of the metal ions. 

3. A solution is to be examined for the presence, in definite amounts, of the following ions: Al+++, Ba++, Cr+++, Fe+++, Pb++, Hg++, Hg+, Na+, NH+, K+, Ag+, Zn++, Cu++, SO, NO,, CO, H+, OH-, Cl-. Assume the absence of all other ions. The following tests are made on separate portions of the solution: (a) Dilute hydrochloric acid produces no effect. (b) Excess sodium hydroxide produces a blue solution containing no suspended precipitate. (c) Addition of barium chloride solution causes a white precipitate. 

On the basis of these three tests, considered as a whole, state which of the above ions are proved present, which are proved absent, and which yield no evidence. Give the reasons for your conclusions.

III. Special Reagents 

Test Solutions (for qualitative experiments): Concentration such that the solution contains 5 mg. of ion per cc. 

Ammoniacal silver sulfate: Dissolve 78 g. of Ag2SO4 in 250 cc. of 4 N NH4OH. 

Dilute to 1000 cc. 

Ammonium molybdate: Dissolve 88 g. (NH4)2MoO, in 600 cc. 6 N NH¸OH. 

Add 240 g. of NH4NO3. Dilute to 1000 cc. 

Ammonium polysulfide: Digest 1000 cc. of 6 N (NH4)2S with 25 g. of flowers of sulfur for several hours, and filter. 

Barium chloride-calcium chloride: Dissolve 61 g. of BaCl2 2H2O and 52 g. of CaCl2.6H2O, and dilute to 1000 cc. Let stand, filter off any turbidity through a double filter, and use clear filtrate. 

Dimethylglyoxime: Dissolve 12 g. of the solid in 1000 cc. of 95% ethyl alcohol. 

Magnesia mixture: Dissolve 100 g. of MgCl2·6H₂O and 100 g. of NH4Cl in water; add 50 cc. of conc. NH4OH, and dilute to 1000 cc. 

Methyl orange: Dissolve 0.02 g. of the solid in 100 cc. of hot water, cool, and filter if necessary. 

Phenolphthalein: Dissolve 1 g. of the solid in 100 cc. of 95% ethyl alcohol. 

Sodium cobaltinitrite: Dissolve 230 g. of NaNO2 in 500 cc. of water. Add 

155 cc. of 6 N HC2H3O2 and 30 g. of Co (NO3)2·6H 20. Let the mixture stand 24 hours; filter, then dilute to 1000 cc. 

Starch: Prepare a thin paste of 20 g. of soluble starch with a little water by grinding in a mortar. Pour the paste into 1000 cc. of boiling water. 

Boil 5 minutes, and filter through a loose plug of cotton wool. 

Turmeric paper: Shake turmeric powder with 95% ethyl alcohol. Filter.

Dip sheets of coarse filter paper into this solution and dry at room temperature.
